## Decisions and Dispositions

### State Oil Co. v. Khan

Argued October 7, 1997 | Decided November 4, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for State Oil reversed and case remanded under *Albrecht v. Herald Co.*, 93 F.3d 1358 (7th Cir. 1996). `OT_1997CHUNK1.md` ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522003/usrep522003.pdf))  
Area of Law: Antitrust; vertical restraints

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a vertical maximum resale-price agreement is unlawful per se under Sherman Act § 1.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Maximum vertical resale-price agreements

Holding and operative rule: *Albrecht v. Herald Co.* is overruled. A vertical agreement fixing a maximum resale price is evaluated under the rule of reason rather than condemned as unlawful per se.

Reasoning: Sherman Act § 1 reaches unreasonable restraints, while per se condemnation is reserved for categories whose harmful character and lack of redeeming competitive value have been established through durable judicial experience. Maximum resale-price arrangements can reduce dealer markups and strengthen interbrand competition, even though particular arrangements may suppress dealer services, facilitate another restraint, or produce other anticompetitive effects. Because the category does not possess near-uniform harmful consequences, *Albrecht*’s conclusive presumption is unsound and increasingly inconsistent with the Court’s treatment of other vertical restraints. Rule-of-reason review corrects that defect without immunizing the arrangement or displacing the rules governing horizontal price fixing, minimum resale-price maintenance, or cartel facilitation. The record does not permit a final determination of State Oil’s liability under the replacement standard. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522003/usrep522003.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Albrecht v. Herald Co.*: overruled; vertical maximum resale-price agreements are governed by the rule of reason.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for application of the rule of reason and consideration of preserved defenses.  
Effect: Khan’s claim is no longer governed by a conclusive rule of illegality. State Oil remains subject to liability if the challenged arrangement is proved unreasonable under the ordinary antitrust inquiry.

### Jeffries v. Harleston

Argued October 7, 1997 | Decided November 17, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: judgment for Jeffries reversed following remand under *Waters v. Churchill*, with instructions to enter judgment for the defendants, 52 F.3d 9 (2d Cir. 1995). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/52/9/573564/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; public-employee speech; § 1983 causation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether CUNY could impose the challenged adverse action because of Jeffries’s off-campus speech consistently with the First Amendment.
2. Whether the special verdicts and defendant-specific roles permit judgment for the officials or require further proceedings on causation and lawful same-decision grounds.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; citizen-speech classification, defendant-specific causation, and remand for O’Connor |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s classification of the Albany address as citizen speech, its limitation on reaction-generated disruption, and its defendant-specific application of *Mt. Healthy*.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Capacity and protection of the Albany speech

Holding and operative rule: Speech delivered off campus is citizen speech when it was not assigned, commissioned, controlled, authorized, or delivered as part of the employee’s teaching or administrative duties. A public university may not punish such speech solely because of controversy, audience hostility, reputational harm, or predicted disruption generated by reaction to its viewpoint. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Jeffries spoke at an unaffiliated Albany event about public-school curriculum and racial history. CUNY did not assign the address, approve its contents, sponsor the event, or authorize Jeffries to speak for the institution. The department-chair role was described in the record as ministerial rather than as an office carrying authority to set university policy. The address therefore was Jeffries’s own expression on a matter of public concern. A university retains authority over assigned classroom instruction, curricular decisions, departmental communications, delegated administrative speech, and independently regulable misconduct. It may also act on actual failure to perform official duties. It may not convert private expression into institutional speech merely because the speaker holds a university title, nor may it treat political pressure and hostility produced by the viewpoint itself as the sufficient constitutional ground for an adverse action. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/52/9/573564/))

##### Question 2: Defendant-specific causation and inconsistent verdicts

Holding and operative rule: The irreconcilable special verdicts and differentiated roles of the defendants require a limited new trial on defendant-specific causation and lawful same-decision grounds. The trustees’ votes could not be treated as automatically lawful superseding causes.

Reasoning: The verdicts simultaneously credited a prediction of disruption and found retaliatory motive, malice or reckless disregard, and but-for causation as to identified defendants. The defendants did not all perform the same acts. Some voted on the shortened chair term, while others allegedly proposed, shaped, or caused the matter to reach the Board. Under *Mt. Healthy*, Jeffries must prove that protected speech substantially motivated each defendant’s legally relevant conduct and that the conduct caused the adverse action. Each defendant may establish that the same action would have occurred for an independently lawful reason. Once reaction to protected private speech is removed as an automatically sufficient ground, the existing verdicts cannot support one appellate judgment resolving every defendant’s liability. A focused retrial, rather than appellate factfinding or reinstatement of internally inconsistent findings, is required.

#### Concurrences

O’Connor: The Albany address was citizen speech, and the special verdicts cannot sustain judgment for all defendants without defendant-specific causation findings. She would not categorically exclude every workplace consequence of public reaction from the received *Pickering* inquiry and therefore does not join that portion of the Court’s rule.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: CUNY could rely on a reasonable prediction that the chair’s speech would impair the department’s operation, notwithstanding the off-campus setting. The nine trustees’ independently cast votes supplied a lawful intervening basis for the action, so the judgment for the defendants should be affirmed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Waters v. Churchill*: narrowed; controversy, audience hostility, and reputational pressure generated by the viewpoint of off-duty citizen speech cannot alone supply the lawful disruption necessary to punish that speech.  
*United States v. National Treasury Employees Union*: extended; protection for citizen speech unrelated to official duties applies to an individualized adverse action grounded solely in reaction to the viewpoint expressed.  
*Board of County Commissioners v. Umbehr* and *O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake*: applied; retaliatory causation and lawful same-decision defenses remain defendant specific.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for a new trial limited to defendant-specific causation, lawful same-decision grounds, immunity, and relief.  
Effect: The Albany speech must be treated as Jeffries’s citizen speech. CUNY may defend the shortened chair term through independently lawful, conduct-based grounds, but not through controversy or reputational pressure generated solely by the viewpoint of the protected address.

### United States v. Rybar

Argued October 8, 1997 | Decided November 24, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: conditional convictions on two § 922(o) machinegun-possession counts affirmed, 103 F.3d 273 (3d Cir. 1996). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/103/273/518277/))  
Area of Law: Commerce Clause; Second Amendment; firearm regulation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether § 922(o) is constitutional under the Commerce Clause as applied to the two possession counts.
2. Whether those possession convictions violate the Second Amendment.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Second Amendment holding for Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s application of *United States v. Hale* to the commerce and Second Amendment questions.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Regulation of the machinegun market

Holding and operative rule: Section 922(o) is a valid regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market as applied to Rybar’s possession of the two machineguns involved in the gun-show transactions.

Reasoning: *United States v. Hale*, decided June 5, 1996, controls. Congress directly closed a defined national transfer market in post-1986 civilian machineguns and included possession because possession, acquisition, inventory, concealment, and transfer are functionally inseparable within that market. Excluding possession would create the principal route for evading the transfer prohibition. Rybar’s application falls within that regulated relationship. He possessed each weapon at a gun show in connection with a completed sale, although only the possession convictions are before the Court. The statute is not sustained through generalized consequences of gun crime, fear, insurance costs, or productivity loss. Its constitutional basis is the direct market prohibition and the anti-evasion work performed by the possession rule. That relationship distinguishes the school-zone possession offense invalidated in *United States v. Lopez*. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/103/273/518277/))

##### Question 2: Second Amendment challenge

Holding and operative rule: Section 922(o) does not violate the Second Amendment as applied to Rybar’s possession of fully automatic machineguns. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: *Hale* already resolved the weapon-category question at the same level of generality. *United States v. Miller* does not establish that every weapon possessing military utility is constitutionally protected, and the Court has not adopted a rule placing fully automatic machineguns within the Amendment’s protected core. The convictions therefore do not require resolution of the broader constitutional treatment of ordinary handguns, commonly possessed arms, home possession, public carriage, or licensing. Rybar’s completed sales provide factual context but do not convert the dismissed transfer allegations into convictions before the Court. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/103/273/518277/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas: He agrees that the Second Amendment does not invalidate these machinegun-possession convictions under controlling simulated law. He would reverse on the Commerce Clause question because § 922(o) contains no offense-specific commerce element and, in his view, cannot constitutionally reach intrastate possession through the anti-evasion rationale.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Hale*: followed; § 922(o) validly includes possession within the closed post-1986 civilian machinegun market and does not violate the Second Amendment as applied to fully automatic machineguns.  
*United States v. Lopez*: distinguished; the statute regulates a defined product market rather than local possession connected to commerce only through generalized consequences.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Rybar’s two § 922(o) possession convictions remain in force. The dismissed National Firearms Act transfer counts and the constitutional treatment of ordinary firearms remain outside the judgment.

### United States v. Parker

Argued October 15, 1997 | Decided December 1, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the criminal information reversed and prosecution reinstated, 108 F.3d 28 (3d Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/108/28/640649/))  
Area of Law: Commerce Clause; federalism; child-support enforcement

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Child Support Recovery Act of 1992 constitutionally reaches willful nonpayment of a valid child-support obligation owed to children residing in another State under the Commerce Clause and the Tenth Amendment.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s application of *United States v. Mussari*.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Commerce power over an interstate support obligation

Holding and operative rule: Congress may criminalize the willful failure to satisfy a valid child-support obligation owed to a child residing in another State because the statute directly protects an interstate payment obligation.

Reasoning: *United States v. Mussari*, decided June 30, 1997, controls the commerce question. Section 228 requires an existing support obligation fixed through valid state process, different-state residence of the obligor and child, and willful nonpayment. The different-state requirement performs constitutional work because satisfaction of the obligation ordinarily requires value to pass between States. The offense is therefore directed at the interstate obligation itself, not supported by an attenuated chain involving family hardship, public assistance, productivity, or other aggregate social consequences. Parker allegedly resided in Florida while the children entitled to payment resided in Pennsylvania. Whether the order remained operative, whether Parker possessed the ability to pay, and whether his default was willful remain questions for the federal prosecution. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/108/28/640649/))

##### Question 2: Tenth Amendment and domestic relations

Holding and operative rule: Section 228 regulates the private obligor and does not commandeer a State or transfer parentage, custody, or support-setting authority to a federal court.

Reasoning: The federal prosecution accepts the valid state support order as its predicate. It does not direct a state legislature, court, or executive officer to administer federal law, and it gives the federal tribunal no power to create or modify the family obligation. State law continues to determine parentage, custody, the amount of support, and any lawful modification. The federal court determines only the federal offense elements, including the existence of the predicate order, different-state residence, arrearage requirements, and willfulness. Direct regulation of Parker as a private person therefore neither violates the anti-commandeering rule nor displaces the States’ continuing domestic-relations authority.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas: Passive nonpayment of a state-created family obligation is not itself interstate commerce merely because the parties live in different States. He would reverse and dismiss because the statute reaches inactivity traditionally governed by state domestic-relations law.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Mussari*: followed; Congress may protect a legally operative interstate support-payment obligation while leaving its creation and modification to state law.  
*United States v. Lopez*: distinguished; § 228 contains a different-state element tied directly to the obligation being regulated.  
*Printz v. United States*: distinguished; the Act regulates a private obligor and commands no state officer to administer federal law.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed, and the federal prosecution may proceed on the existing state support order and the federal statutory elements.  
Effect: The information remains reinstated. The federal court may adjudicate willful nonpayment but may not determine parentage, custody, the amount of support, or disregard a lawful state modification.

### Foster v. Love

Argued October 6, 1997 | Decided December 2, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: injunction against Louisiana’s October-final-election feature affirmed, 90 F.3d 1026 (5th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522067/usrep522067.pdf))  
Area of Law: Elections; federal election timing; preemption

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Louisiana’s October open-primary system, which could finally elect federal officers before the federally prescribed November election day, conflicts with federal election statutes.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer; all except the statutory-history discussion for Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the October event cannot consummate the legal election of a federal officer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Uniform federal election day

Holding and operative rule: Louisiana’s system conflicts with federal law insofar as a candidate receiving an October majority is thereby elected to federal office and no legally operative election occurs on the federally prescribed November day.

Reasoning: Congress has exercised its express authority to establish a uniform day for congressional elections. The operative question is when the legal choice of the officer is consummated, not the label a State assigns to the procedure. Louisiana calls the October event an open primary, but a candidate receiving a majority in that event is finally elected and faces no November contest. The October proceeding therefore performs the legal function Congress assigned to the November election day. Louisiana remains free to conduct nominations, preliminary contests, and runoff procedures, and federal law does not prescribe a particular ballot form. It may not make the October result the final legal selection of a federal officer. Relief properly remains confined to that conflicting feature. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522067/usrep522067.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson*: applied; Congress may prescribe federal-election procedures administered through state electoral machinery.  
*Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*: applied; an operative federal timing command preempts a state procedure that consummates the election on a different day.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed, and the injunction shall remain limited to preventing Louisiana from finally choosing federal officers before the federal election day.  
Effect: Louisiana may retain an October preliminary contest, including its open-primary format, if the legally operative federal election occurs in November. The judgment does not regulate purely state offices.

### Richenberg v. Cohen

Argued November 3, 1997 | Decided December 8, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: denial of relief from Captain Richenberg’s discharge affirmed, 97 F.3d 256 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/97/256/555028/))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; military service; sexual-orientation classification

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Air Force may discharge a service member under a rule that treats a statement of homosexual identity as presumptive proof of prohibited conduct without a genuinely available individualized rebuttal.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s application of *Walmer v. Department of Defense* and *Thomasson v. Perry*.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Identity-statement presumption and individualized rebuttal

Holding and operative rule: Section 654’s identity-statement presumption must permit rebuttal through all evidence relevant to actual conduct, propensity, and intent without requiring denial of homosexual identity. Richenberg’s discharge cannot stand because the military identified no supported ground beyond identity and attraction.

Reasoning: *Walmer* subjects a military sexual-orientation classification to intermediate scrutiny, while *Thomasson* establishes that the statutory statement presumption must be genuinely rebuttable. Richenberg denied prohibited conduct and intent, presented a favorable service record, and supplied individualized evidence bearing on whether his statement predicted future misconduct. The discharge board nevertheless treated his refusal to deny identity, together with acknowledgment of attraction, as effectively defeating rebuttal. Those considerations merely restate the classification and do not establish a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship to discipline, readiness, or operational effectiveness. Professional military judgment remains entitled to substantial evidentiary weight, but it cannot substitute for individualized support or make identity itself conclusive. A statement that actually admits prohibited conduct or intent may be treated according to its content, and neutral conduct rules remain available. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/97/256/555028/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas: The military’s statement presumption was formally rebuttable, and Richenberg’s answers permitted the board to conclude that he had not overcome it. The dissent would defer to the professional military judgment supporting the statutory policy and affirm the discharge.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Walmer v. Department of Defense*: applied; the military bears the burden of establishing an important objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped fit.  
*Thomasson v. Perry*: followed; an identity-statement presumption must be genuinely rebuttable, and denial of identity cannot be made the practical condition of rebuttal.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to set aside Richenberg’s discharge under the challenged identity-statement presumption.  
Effect: The discharge may not rest on identity, attraction, or refusal to deny orientation. Any further military action must rest on an individualized, supported finding concerning actual conduct, propensity, intent, credibility, or another lawful ground.

### Hudson v. United States

Argued October 8, 1997 | Decided December 10, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the indictments reversed because the OCC sanctions did not bar later criminal prosecution, 92 F.3d 1026 (10th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522093/usrep522093.pdf))  
Area of Law: Double Jeopardy; civil sanctions; banking regulation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the OCC monetary assessments and occupational debarment were criminal punishment.
2. If so, whether the later prosecution was barred by the Double Jeopardy Clause.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: The OCC assessments and occupational debarment are civil on this record because their combined design and operation do not supply the clearest proof necessary to override Congress’s civil designation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Classification of the OCC sanctions

Holding and operative rule: The OCC monetary assessments and occupational debarment are civil on this record because their combined design and operation do not supply the clearest proof of criminal punishment. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Congress designated the monetary assessments as civil and entrusted debarment to banking regulators. That designation is not conclusive, but *United States v. Ursery* requires the clearest proof that a nominally civil measure is so punitive in design or actual operation that it must be classified as criminal punishment. The sanctions serve conventional regulatory purposes. Monetary assessments impose accountability for unsafe or unlawful banking conduct, while occupational debarment protects positions of trust within a regulated industry. Deterrence, severity, and a connection to conduct that also supports criminal liability are relevant but are not sufficient by themselves. Unlike the exaction in *Kurth Ranch*, these sanctions are not structured as an extraordinary post-conviction tax. Unlike the confinement invalidated in *Kansas v. Hendricks*, their actual operation does not defeat an asserted civil treatment or release structure. The demanding override requirement is therefore unmet. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522093/usrep522093.pdf))

##### Question 2: Same offense and successive prosecution

Not reached; the OCC proceedings did not impose criminal punishment, so no antecedent criminal sanction exists for comparison with the later indictment.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The judgment is correct because the OCC proceedings were not criminal prosecutions. They would continue to understand the Double Jeopardy Clause principally as a protection against successive criminal prosecutions rather than as a general judicial inquiry into multiple punishments imposed through civil proceedings.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Ursery*: followed; a civil designation controls unless combined design and actual operation provide the clearest proof of criminal punishment.  
*United States v. Halper*: limited; sanction-specific severity or disproportionality alone does not displace the civil-design and clearest-proof classification rule.  
*Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch*: distinguished; the OCC sanctions lack the extraordinary criminal trigger, timing, and operation of the punitive tax.  
*Kansas v. Hendricks*: distinguished; the record shows no civil structure defeated by prisonlike or otherwise punitive actual operation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is affirmed, and the criminal prosecutions may proceed.  
Effect: The administrative sanctions create no Double Jeopardy bar to the indictments. The Court does not decide the same-offense relationship, an Excessive Fines claim, or the classification of materially different banking sanctions.

---

### Kalina v. Fletcher

Argued October 7, 1997 | Decided December 10, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: absolute prosecutorial immunity denied for the prosecutor’s personally sworn factual certification, 93 F.3d 653 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522118/usrep522118.pdf))  
Area of Law: § 1983; prosecutorial immunity; arrest warrants

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a prosecutor has absolute immunity from damages for personally swearing to factual statements in a certification used to obtain an arrest warrant.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s function-specific distinction between advocacy and a complaining witness’s factual oath.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Function-specific absolute immunity

Holding and operative rule: A prosecutor does not receive absolute prosecutorial immunity for personally swearing to historical facts offered to establish probable cause. Genuine advocacy in preparing and presenting the charging case remains absolutely protected.

Reasoning: Absolute immunity follows the function performed rather than the official’s professional title. Preparing an information, selecting charges, and presenting a motion for an arrest warrant are advocative acts integral to the judicial process. Personally signing a factual certification under penalty of perjury is different. In executing that oath, Kalina supplied the evidentiary function ordinarily performed by a complaining witness. The complaint alleges that material statements in the certification were false, and those allegations must be accepted at this stage. The decision removes only absolute prosecutorial immunity from that particular act. It does not determine falsity, probable cause, causation, damages, or qualified immunity, and it does not withdraw absolute protection from Kalina’s charging and courtroom advocacy. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522118/usrep522118.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Common-law immunity did not protect a complaining witness from damages merely because the witness was also a prosecutor. The historical functional comparison therefore resolves the case without extending absolute immunity beyond its established boundary.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Imbler v. Pachtman* and *Buckley v. Fitzsimmons*: clarified; absolute prosecutorial immunity protects advocative functions, not every act performed by a prosecutor.  
*Malley v. Briggs*: applied; a prosecutor who personally swears to probable-cause facts performs the function of a complaining witness.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the claim concerning Kalina’s personal factual oath may proceed without absolute prosecutorial immunity.  
Effect: Fletcher may litigate the sworn-certification claim. Falsity, probable cause, causation, damages, and qualified immunity remain for the lower courts.

---

### General Electric Co. v. Joiner

Argued October 14, 1997 | Decided December 15, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment reversed after heightened appellate review of the exclusion of expert testimony, 78 F.3d 524 (11th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522136/usrep522136.pdf))  
Area of Law: Evidence; expert testimony; appellate review

Questions Presented:
1. What standard of appellate review governs a district court’s exclusion of expert testimony under Federal Rule of Evidence 702, and whether the exclusion here was permissible.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; appellate-review holding for Stevens |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Case-specific Rule 702 admission and exclusion decisions receive abuse-of-discretion review, and the district court acted within that discretion in rejecting the challenged causation opinions because the supporting data did not reliably bridge the inference required by the conclusions.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Appellate standard for Rule 702 rulings

Holding and operative rule: Case-specific decisions admitting or excluding expert evidence under Rule 702 are reviewed for abuse of discretion, including when the evidentiary ruling is outcome determinative. Interpretation of the Rule remains independently reviewable. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Trial judges must assess the relevance and reliability of expert testimony in the context of the record before them. That responsibility includes evaluating methodology, fit, factual assumptions, and whether the evidence can assist the trier of fact. Appellate courts preserve the governing legal boundaries, but they do not replace the trial court’s contextual assessment with an independent evaluation merely because exclusion leads to summary judgment. A heightened standard for dispositive evidentiary rulings would alter the ordinary allocation of trial and appellate responsibility and would create different review depending on the consequence of the same type of ruling. The Eleventh Circuit therefore applied the wrong standard.

##### Question 2: Analytical gap in the challenged causation opinions

Holding and operative rule: A district court may exclude an expert opinion when the data do not reliably support the inference required by the conclusion. The district court did not abuse its discretion in excluding the challenged PCB causation opinions.

Reasoning: The animal studies involved materially different exposure levels, species, and tumor types, while the epidemiological materials did not reliably support the proposition that Joiner’s exposure caused or promoted his small-cell lung cancer. Rule 702 does not require admission of an opinion connected to the underlying data only by the expert’s assertion. Conclusions and methodology are not wholly separable when the expert must make an inferential leap the cited research does not support. The district court identified that analytical gap and acted within the range of permissible gatekeeping judgment. This conclusion does not restore a general-acceptance requirement, permit exclusion merely because scientific evidence is disputed, or authorize a judge to choose between adequately supported competing conclusions. Questions concerning exposure to furans and dioxins, and the admissibility of a materially different combined-exposure theory, remain open. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522136/usrep522136.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer: Scientific and technical disputes may require active use of pretrial conferences, focused hearings, court-appointed experts, special masters, and other procedures authorized by the Rules. Those tools assist rather than replace the judge’s obligation to perform reliable Rule 702 gatekeeping.

#### Dissents

Stevens: He agrees that abuse of discretion is the governing appellate standard but would leave its application to the Eleventh Circuit on a complete record. The Court should not itself resolve the reliability of the challenged opinions when the briefing and record concerning the studies are incomplete.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc.*: clarified; case-specific Rule 702 rulings receive abuse-of-discretion review, and a court may reject an opinion when the analytical gap between data and conclusion is too great.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings applying abuse-of-discretion review consistently with the Court’s ruling on the challenged PCB causation opinions.  
Effect: The identified expert-evidence exclusion may stand. The separate questions concerning exposure to furans and dioxins and any combined-exposure theory remain unresolved.

---

### City of Chicago v. International College of Surgeons

Argued October 14, 1997 | Decided December 15, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: supplemental jurisdiction over the state administrative-review claims rejected and remand to state court directed, 91 F.3d 981 (7th Cir. 1996). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-522/pdf/USREPORTS-522-156.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; removal; supplemental jurisdiction

Questions Presented:
1. Whether removal of a complaint containing federal constitutional claims gives the district court supplemental jurisdiction over state-law claims seeking review of local administrative decisions.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s application of § 1367 to related claims arising from the same municipal landmark decisions.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Supplemental jurisdiction over administrative-review claims

Holding and operative rule: A district court possessing original jurisdiction over federal constitutional claims has supplemental jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1367 over related state-law claims seeking review of the same local administrative decisions.

Reasoning: The College pleaded substantial federal constitutional claims and state administrative-review claims in one action challenging the same landmark determinations. Those claims share the municipal proceedings, evidentiary record, alleged injuries, and requested relief, and therefore form part of the same Article III case or controversy. Section 1367 contains no exception for a state claim merely because state law assigns it an administrative-review form or describes the reviewing court’s task as appellate. *Peacock v. Thomas* is distinguishable because that case involved a new action, a new defendant, and a new basis for personal liability. Here the claims are components of one controversy between the existing parties. Recognition of statutory jurisdiction does not require the district court to retain every state claim. Discretion under § 1367(c), abstention principles, and ordinary remedial law remains analytically separate and follows the jurisdictional determination. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-522/pdf/USREPORTS-522-156.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens: State administrative-review claims invoke a form of appellate supervision that Congress did not assign to federal district courts. The dissent would affirm the remand rather than transform § 1367 into authority for federal review of local administrative adjudications.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Peacock v. Thomas*: distinguished; related claims against the same parties arising from the same administrative decisions are not a new liability action requiring an independent jurisdictional grant.  
*Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co.*: applied; the existence of federal jurisdiction is determined before abstention, retention, stay, or remand discretion is exercised.  
*United Mine Workers v. Gibbs*: followed; claims sharing a common nucleus of operative fact form one constitutional case or controversy.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court recognize supplemental jurisdiction before considering § 1367(c), abstention, retention, stay, or remand.  
Effect: The federal district court has adjudicatory power over the related state administrative-review claims. The Court does not require their retention, alter the governing state standards of review, or decide whether discretionary remand is appropriate.

---

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Tanford v. Brand

Argued November 3, 1997 | Decided December 22, 1997  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: challenge to the invocation and benediction rejected, 104 F.3d 982 (7th Cir. 1997). ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/104/104.F3d.982.96-3053.html))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Establishment Clause

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a public university violates the Establishment Clause by selecting clergy to deliver invocations and benedictions as part of its official commencement ceremony.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Official prayer at a public-university commencement

Holding and operative rule: When a public university selects clergy and conducts invocations and benedictions as part of its official commencement ceremony, it sponsors a religious exercise in violation of the Establishment Clause. Voluntary attendance and an adult audience do not cure the governmental sponsorship.

Reasoning: Indiana University determined that prayer would occur, selected the officiating clergy, placed the invocation and benediction in the official program, and presented the exercises through the authority and structure of the university ceremony. The expression was therefore governmental worship, not private religious speech receiving neutral access to a forum. *Lee v. Weisman* is not confined to secondary schools because the antecedent constitutional defect is the State’s organization and conduct of prayer. The audience’s maturity and formal ability to decline attendance reduce some forms of coercive pressure, but they do not authorize a public institution to make worship part of its own ceremony. The University may solemnize commencement through secular remarks and may permit genuinely private religious expression on neutral terms. It may not itself decide that prayer will be delivered and choose the religious officer who will deliver it. The historical record confirmed that the invocations and benedictions were recurring official features delivered by clergy selected through the University’s commencement process. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/104/104.F3d.982.96-3053.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: A brief nonsectarian invocation at a ceremony attended voluntarily by adults involves neither compulsory participation nor religious indoctrination. The dissent would treat the practice as a permissible ceremonial acknowledgment and would affirm.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lee v. Weisman*, 505 U.S. 577 (1992): extended; official sponsorship of prayer at a public institution’s own ceremony violates the Establishment Clause without regard to the audience’s age or the formal voluntariness of attendance.  
*Marsh v. Chambers*, 463 U.S. 783 (1983): declined to extend beyond the historically distinct legislative-prayer setting.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court enter appropriate prospective relief barring Indiana University from selecting or conducting invocations and benedictions as official worship.  
Effect: Indiana University may no longer place university-selected clergy-led prayer on its official commencement program. Nondevotional ceremonial remarks and genuinely private expression in a neutral forum remain available.

### Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education

Argued November 4, 1997 | Decided January 5, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the school defendants affirmed, 107 F.3d 1220 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/107/1220/502704/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; public employment; due process

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a public employer’s refusal to rehire employees because they filed lawful claims for unemployment benefits violates the Petition Clause, and whether a public employee’s petition must concern a matter of public concern before it receives First Amendment protection.
2. Whether substitute employees who possessed no entitlement to reemployment had a protected property interest in being rehired, or an independent substantive-due-process or equal-protection claim against the refusal to rehire them.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Petition Clause retaliation claim | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The claim returns for defendant-specific causation, lawful same-decision grounds, immunity, and relief. |
| Property, substantive-due-process, and equal-protection claims | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Summary judgment remains in force on these claims. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; property, substantive-due-process, and equal-protection holding for O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm in full | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the Petition Clause disposition, affirm the remaining dispositions, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s conclusion that a lawful, non-sham benefits claim is protected petitioning without a public-concern prerequisite, while agreeing that the drivers possessed no property entitlement to rehire and established no separate substantive-due-process or equal-protection claim.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retaliation for filing unemployment-benefit claims

Holding and operative rule: A public employee’s lawful, non-sham invocation of an established administrative process to claim benefits is protected by the Petition Clause and need not concern a matter of public concern. A public employer may not refuse reemployment solely because the petition required payment of benefits prescribed by law.

Reasoning: The drivers invoked the adjudicative process Ohio established to determine eligibility for unemployment compensation. Their claims sought governmental redress concerning benefits allegedly due to them and were neither fraudulent nor sham proceedings on the supplied record. The Petition Clause protects the act of asking government to remedy an asserted legal grievance; its protection is not confined to petitions concerning public policy or matters of general political interest. The public-concern requirement developed for employee speech does not automatically erase the distinct constitutional significance of a formal petition to a governmental decisionmaker. At the same time, the Clause does not guarantee success, continued employment, or immunity from neutral job requirements. The drivers must prove that their petitions substantially motivated the refusal to rehire and caused the challenged action. The defendants may establish that the same decisions would have occurred for independent performance, qualification, operational, or other lawful reasons. The record showed consecutive school-year contracts and a refusal to rehire attributed to the drivers’ lawful summer-benefit claims, permitting the retaliation issue to proceed. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/107/1220/502704/))

##### Question 2: Employment entitlement and alternative constitutional claims

Holding and operative rule: The drivers possessed no protected property entitlement to rehire, and the refusal to rehire establishes no independent substantive-due-process or equal-protection violation on this record. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: A property interest must arise from an independent source that meaningfully limits governmental discretion. The drivers served under consecutive nine-month contracts, the Board formally declined automatic renewal each year, and no statute, contract, rule, or mutually explicit understanding guaranteed placement on the next substitute list. Their prior service and expectation of favorable consideration therefore did not create an entitlement. The Petition Clause directly governs the asserted retaliation, so substantive due process does not supply a duplicate claim. The equal-protection theory identifies neither a protected classification nor a distinct irrational classification independent of the asserted retaliation. Those dispositions remain affirmed even though the First Amendment claim proceeds.

#### Concurrences

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The separate writing agrees that the drivers lacked a property interest and established no substantive-due-process or equal-protection claim. It would apply the public-concern requirement to the Petition Clause claim and affirm the judgment in full.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The dissenting portion concludes that public-employee petitions concerning only private employment benefits receive no greater protection than equivalent employee speech. Because the claims did not address a matter of public concern, these Justices would reject the retaliation claim.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Board of Regents v. Roth*, 408 U.S. 564 (1972): applied; repeated fixed-term employment does not create a property entitlement to renewal without an independent legal constraint on the employer’s discretion.  
*Connick v. Myers*, 461 U.S. 138 (1983): declined to extend; its public-concern requirement does not govern a lawful, non-sham petition invoking an established adjudicative benefits process.  
*Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*, 429 U.S. 274 (1977): applied; retaliatory causation and the lawful same-decision defense remain separate and defendant specific.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed as to the property, substantive-due-process, and equal-protection claims, reversed as to the Petition Clause claim, and remanded for proceedings on causation, lawful same-decision grounds, qualified immunity, damages, and other authorized relief.  
Effect: The drivers receive no entitlement to reemployment merely from their prior contracts. They may attempt to prove that district officials refused to rehire them solely because they filed lawful unemployment-benefit petitions.

---

### Baker v. General Motors Corp.

Argued October 15, 1997 | Decided January 13, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: judgment for the Bakers reversed because Elwell’s testimony was held inadmissible under the Michigan injunction, 86 F.3d 811 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522222/usrep522222.pdf))  
Area of Law: Full faith and credit; judgments; evidence

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Full Faith and Credit Clause requires a Missouri court to enforce a Michigan injunction barring a former General Motors employee from testifying in litigation against GM.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | O’Connor, Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s distinction between a judgment’s adjudicative consequences and an issuing court’s commands governing proceedings elsewhere.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Extraterritorial effect of the Michigan injunction

Holding and operative rule: Full faith and credit does not require one State’s court to enforce an injunction as an evidentiary exclusion rule in a different State’s action involving parties who were neither parties nor privies to the original proceeding. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Section 1738 requires recognition of the adjudicative effect the rendering State gives its judgment, subject to jurisdiction and due process. It does not transfer the issuing court’s enforcement machinery or give that court authority over another sovereign’s tribunal. Michigan could bind Elwell and GM within the lawful scope of their consent judgment, and ordinary claim or issue preclusion could attach to matters actually adjudicated between persons legally bound. The Bakers, however, were strangers to the Michigan litigation, exercised no control over it, and were not represented there. Michigan acquired no authority over the Missouri proceeding or its evidentiary process. The injunction therefore could not require the Missouri court to exclude otherwise competent testimony concerning a separate products-liability dispute. GM remains free to assert privilege, trade-secret protection, work-product protection, preclusion, and ordinary evidentiary objections. The judgment does not create a public-policy exception to full faith and credit; it enforces the boundary between recognition of a judgment and extraterritorial administration of another court’s proceedings. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522222/usrep522222.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia: Full faith and credit carries the legal effect of a judgment, not the particular enforcement methods available to the rendering court. The Michigan injunction therefore could not operate as Missouri’s own evidentiary command.

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: The Bakers were nonparties, and the Michigan judgment did not decide their rights or the admissibility of evidence in their action. That narrower nonparty ground is sufficient to reverse.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein*, 516 U.S. 367 (1996): applied; a court first identifies the rendering State’s adjudicative effect, but § 1738 does not enlarge the rendering court’s jurisdiction.  
*Richards v. Jefferson County*, 517 U.S. 793 (1996): followed; nonparties are not bound absent a recognized relationship and constitutionally adequate representation.  
*Fall v. Eastin*, 215 U.S. 1 (1909): applied; a judgment’s adjudicative consequences may travel while the issuing court’s enforcement mechanisms do not automatically travel with it.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for consideration of Elwell’s testimony under ordinary evidentiary, privilege, preclusion, and protective-order principles rather than as automatically excluded by the Michigan injunction.  
Effect: The Michigan judgment remains enforceable between persons and conduct it lawfully binds. It does not control what evidence the Missouri tribunal may hear in the Bakers’ separate action.

---

### Lee v. Harcleroad

Argued November 5, 1997 | Decided January 20, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: district-court injunction vacated and dismissal for lack of a justiciable controversy directed, 107 F.3d 1382 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/107/1382/502927/))  
Area of Law: Article III; standing; ripeness

Questions Presented:
1. Whether physicians, terminally ill patients, and care facilities presented a ripe Article III controversy challenging Oregon’s physician-assisted-suicide law before a concrete enforcement or application dispute.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the asserted injuries are contingent, nonimminent, and insufficient to support pre-enforcement adjudication.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Standing and ripeness before a concrete application

Holding and operative rule: Plaintiffs lack standing and their claims are unripe when the asserted injuries depend on an unformed chain of future eligibility, requests, independent medical decisions, safeguard failures, and unidentified participants, with no present compliance burden or credible enforcement threat.

Reasoning: The patient claim depended on future terminal eligibility, a later request for medication, the participation of physicians not yet identified, repeated erroneous assessments of competence and voluntariness, failures by statutory witnesses and safeguards, and eventual ingestion contrary to the patient’s true wishes. That sequence was possible but not actual or imminent. The physicians identified no present patient request, defined medical act, concrete statutory command, current compliance expenditure, or credible threat of enforcement. The facilities similarly showed no identified resident, present participation conflict, or immediate legal consequence. Oregon’s enactment therefore had not been applied to any plaintiff in a manner permitting the Court to determine injury, causation, or the legality of a concrete governmental act. The dismissal remains without prejudice. The Court expresses no view on the statute’s compatibility with due process, equal protection, federal disability law, or any other merits theory. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/107/1382/502927/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife*, 504 U.S. 555 (1992): applied; injury depending on multiple independent future contingencies is not actual or imminent.  
*City of Los Angeles v. Lyons*, 461 U.S. 95 (1983): applied; the possibility that a challenged procedure may injure someone does not establish that the named plaintiff faces the required personal threat.  
*Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno*, decided April 28, 1997: distinguished; Oregon’s law had not directly prohibited an existing operation, caused immediate cessation, or created a present credible enforcement threat against these plaintiffs.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the action shall remain dismissed without prejudice for lack of standing and ripeness.  
Effect: The decision neither validates nor invalidates Oregon’s statute. An eligible patient facing imminent application, a provider confronting a concrete statutory duty and credible enforcement threat, or a facility with an identified participation conflict may pursue an appropriate later challenge.

### South Dakota v. Yankton Sioux Tribe

Argued December 8, 1997 | Decided January 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: judgment that the reservation remained intact affirmed, 99 F.3d 1439 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522329/usrep522329.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal Indian law; reservation diminishment

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the 1894 surplus-land agreement diminished the Yankton Sioux Reservation and, if so, the territorial extent of that diminishment.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the enacted cession language and fixed-sum arrangement diminished the reservation as to the ceded surplus lands without establishing total disestablishment.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Diminishment of the ceded surplus lands

Holding and operative rule: The 1894 Act diminished the Yankton Sioux Reservation by removing the ceded, unallotted surplus lands from reservation status. The present record does not establish that Congress disestablished the reservation altogether or eliminated the status of retained tribal and trust lands.

Reasoning: The inquiry begins with the enacted text and structure. The Tribe agreed to cede, sell, relinquish, and convey all interests in its unallotted lands, and the United States agreed to pay a fixed sum rather than merely act as the Tribe’s sales agent and remit proceeds from later settlement. That structure is characteristic of a present surrender of territory. Contemporaneous descriptions and the rapid transfer of the ceded lands reinforce the textual conclusion. Later governmental references to the reservation were inconsistent and cannot overcome the stronger statutory evidence. Demographic and jurisdictional history provides additional confirmation but does not independently create diminishment. The Court limits its judgment to the land actually ceded. The landfill site lies on ceded non-Indian fee land and is outside the diminished reservation boundary, but questions concerning allotments, trust parcels, land still owned by the Tribe, and surviving federal or tribal authority remain for any necessary proceedings below. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522329/usrep522329.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Hagen v. Utah*, 510 U.S. 399 (1994): followed; operative cession language and a fixed-sum payment structure may establish congressional intent to diminish a reservation.  
*Solem v. Bartlett*, 465 U.S. 463 (1984): applied; statutory text and contemporaneous understanding carry primary weight, while later demographic and jurisdictional treatment remains secondary evidence.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to recognize that the ceded surplus lands, including the disputed landfill site, are outside the diminished reservation and to resolve any remaining parcel-specific or statutory questions consistently with that boundary.  
Effect: South Dakota may exercise the authority that follows from the landfill site’s location outside the reservation, subject to any independent federal restriction. The judgment does not declare the entire historic reservation disestablished.

---

### Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB

Argued October 15, 1997 | Decided January 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: Board order enforced, 83 F.3d 1483 (D.C. Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522359/usrep522359.pdf))  
Area of Law: Labor law; administrative law; substantial evidence

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the NLRB’s good-faith reasonable-doubt standard for employer polling is rational and consistent with the National Labor Relations Act.
2. Whether the Board’s finding that Allentown lacked such a doubt is supported by substantial evidence on the record as a whole.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Part I for all participating Justices; Part II for Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Parts III and IV for Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete separate writing |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins Part I and Parts III and IV of the Opinion of the Court and authors an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas.  
Announced ground: He agrees that the Board’s finding is unsupported by substantial evidence. He would also hold that the existing unitary reasonable-doubt threshold is insufficiently grounded in the Act when applied identically to a safeguarded poll and the materially more consequential act of withdrawing recognition.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Facial validity of the employer-polling standard

Holding and operative rule: The Board’s good-faith reasonable-doubt standard for a safeguarded employer poll is facially rational and consistent with the National Labor Relations Act. (joined by Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The Act protects employee choice while permitting the Board to preserve stability in established bargaining relationships. An employer poll can interfere with that relationship or create pressure even when conducted through secret ballot and other safeguards. The Board therefore may require an objective basis for doubt before permitting the employer to initiate the poll. Polling, a Board-supervised election, and withdrawal of recognition have different consequences, but those differences do not make it irrational to use a common reasonable-doubt threshold. The Board could rationally judge a poll more intrusive than a formal election yet less disruptive than unilateral withdrawal and place the threshold between the competing considerations. The Court does not decide that the standard is the only permissible one or that every procedural safeguard is independently required. It holds only that the announced standard falls within the Board’s statutory authority.

##### Question 2: Substantial evidence supporting the Board’s finding

Holding and operative rule: The Board’s finding that Allentown lacked a good-faith reasonable doubt about the union’s continuing majority support is unsupported by substantial evidence on the record as a whole.

Reasoning: The Board accepted that at least seven of the thirty-two employees had expressed opposition to the union. Statements from additional employees and a union steward further indicated that support had materially eroded. The Board discounted those statements as speculative while effectively demanding proof approaching actual loss of majority support, even though its announced standard asked only whether the employer possessed a good-faith reasonable doubt. An agency must apply the legal standard it publicly states. It cannot preserve one nominal standard while imposing a materially more demanding evidentiary rule through adjudicative practice. Substantial-evidence review remains deferential, but it requires enough evidence for a reasonable factfinder applying the correct substantive standard. No evidence identified by the Board adequately counterbalanced the employee statements, and the independently supervised secret-ballot poll itself complied with the procedural safeguards found applicable by the administrative law judge. The existing bargaining order therefore cannot stand. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522359/usrep522359.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stone, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: Polling does not alter bargaining rights in the same manner as withdrawal of recognition, and the Act does not adequately support making both actions depend on the same stringent threshold. These Justices nevertheless agree that accepting the Board’s standard as valid, the finding against Allentown is unsupported.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The Board’s standard is lawful, and the whole record permits the agency to discount ambiguous employee statements made during a change in ownership. These Justices would defer to the Board’s evidentiary assessment and affirm enforcement.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Auciello Iron Works, Inc. v. NLRB*, 517 U.S. 781 (1996): distinguished; the binding consequences of voluntary recognition do not determine how evidence of later employee doubt must be evaluated.  
*Universal Camera Corp. v. NLRB*, 340 U.S. 474 (1951): applied and clarified; substantial-evidence review tests the agency’s finding against the substantive standard the agency publicly announces and the record considered as a whole.  
*Fall River Dyeing & Finishing Corp. v. NLRB*, 482 U.S. 27 (1987): applied; an incumbent union’s presumption of majority support becomes rebuttable after the protected conclusive period.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to deny enforcement of the challenged Board order.  
Effect: The Board’s good-faith reasonable-doubt polling standard remains facially valid. The order against Allentown cannot be enforced because the Board did not support its application of that standard with substantial evidence, and the agency may not substitute a new evidentiary threshold retroactively in this proceeding.
