### Brogan v. United States

Argued December 2, 1997 | Decided January 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: conviction affirmed, 96 F.3d 35 (2d Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522398/usrep522398.pdf))  
Area of Law: Criminal law; false statements; statutory interpretation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether 18 U.S.C. § 1001 contains an exculpatory-no exception for a person who falsely denies wrongdoing to federal investigators.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas; all except the discussion of prosecutorial abuse for Souter |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete separate writing |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | Souter | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that § 1001’s enacted language contains no exculpatory-no exception and that the Fifth Amendment protects silence rather than a false answer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: False denial to federal investigators

Holding and operative rule: Section 1001 applies to a knowingly false, material negative answer given to federal investigators. The statute contains no judicially created exculpatory-no exception.

Reasoning: The statute reaches any materially false statement made knowingly and willfully in a matter within federal agency jurisdiction. A direct negative answer is a statement, and the enacted text supplies no distinction between a false denial and a longer affirmative fabrication. The proposed exception also lacks a stable boundary. Its application could depend on whether an investigator asked one question or several, whether the suspect added explanatory words, or whether the denial preceded a more detailed account. Those distinctions do not arise from the statute. The Fifth Amendment permitted Brogan to decline to answer, but it did not confer a privilege to answer falsely. Concerns that investigators may invite an additional charge after already obtaining evidence of the underlying offense bear on materiality, mens rea, charging policy, and possible congressional revision. They do not authorize the Court to add a categorical defense. Brogan knowingly denied receiving cash or gifts, and the jury found the denial materially false. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522398/usrep522398.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter: He joins the statutory holding and affirmance but not the Court’s response to concerns about prosecutorial use of § 1001. On that concern, he joins Ginsburg’s separate opinion.

Ginsburg, joined by Souter: The statute’s text reaches Brogan’s denial, but its breadth permits investigators to generate an additional felony through questioning after the Government already possesses contrary evidence. Congress should consider whether the statute requires a more confined rule.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Breyer: The longstanding exculpatory-no doctrine reflects a narrow construction of a criminal statute and avoids converting an instinctive denial during a government interrogation into a separate felony. The dissent would reverse the § 1001 conviction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bryson v. United States*, 396 U.S. 64 (1969): followed; a person may remain silent or challenge the Government’s demand through lawful means but may not knowingly answer with a material falsehood.  
*Hubbard v. United States*, 514 U.S. 695 (1995): distinguished; its construction of the statutory term department or agency does not authorize a separate exception for false denials made within an agency’s jurisdiction.  
*United States v. Gilliland*, 312 U.S. 86 (1941): applied; § 1001 is not confined to statements made to obtain a governmental benefit.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Brogan’s § 1001 conviction remains in force. The decision does not resolve coercive interrogation, literal truth, ambiguous questioning, immaterial statements, or claims concerning improper charging discretion.

### Oubre v. Entergy Operations, Inc.

Argued November 12, 1997 | Decided January 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for Entergy affirmed, 112 F.3d 787 (5th Cir. 1996) (per curiam). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522422/usrep522422.pdf))  
Area of Law: Employment law; ADEA waivers; statutory conditions

Questions Presented:
1. Whether an employee’s retention of severance benefits ratifies an ADEA release that fails the Older Workers Benefit Protection Act’s mandatory waiver requirements.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | O’Connor | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s enforcement of the OWBPA’s mandatory waiver conditions and its separation of waiver validity from any later setoff or restitution issue.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Effect of a noncompliant ADEA release

Holding and operative rule: An ADEA release that fails the OWBPA’s mandatory requirements is unenforceable as an ADEA waiver. Retention of severance payments does not cure the defect through ratification, and tendering the payments back is not a precondition to filing the ADEA action.

Reasoning: Section 626(f) states that an individual may not waive an ADEA claim unless the waiver is knowing and voluntary and satisfies enumerated minimum conditions. Entergy’s release omitted required consideration and revocation periods and did not specifically refer to ADEA claims. The release therefore could have no effect on Oubre’s ADEA claim. Common-law ratification cannot supply statutory compliance after the fact because retaining the consideration satisfies none of the missing requirements. A tender-back prerequisite would produce the same forbidden result indirectly by preventing suit unless the employee first honored a release Congress declared insufficient. It would also weaken the statute’s operation because a discharged employee may have spent the severance payment before discovering the defect. The employer that seeks an ADEA waiver bears responsibility for satisfying the conditions Congress prescribed. Any independently authorized claim for restitution, recoupment, or setoff remains distinct and may be considered without treating repayment as a condition of access to the federal cause of action. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522422/usrep522422.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer, joined by O’Connor: The invalid waiver does not necessarily entitle an employee to retain both the severance consideration and an unreduced recovery. Ordinary setoff, restitution, and recoupment questions remain available if authorized and should be resolved separately from the right to bring suit.

#### Dissents

Scalia: General contract principles should remain available unless Congress clearly displaced them. He would permit ratification or tender-back rules to govern a release whose original execution was defective.

Thomas: The OWBPA specifies conditions governing the original waiver but does not expressly abolish ratification of a voidable agreement or the traditional tender-back requirement. He would affirm the judgment for Entergy.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lockheed Corp. v. Spink*, 517 U.S. 882 (1996): distinguished; the validity of offering benefits in exchange for releases under ERISA does not satisfy the OWBPA’s separate mandatory conditions for an ADEA waiver.  
Common-law ratification and tender-back doctrines: limited; they cannot give effect to an ADEA waiver that fails 29 U.S.C. § 626(f).

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of Oubre’s ADEA claim and any independently authorized restitution, recoupment, or setoff issue.  
Effect: Oubre may pursue the ADEA action without first returning the severance payments. The decision does not establish liability on the discrimination claim or prevent enforcement of a waiver that complies with § 626(f).

### Alexander v. Whitman

Argued November 5, 1997 | Decided February 2, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the federal constitutional claims affirmed, 114 F.3d 1392 (3d Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/114/1392/493912/))  
Area of Law: Fourteenth Amendment; personhood; wrongful death

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a fetus stillborn as a result of prenatal injury is a person protected by the Equal Protection or Due Process Clauses of the Fourteenth Amendment for purposes of state wrongful-death and survival remedies.
2. Whether conditioning wrongful-death and survival causes of action on live birth violates the mother’s substantive-liberty or equal-protection rights.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusions that prenatal potential life is distinct from Fourteenth Amendment personhood and that New Jersey may use live birth as an administrable remedial boundary.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Fourteenth Amendment personhood before live birth

Holding and operative rule: A fetus that was not born alive is not a person within the Fourteenth Amendment under the constitutional law governing this case. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The constitutional text, its received legal operation, and controlling precedent do not establish prenatal personhood. Existing doctrine recognizes that government possesses legitimate and substantial interests in potential life, but that recognition is not equivalent to assigning the fetus independent constitutional personhood. New Jersey’s refusal to open an estate or derivative federal personhood remedy for a stillborn fetus therefore does not deny a constitutional right belonging to the fetus. The Court does not minimize the alleged prenatal injury or the loss involved. It decides only the federal constitutional status necessary to the claims presented. The mother’s own tort, malpractice, bodily-injury, and emotional-distress claims remain governed by state law and are not extinguished by this judgment. The record concerned a fetus near term that appeared healthy shortly before delivery but was stillborn; those medical allegations do not alter the constitutional category. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/114/1392/493912/))

##### Question 2: New Jersey’s live-birth remedial line

Holding and operative rule: New Jersey’s live-birth prerequisite for fetal estate, survival, and derivative wrongful-death remedies is rationally related to administrable boundaries concerning legal personhood, estate creation, causation, and damages. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The Constitution does not require a State to create every tort or derivative remedy that it might rationally recognize. Live birth supplies a clear legal event at which an independent estate and the associated derivative claims arise. That line may produce difficult outcomes near birth, but imperfection does not make it irrational. It provides a uniform rule and avoids converting uncertain medical distinctions concerning gestational status, independent survival, and causation into a constitutional mandate. The classification neither burdens a fundamental right belonging to the plaintiffs nor targets a suspect class. New Jersey remains free to enact broader prenatal-injury or stillbirth remedies, and its existing law may preserve claims belonging directly to the pregnant person. The Fourteenth Amendment does not compel the additional causes of action sought here.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The judgment may be affirmed without reaffirming the Court’s prenatal-personhood precedents. The Constitution does not require a State to create wrongful-death, probate, or survival causes of action for prenatal injury, and that narrower ground resolves the case.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Roe v. Wade*, 410 U.S. 113 (1973): followed at the stated scope; the Fourteenth Amendment’s use of person does not include prenatal life.  
*Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey*, 505 U.S. 833 (1992): applied; the State’s constitutionally cognizable interest in potential life is distinct from recognition of fetal constitutional personhood.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The federal constitutional claims remain dismissed. New Jersey may retain its live-birth threshold or adopt broader state remedies, and the mother’s independently available claims remain unaffected.

---

### United States v. Fields

Argued November 5, 1997 | Decided February 9, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: convictions affirmed and suppression ruling sustained, 113 F.3d 313 (2d Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/113/313/489516/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; visual observation; exigent circumstances

Questions Presented:
1. Whether officers conducted a Fourth Amendment search when they entered the side yard of a multiunit residence and observed a bedroom’s interior through a narrow opening beneath the window blinds.
2. Whether the subsequent warrantless entry was justified by exigent circumstances after police activity alerted the occupants, or whether the asserted risk of evidence destruction was unlawfully created by the officers.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Observation holding for Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; police-created-exigency holding for Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports vacatur and remand | Observation holding and remand only |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Officers conducted a search by deliberately entering a fenced, nonpublic vantage unavailable from the public way to obtain a view into a substantially screened bedroom. The later warrantless entry could not rest on an evidence-destruction risk foreseeably produced by the officers’ decision to execute a tactic designed to alert or flush the occupants rather than pursue a warrant.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Deliberate observation from a restricted vantage

Holding and operative rule: Officers conducted a Fourth Amendment search when, without lawful access or permission, they entered the fenced, nonpublic side yard to obtain a deliberate view through a narrow opening into the substantially screened bedroom.

Reasoning: Fields had a key, paid regularly for access, entered without the tenant’s presence, and repeatedly used the apartment with permission. Crawley was present as Fields’s invited guest. Those relationships supported a legitimate privacy interest in the bedroom even though neither defendant held the lease. The side yard was used by occupants of the multiunit building, but it was fenced, unavailable from the public street, and not shown to be open to police or the general public. Officers entered the rear and side yards because the bedroom could not be seen from a public way. The partial gap in the blinds did not publish the room to every observer regardless of vantage. Plain-view principles require lawful presence at the place from which the observation occurs. The holding does not create an exclusive privacy interest in every common area. It addresses the deliberate acquisition of home-interior information from a restricted vantage officers entered specifically to obtain that view. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/113/313/489516/))

##### Question 2: Police-created evidence-destruction exigency

Holding and operative rule: Officers may not justify a warrantless residential entry by an evidence-destruction risk foreseeably produced by their deliberate choice to alert or flush the occupants rather than pursue an available warrant. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Once the officers observed the apparent narcotics activity, they possessed the factual basis they later invoked as probable cause. They made no attempt to obtain a warrant. Instead, they planned to send officers to the front door while other officers waited behind the building, anticipating that discovery of the police would cause the occupants to flee with the drugs. As officers moved into position, a person on the rear porch announced the police presence, after which officers immediately forced entry based on a feared destruction of evidence. The warning and ensuing movement were not an independent emergency arising during ordinary investigation. They were the predictable consequence of the chosen roust-and-entry plan. Permitting the resulting risk to supply the warrant exception would allow officers to manufacture the condition on which warrantless entry depends. Independent danger, hot pursuit, an unforeseen destruction risk, or another emergency not created through such a tactic remains sufficient when its requirements are proved. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/113/313/489516/))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The officers’ use of the fenced side yard to obtain the otherwise unavailable bedroom view was a search, and the resulting judgment must be vacated for remedial analysis. They would not adopt the Court’s broader foreseeability rule for police-created exigency and would leave the later entry to be assessed after the consequences of the initial search are determined.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Kennedy: The side yard was a common area available to the building’s tenants, and the defendants left a plainly visible opening beneath the blinds. The later warning, the suspected quantity of narcotics, Fields’s known violence, and the likelihood of flight or destruction supplied adequate exigent circumstances, so the judgment should be affirmed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Pinson*, decided May 8, 1995, and *United States v. Myers*, decided June 11, 1996: clarified; their protection against deliberate acquisition of otherwise unavailable home-interior information extends to a restricted physical vantage, while ordinary observation from a lawful public vantage remains unaffected.  
*California v. Ciraolo*, 476 U.S. 207 (1986), and *Florida v. Riley*, 488 U.S. 445 (1989): distinguished; those observations occurred from lawful public vantage points rather than a fenced, nonpublic area entered to obtain the view.  
*Richards v. Wisconsin*, 520 U.S. 385 (1997): distinguished; case-specific authority governing execution of an existing warrant does not authorize officers to manufacture the factual predicate for warrantless residential entry.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for determination of suppression, consent, independent source, attenuation, objectively reasonable reliance, and the effect of the later warrant under the holdings stated here.  
Effect: The Court enters no final suppression order and does not itself vacate the convictions. Their continued validity depends on whether the Government can establish a lawful basis for the evidence independent of the unconstitutional observation and any impermissibly created exigency.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Johnson v. Rodriguez

Argued November 10, 1997 | Decided February 17, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: due-process and equal-protection claims rejected and a limited retaliation theory remanded, 110 F.3d 299 (5th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/110/299/527119/))  
Area of Law: Due process; equal protection; First Amendment retaliation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether denial of parole implicated a protected liberty interest, denied equal protection, or unlawfully retaliated against a prisoner for filing legal complaints and assisting other prisoners.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Protest-letter due-process and equal-protection claims | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Dismissal of these claims remains in force. |
| Retaliation standard and classwide relief | Vacated and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The lower courts shall separate protected from unprotected litigation, apply defendant-specific causation and the lawful same-decision defense, and reassess the basis and scope of prospective relief. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to Question 1 | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Question 1 for Scalia and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Parole files, liberty, and equal protection

Holding and operative rule: Texas’s discretionary parole system creates no protected liberty interest in release. Consideration of allegedly false or irrelevant confidential material therefore does not itself violate procedural due process, and the protest-letter process establishes no equal-protection violation without purposeful classification or irrational treatment of an identifiable group.

Reasoning: State law preserved broad discretion to deny parole and imposed no substantive command requiring release upon satisfaction of specified conditions. Without an entitlement to parole, the Due Process Clause does not make the federal courts general supervisors of the accuracy, relevance, or disclosure of every item in a confidential parole file. The Equal Protection Clause remains independently applicable, but Johnson identified no protected classification, formally adopted criterion, or pattern establishing that the Board intentionally divided similarly situated applicants through the unpredictable receipt of protest letters. The judgment does not approve reliance on knowingly fabricated evidence when another constitutional prohibition applies, create a right to an adversarial parole hearing, or require disclosure of confidential submissions. It holds only that the theories pleaded here fail without a protected entitlement or a proved classification. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/110/299/527119/))

##### Question 2: Retaliation for protected litigation and institutional relief

Holding and operative rule: A parole board may not deny discretionary parole because a prisoner pursued protected, nonfrivolous litigation. The claimant must show that protected activity substantially motivated the adverse decision; the responsible official may then prove that the same decision would have occurred for independently lawful reasons.

Reasoning: The absence of an entitlement to parole does not authorize government to withhold that benefit for a constitutionally forbidden reason. Protected activity includes a prisoner’s own nonfrivolous direct appeal, habeas petition, and conditions-of-confinement litigation. Assistance to another prisoner is protected only when it is necessary to preserve that person’s meaningful access to court, not as an unrestricted constitutional right to conduct jailhouse legal practice. Johnson must identify the protected activity, the responsible decisionmaker, and its causal role in the parole denial. The Board may rely on criminal history, institutional conduct, release risk, or another lawful ground and may prevail by establishing that it would have reached the same result. Classwide prospective relief additionally requires Johnson’s own continuing injury, an ongoing official policy or widespread authorized practice, corresponding injury to the class, and an order fitted to the proved violation. Isolated references to litigiousness or unauthorized employee conduct cannot sustain a systemwide injunction. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/110/299/527119/))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: They agree that Texas created no parole entitlement and that the protest-letter theories fail. They also agree that unrestricted assistance to other prisoners is not independently protected.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: They would leave the Fifth Circuit’s narrower retaliation standard undisturbed. In their view, the constitutional right of access requires concrete impairment of a nonfrivolous legal claim and should not be extended to treat denial of discretionary parole as a distinct injury merely because litigation was considered.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Sandin v. Conner* and *Young v. Harper*: applied; Texas’s discretionary parole system creates no protected entitlement to release.  
*Lewis v. Casey*: clarified; protected access encompasses nonfrivolous litigation and necessary legal assistance, while institutional relief requires named-plaintiff injury and a proved systemwide violation.  
*Board of County Commissioners v. Umbehr* and *O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake*: extended; the unconstitutional-conditions rule reaches denial of discretionary parole because of protected litigation.  
*Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*: applied; retaliatory causation and the lawful same-decision defense must be resolved defendant by defendant.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed as to the protest-letter claims, vacated as to the governing retaliation standard and remedial scope, and remanded for proceedings consistent with this opinion.  
Effect: Johnson receives no parole entitlement and no general right to correction or disclosure of his confidential file. He may attempt to prove that protected, nonfrivolous litigation caused an adverse parole decision, but classwide relief requires proof of an ongoing authorized institutional violation.

---

### Regions Hospital v. Shalala

Argued December 1, 1997 | Decided February 24, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: judgment sustaining the Secretary’s graduate-medical-education reaudit rule affirmed, 91 F.3d 57 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522448/usrep522448.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; Medicare reimbursement; retroactivity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Secretary may reopen and reaudit a hospital’s base-year graduate-medical-education costs when calculating later Medicare reimbursement.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s distinction between the closed 1984 payment and the historical cost figure used in later reimbursement calculations.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Reaudit of the base-year cost figure

Holding and operative rule: The Secretary may correct the allowable 1984 graduate-medical-education cost figure for future reimbursement years and for later payment determinations that remain independently open. That use does not reopen the final 1984 payment and is not impermissibly retroactive.

Reasoning: The statutory formula uses allowable 1984 graduate-medical-education costs as an input for calculating reimbursement in later years. Finality of the 1984 payment protects the amount paid for that closed year; it does not create a perpetual right to carry a cost that was not allowable under the governing base-year rules into every later calculation. The reaudit rule distinguishes those matters. It authorizes correction of the historical input used prospectively and in later payment years still open under independently applicable procedures, while prohibiting revision or recoupment of the closed 1984 determination. Under *Landgraf*, a present rule does not operate retroactively merely because it consults an antecedent fact. The hospital remains free to contest whether a particular disallowance follows the governing cost rules, whether the audit complied with required procedure, and whether any later year was actually open. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522448/usrep522448.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: The statutory base is the amount recognized as reasonable in the settled base year, not an independently reconstructed amount. They would hold that the Secretary cannot preserve the final 1984 payment while replacing the determination from which Congress directed later payments to be calculated.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Landgraf v. USI Film Products*: applied; use of an antecedent fact in a later or independently open calculation is not retroactive when no new legal consequence is attached to the closed payment.  
*Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital*: followed; Medicare reimbursement must conform to the enacted formula and valid contemporaneous cost rules.  
*Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala*: applied; the Secretary’s interpretation is not automatically conclusive, but the reaudit rule is sustained by the statute’s prospective reimbursement structure.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The Secretary may use the corrected allowable 1984 cost figure in future reimbursement calculations and in later payment years that remain independently open. The Secretary may not use this judgment to revise or recoup the closed 1984 payment or to disregard applicable reopening limits.

### Rivet v. Regions Bank of Louisiana

Argued January 21, 1998 | Decided February 24, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: removal and dismissal sustained on the ground that a prior federal judgment completely preempted the state action, 108 F.3d 576 (5th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522470/usrep522470.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; removal; claim preclusion

Questions Presented:
1. Whether claim preclusion based on a prior federal judgment creates federal-question removal jurisdiction under the complete-preemption doctrine.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s separation of removal jurisdiction from the merits of a federal claim-preclusion defense.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Removal based on a prior federal judgment

Holding and operative rule: Federal claim preclusion based on an earlier federal judgment is a defense and does not authorize removal through complete preemption.

Reasoning: Federal-question jurisdiction ordinarily exists only when federal law supplies an element of the plaintiff’s properly pleaded cause of action. Claim preclusion does not replace a state cause of action with a federal one. It supplies a defense that may require dismissal after a court possessing jurisdiction determines the effect of the earlier judgment. Complete preemption is different. It arises only when Congress has displaced a state cause of action and supplied an exclusive federal substitute, thereby making the claim federal from its inception. Treating every federal preclusion defense as complete preemption would permit removal whenever a defendant invoked an earlier federal decree and would consume the well-pleaded-complaint rule. The state court remains competent and obligated to determine the prior federal judgment’s preclusive effect under governing federal law. The federal courts lacked removal jurisdiction and therefore could not enter a merits dismissal. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522470/usrep522470.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Caterpillar Inc. v. Williams*: reaffirmed; a federal defense does not create federal-question removal jurisdiction.  
*Federated Department Stores, Inc. v. Moitie*: clarified; federal claim preclusion may defeat a state claim but does not transform it into a federal cause of action.  
*Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Taylor*: distinguished; complete preemption requires congressional replacement of the state claim with an exclusive federal cause of action.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to vacate the federal merits judgment and return the action to state court.  
Effect: Regions Bank may present its federal claim-preclusion defense in state court. No federal ruling remains concerning whether the prior judgment actually bars the landowners’ claims.

---

### National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co.

Argued October 6, 1997 | Decided February 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: competing banks permitted to sue and the agency’s multiple-group interpretation of the Federal Credit Union Act rejected, 90 F.3d 525 (D.C. Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522479/usrep522479.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; statutory standing; statutory interpretation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether competing banks fall within the zone of interests of the Federal Credit Union Act and whether the Act permits a federal credit union to serve multiple unrelated occupational groups.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s statutory conclusion that the common-bond limitation structures the customer market federal occupational credit unions may serve and does not permit aggregation of unrelated occupational groups.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Competitor standing and the statutory zone of interests

Holding and operative rule: Competing banks may seek Administrative Procedure Act review of an agency interpretation that expands federal-credit-union membership beyond the Act’s occupational common-bond limitation.

Reasoning: The banks alleged concrete loss of customers and business resulting from agency approvals that enlarged a competing credit union’s eligible membership. Vacating the approvals would redress that injury. The statutory zone inquiry does not require the plaintiff to be Congress’s primary beneficiary. It asks whether the asserted interest is at least arguably related to the provision whose violation is alleged. The common-bond provision defines the membership market available to a federally chartered occupational credit union. The banks’ alleged injury is the direct competitive consequence of the agency’s expansion of that market, not a remote objection to general administration. The holding does not make every competitor challenge cognizable. The challenged provision must structure the competitive field, and the asserted injury must follow from the precise agency action alleged to exceed it. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522479/usrep522479.pdf))

##### Question 2: Meaning of the occupational common bond

Holding and operative rule: The Federal Credit Union Act does not permit one occupational credit union to aggregate multiple unrelated employee groups whose only connection is membership in the same credit union.

Reasoning: The statute links membership to a common occupational bond. Under the NCUA’s interpretation, separately employed groups with no occupational relationship could be added indefinitely, and membership in the credit union itself would become the only shared characteristic. That construction makes the enacted common-bond limit perform no work. Administrative history and policy judgments concerning efficiency, diversification, or access cannot supply a connection Congress required but the proposed membership groups lack. The provision permits a credit union organized around one occupational group and leaves associational and geographic charters to their separately enacted terms. Congress remains free to broaden the statute, but the agency may not eliminate the existing boundary through interpretation. The approvals adding unrelated occupational groups therefore exceeded the NCUA’s statutory authority. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522479/usrep522479.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer: The common-bond provision protects the internal cohesion and administration of credit unions rather than the competitive interests of commercial banks. The dissent would find the banks outside the statutory zone and, alternatively, would sustain the agency’s longstanding interpretation of an ambiguous membership provision.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Association of Data Processing Service Organizations, Inc. v. Camp* and *Clarke v. Securities Industry Assn.*: applied; a competitor may challenge an agency action when the provision at issue structures the market and the asserted injury directly follows from its violation.  
*Bennett v. Spear*: applied; the zone-of-interests inquiry turns on the particular statutory provision invoked rather than a generalized assessment of the statute’s overall purposes.  
*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*: applied; deference does not permit an agency interpretation that eliminates an operative textual limitation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and the challenged membership approvals shall be set aside.  
Effect: The NCUA must administer occupational charters under the statutory common-bond limitation. The decision does not restrict associational or geographic credit unions governed by different language.

---

### Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government

Argued December 10, 1997 | Decided February 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: former reservation lands transferred under the Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act held to remain Indian country as a dependent Indian community, 101 F.3d 1286 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522520/usrep522520.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal Indian law; Indian country; ANCSA lands

Questions Presented:
1. Whether lands transferred to Native corporations under the Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act constitute a dependent Indian community and therefore Indian country.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that dependent-community status requires both a federal set-aside and federal superintendence.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Dependent Indian community status

Holding and operative rule: A dependent Indian community under 18 U.S.C. § 1151(b) requires land set aside by the Federal Government for Indian use and maintained under federal superintendence. The ANCSA fee lands at issue satisfy neither requirement.

Reasoning: ANCSA revoked the former Venetie reservation and transferred the lands in fee to Native corporations without the ordinary restraints on alienation associated with reservation or trust property. The settlement’s structure sought to replace continuing territorial supervision with corporate ownership and economic autonomy. Native ownership and occupancy do not themselves establish a federal set-aside, and the receipt of federal programs or services available to Native communities does not amount to federal superintendence over the territory. The Village therefore cannot derive territorial taxing authority from the proposition that these fee lands are Indian country as a dependent Indian community. The judgment concerns only that statutory theory. It does not question the Village’s tribal identity, affect trust parcels or other lands carrying a different status, or resolve authority arising from treaty, contract, federal statute, ownership, or another independent source. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522520/usrep522520.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Sandoval*: clarified; dependent-community status requires both a federal set-aside for Indian use and federal superintendence over the land.  
*Hagen v. Utah*: applied; congressional restructuring of territorial status is determined principally from enacted text and statutory design.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and judgment shall be entered that the ANCSA fee lands at issue are not Indian country under the dependent-community provision.  
Effect: The Village may not impose the challenged territorial tax on the premise that the construction site lies within a dependent Indian community. Other tribal powers, contractual rights, and parcel-specific land statuses remain unresolved.

---

### Shahar v. Bowers

Argued November 10, 1997 | Decided March 2, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment upholding rescission of a staff-attorney offer affirmed en banc, 114 F.3d 1097 (11th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/114/1097/493714/))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; public employment; sexual-orientation classification

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Georgia Attorney General violated equal protection and associational liberty by rescinding a staff-attorney offer because the applicant participated in a same-sex religious marriage ceremony.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s conclusion that an employment decision expressly triggered by a same-sex commitment ceremony uses a direct proxy for sexual orientation and must satisfy intermediate scrutiny.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Equal protection and the employment decision

Holding and operative rule: A public employer’s adverse action expressly triggered by an applicant’s same-sex commitment ceremony classifies through a proxy for sexual orientation and receives intermediate scrutiny. This record does not support summary judgment for the employer under that standard.

Reasoning: Bowers extended a staff-attorney offer and withdrew it after learning of Shahar’s planned same-sex religious commitment ceremony. The stated basis permits the inference that the ceremony, and therefore sexual orientation, caused the decision. Protecting client confidences, securing faithful representation, and avoiding actual conflicts within the Attorney General’s office are important governmental objectives. The record, however, identifies no refusal to represent the State, disclosure of confidential information, conflict with assigned duties, professional misconduct, or other individualized evidence connecting Shahar’s private commitment to those interests. Anticipated public hostility, presumed disloyalty, and identity-to-conduct stereotypes cannot establish the substantial relationship intermediate scrutiny requires. The judgment is reversed only because summary judgment was entered for Bowers. Liability, causation, qualified immunity, damages, and the appropriate employment remedy remain for proceedings on a developed record. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/114/1097/493714/))

##### Question 2: Associational and religion claims

Not reached; the equal-protection ruling requires further proceedings, and the separate intimate-association, expressive-association, and Free Exercise theories are unnecessary to the judgment.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas: The Attorney General’s staff attorneys occupy positions of confidence and representation, and the office could reasonably consider whether a publicly described marriage ceremony contrary to state law would create operational conflict or public attribution. The dissent would not treat the ceremony as a conclusive proxy classification and would affirm the employer’s judgment on this record.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Walmer v. Department of Defense*: extended; intermediate scrutiny for sexual-orientation classifications applies to civilian public employment.  
*Thomasson v. Perry* and *Richenberg v. Cohen*: extended; a proxy classification and identity-based stereotype cannot substitute for individualized evidence of conduct, intent, or job-related conflict.  
*Romer v. Evans*: applied; anticipated hostility and disapproval of an applicant’s status do not establish a constitutionally sufficient employment rationale.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the equal-protection claim under intermediate scrutiny.  
Effect: Shahar is not awarded the position or damages by this judgment. The lower courts must resolve liability, causation, qualified immunity, and remedy, while the separate association and religion claims remain open.

---

### Spencer v. Kemna

Argued November 12, 1997 | Decided March 3, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: habeas challenge to an expired parole revocation dismissed as moot, 91 F.3d 1114 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523001/usrep523001.pdf))  
Area of Law: Article III; habeas corpus; mootness

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a challenge to parole revocation remains a live Article III controversy after the petitioner completes the entire sentence and shows no concrete continuing consequence.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s refusal to presume continuing legal consequences from every completed parole revocation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Mootness after expiration of the sentence

Holding and operative rule: A habeas challenge to parole revocation becomes moot after the petitioner completes the entire sentence when no concrete, nonconjectural continuing legal consequence of the revocation is shown.

Reasoning: A criminal conviction carries established legal disabilities that justify a presumption of collateral consequences after release. A completed parole revocation does not automatically produce comparable consequences. Spencer’s asserted injuries depended on future events: commission of another offense, commencement of a later proceeding, discretionary reliance by a sentencing or parole authority, or use of the revocation for impeachment. None was presently operative or legally certain. Article III requires a continuing personal injury that a favorable judgment would redress, not a possibility that the adjudication may become relevant after new misconduct and independent official decisions. The capable-of-repetition exception also fails because the record establishes no reasonable expectation that Spencer personally will again be subjected to the same parole-revocation process. The Court therefore expresses no view on the validity of the revocation itself. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523001/usrep523001.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter, joined by O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer: They agree that the habeas petition is moot but question whether *Heck v. Humphrey* should bar a damages action when a former prisoner can no longer obtain habeas relief.

Ginsburg: Completion of the sentence ends this habeas controversy but does not itself determine whether an otherwise valid civil damages action remains available.

#### Dissents

Stevens: The official revocation finding carries a continuing reputational and legal consequence because it formally adjudges serious misconduct and may be used against Spencer. He would find a live controversy and reach the revocation challenge.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lane v. Williams*: followed; speculative future consequences do not preserve a habeas challenge to an expired parole determination.  
*Sibron v. New York*: declined to extend; the presumption attached to criminal convictions does not apply automatically to parole revocations.  
*Heck v. Humphrey*: distinguished; uncertainty concerning a later damages action does not keep an otherwise moot habeas petition alive.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed, and the habeas petition remains dismissed as moot.  
Effect: No federal court adjudicates the merits of Spencer’s parole revocation in this proceeding. A case involving a present civil disability, supervision consequence, registry obligation, or properly pleaded damages claim would present different questions.

---

### Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach

Argued November 10, 1997 | Decided March 3, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: the multidistrict-litigation transferee court’s self-transfer and resulting trial judgment approved, 102 F.3d 1524 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523026/usrep523026.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal civil procedure; multidistrict litigation; venue

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a multidistrict-litigation transferee court may invoke 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a) to assign a transferred action to itself for trial despite § 1407(a)’s remand command.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, and O’Connor in full; Scalia except Part II-C; Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, and O’Connor; all except Part II-C for Scalia; complete opinion for Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s enforcement of § 1407(a)’s mandatory remand command.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Self-transfer by an MDL transferee court

Holding and operative rule: A multidistrict-litigation transferee court may not retain a transferred action for trial by transferring the case from itself to itself under § 1404(a). Section 1407(a) requires remand when coordinated pretrial proceedings conclude.

Reasoning: Section 1407 authorizes temporary transfer for coordinated or consolidated pretrial proceedings and directs that each action shall be remanded to the district from which it was transferred at or before the conclusion of those proceedings. Section 1404 remains available within its lawful field, but it does not authorize the transferee court to defeat § 1407’s specific return command by assigning the case to the same court for trial. The statutes coexist in sequence: the Panel transfers the action for pretrial management, the transferee court completes that work, and the action returns to the originating district. Any later venue transfer must occur through an authorized procedure after remand. Administrative convenience, party efficiency, and longstanding Panel practice cannot replace the sequence Congress enacted. Lexecon adequately preserved its objection, and the completed trial does not make the statutory violation harmless. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523026/usrep523026.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

28 U.S.C. § 1407(a): clarified; its remand command is mandatory and cannot be avoided through self-transfer under § 1404(a).  
Judicial Panel Rule 14(b): limited; the rule is invalid insofar as it permits an MDL transferee court to assign a transferred action to itself for trial.  
*Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis*: distinguished; finality interests do not cure violation of the specific statutory remand and venue sequence.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the action be returned to the district from which it was transferred.  
Effect: The transferee court’s trial judgment cannot stand. After remand, any further transfer must occur through a lawful procedure available to a court possessing the action in the authorized venue posture.

---

### Bogan v. Scott-Harris

Argued December 3, 1997 | Decided March 3, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: individual-capacity damages judgments against the mayor and city-council vice president affirmed, 134 F.3d 427 (1st Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523044/usrep523044.pdf))  
Area of Law: Civil rights remedies; legislative immunity; municipal government

Questions Presented:
1. Whether local legislators and executive officials are absolutely immune from § 1983 damages for acts that are legislative in form and substance.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s functional classification of the budget proposal, vote, and signature as legislative acts.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Absolute immunity for local legislative acts

Holding and operative rule: Local officials are absolutely immune from § 1983 damages for introducing, voting for, and signing an ordinance that prospectively abolishes a governmental office as part of a budgetary policy decision.

Reasoning: Legislative immunity turns on the function performed rather than the official’s title, level of government, or alleged motive. The budget ordinance made a discretionary allocation of municipal resources, altered the city’s institutional structure, and prospectively eliminated an office through the lawmaking process. Introducing the proposal and voting on it were legislative acts. The mayor’s signature was likewise legislative because it formed an integral part of the ordinance’s enactment rather than an individual personnel order implementing an already established policy. A retaliatory motive allegation does not change the legal character of those acts; otherwise absolute immunity would disappear whenever a plaintiff pleaded an improper reason for legislation. The judgment concerns only damages against the individual officials. Municipal liability, prospective relief, and independently pleaded conduct outside the legislative process remain governed by their own requirements. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523044/usrep523044.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Tenney v. Brandhove*: extended; absolute legislative immunity under § 1983 applies to local legislators performing legislative acts.  
*Lake Country Estates, Inc. v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency*: followed; the function performed, not the governmental level or official title, determines legislative immunity.  
*Supreme Court of Virginia v. Consumers Union of the United States, Inc.*: applied; an executive official receives legislative immunity when performing an integral legislative function.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is reversed, and the individual-capacity damages claims against Bogan and Roderick shall be dismissed on absolute legislative-immunity grounds.  
Effect: The officials incur no personal damages liability for proposing, voting on, or signing the ordinance. Claims against the municipality, claims for prospective relief, and any independently pleaded nonlegislative conduct remain unaffected.

### Kawaauhau v. Geiger

Argued January 21, 1998 | Decided March 3, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: malpractice judgment held dischargeable because 11 U.S.C. § 523(a)(6) requires intentional injury, 113 F.3d 848 (8th Cir. 1997) (en banc). ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523057/usrep523057.pdf))  
Area of Law: Bankruptcy; discharge exceptions; statutory mens rea

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a malpractice debt arising from negligent or reckless medical treatment is a debt for willful and malicious injury under 11 U.S.C. § 523(a)(6).

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that statutory willfulness must attach to the injury rather than merely to the act that produced it.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Willful and malicious injury

Holding and operative rule: A debt arising from negligently or recklessly inflicted injury does not satisfy § 523(a)(6)’s willfulness requirement. The provision requires a deliberate or intentional injury, not merely a deliberate act that happens to cause harm.

Reasoning: The statutory phrase directs willfulness to the injury itself. Reading it to cover every intentional act that leads to injury would collapse the settled distinction among intentional torts, recklessness, and negligence and would make a broad range of ordinary malpractice and accident debts nondischargeable. The surrounding discharge exceptions confirm that Congress specifies separately when particular forms of negligent or reckless conduct are excluded from discharge. The malpractice judgment established deficient treatment and resulting injury, but it did not establish that Geiger desired the injury or acted with the intent § 523(a)(6) requires. The Court therefore does not revisit the malpractice findings or make a new determination of subjective intent. It also does not decide whether knowledge that injury is substantially certain satisfies the statute or define the independent work performed by the word malicious. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523057/usrep523057.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Tinker v. Colwell*: limited; implied malice does not make a debt for negligent or reckless injury nondischargeable under § 523(a)(6).  
*Davis v. Aetna Acceptance Co.*: followed; the discharge exception is directed to intentional torts rather than injuries resulting from merely negligent or reckless conduct.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The malpractice debt remains dischargeable under § 523(a)(6). The judgment does not determine whether another statutory discharge exception applies or how § 523(a)(6) governs injury caused with knowledge of substantial certainty.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Ramirez

Argued January 13, 1998 | Decided March 4, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: suppression order affirmed, 91 F.3d 1297 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523065/usrep523065.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; warrant execution; knock-and-announce

Questions Presented:
1. Whether officers executing a warrant must satisfy a heightened no-knock standard whenever entry causes property damage.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Property damage does not increase the case-specific reasonable-suspicion showing required for a no-knock entry, although the nature and extent of the damage remain part of the Fourth Amendment’s overall reasonableness inquiry. Section 3109 incorporates the traditional exigency exceptions, which were satisfied here.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: No-knock entry accompanied by property damage

Holding and operative rule: Property damage does not trigger a heightened threshold for dispensing with knock-and-announce requirements. A no-knock entry remains governed by case-specific reasonable suspicion, while any destruction occurring during execution is reviewed independently for objective reasonableness under the Fourth Amendment.

Reasoning: *Wilson v. Arkansas* made announcement part of the Fourth Amendment reasonableness inquiry, while *Richards v. Wisconsin* permits an unannounced entry when officers reasonably suspect that announcement would be dangerous, futile, or materially interfere with the investigation. Neither rule makes the entry threshold depend on whether property must be damaged. Here, reliable information placed a violent escaped prisoner at Ramirez’s home, identified his access to firearms, and indicated that weapons were stored in the garage. Breaking one garage window to discourage access to that weapons supply was proportionate to the danger reasonably perceived when the warrant was executed. Section 3109 likewise incorporates the common-law exceptions to the announcement requirement and applies the same exigency standard. Excessive, unnecessary, punitive, or objective-unrelated destruction could present a different constitutional question, but this single act did not make the entry unreasonable. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523065/usrep523065.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Wilson v. Arkansas*: clarified; the manner of warrant execution remains subject to overall reasonableness, but property damage does not alter the threshold for dispensing with announcement.  
*Richards v. Wisconsin*: followed; case-specific reasonable suspicion governs no-knock entry whether or not property damage accompanies entry.  
*Miller v. United States* and *Sabbath v. United States*: clarified; 18 U.S.C. § 3109 incorporates the traditional common-law exigency exceptions to prior announcement.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for further proceedings under the holding that the entry and manner of execution complied with both § 3109 and the Fourth Amendment.  
Effect: The suppression judgment cannot stand on the asserted heightened-destruction standard. The Court does not decide the remedy for materially different or independently unreasonable destruction of property.
