### Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc.

Argued December 3, 1997 | Decided March 4, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment against the same-sex harassment claim affirmed, 83 F.3d 118 (5th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523075/usrep523075.pdf))  
Area of Law: Title VII; hostile work environment; same-sex harassment

Questions Presented:
1. Whether same-sex workplace harassment can constitute discrimination because of sex under Title VII.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s rejection of the categorical same-sex bar and its application of the ordinary Title VII hostile-environment requirements.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Same-sex harassment under Title VII

Holding and operative rule: Title VII does not categorically exclude harassment because the alleged harasser and employee are of the same sex. The employee must still establish that sex caused the challenged treatment, that the environment was objectively and subjectively severe or pervasive, and that the conduct is attributable to the employer under the governing liability rules.

Reasoning: Title VII prohibits discrimination because of sex and contains no opposite-sex pairing requirement. The sex of the alleged harasser therefore cannot end the case at the threshold. Evidence of sexual desire, sex-specific hostility, or materially different treatment of men and women may support causation, but none is a mandatory evidentiary route or a substitute for proof. Sexual language, physical contact, workplace cruelty, or the harasser’s orientation does not alone establish discrimination because of sex. The existing *Harris* standard continues to require an objectively and subjectively abusive environment assessed through all the circumstances, including severity, frequency, threat, humiliation, and workplace context. Because the Fifth Circuit disposed of Oncale’s claim solely through a categorical same-sex rule, the ordinary causation, hostile-environment, employer-attribution, defense, and remedy questions remain for the lower courts. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523075/usrep523075.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Thomas: He joins because the Court preserves the plaintiff’s obligation in every harassment case to prove discrimination because of sex rather than merely offensive or sexualized conduct.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc.*: followed; the same objective and subjective severe-or-pervasive standard governs same-sex and opposite-sex hostile-environment claims.  
*Meritor Savings Bank v. Vinson*: clarified; actionable sex-based harassment is not limited by the sex of the alleged harasser.  
Title VII’s because-of-sex requirement: reaffirmed; sexual content or workplace abuse is insufficient without proof of discriminatory causation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for application of the ordinary Title VII hostile-environment and employer-liability rules.  
Effect: The categorical same-sex bar is removed. The Court finds neither the allegations true nor Sundowner liable, and it leaves causation, severity or pervasiveness, employer attribution, defenses, and relief unresolved.

---

### Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment

Argued October 6, 1997 | Decided March 4, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: EPCRA citizen suit sustained despite pre-suit compliance, 90 F.3d 1237 (7th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523083/usrep523083.pdf))  
Area of Law: Article III; standing; jurisdictional sequencing

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a federal court may assume Article III jurisdiction to decide an easier merits question.
2. Whether a plaintiff seeking civil penalties payable to the Treasury and retrospective declarations has standing after the defendant cured the reporting violation before suit.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas in full; Breyer as to Parts I and IV | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas; Parts I and IV for Breyer |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | Kennedy | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete separate writing |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Concurs in the judgment | Parts I, III, and IV for Souter; Part III for Ginsburg |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand with instructions to dismiss.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s rejection of assumed Article III jurisdiction and its conclusion that none of the requested relief would redress the completed informational injury.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Assumed Article III jurisdiction

Holding and operative rule: A federal court may not assume Article III jurisdiction merely because the merits appear easier or would produce the same prevailing party. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: Article III defines the federal judicial power rather than prescribing a waivable order of convenience. A court lacking constitutional authority cannot issue an otherwise correct interpretation of federal law, because the merits pronouncement itself would be beyond the judicial power. Prior decisions that avoided difficult jurisdictional questions in exceptional circumstances do not establish a general doctrine of hypothetical jurisdiction, and questions concerning whether a statute creates a cause of action do not become jurisdictional merely because they are antecedent to relief. The Court therefore must resolve the contested Article III issue before construing EPCRA’s citizen-suit provision. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523083/usrep523083.pdf))

##### Question 2: Redressability after completed compliance

Holding and operative rule: After the defendant filed every overdue report before suit, Citizens for a Better Environment lacked standing because Treasury penalties, retrospective declarations, inspection authority, and litigation expenses would not repair its past informational injury or prevent a demonstrated continuing or imminent injury. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Redressability requires a likely remedy for the plaintiff’s own injury. The requested civil penalties would be paid to the United States rather than to Citizens for a Better Environment. A declaration that Steel Company violated the statute in the past would not supply the missing information sooner or otherwise undo the delay. Inspection and prospective reporting orders were unsupported because the complaint identified no continuing nondisclosure or credible likelihood of recurrence. Litigation expenses are ancillary to a valid action and cannot create standing when the underlying relief is nonredressive. The rule leaves open suits involving continuing nondisclosure, a concrete threat of renewed violation, compensatory relief, a plaintiff-directed statutory bounty, or another measure that would materially remedy the claimant’s injury. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523083/usrep523083.pdf))

#### Concurrences

O’Connor, joined by Kennedy: The ordinary course requires jurisdiction before merits, and Citizens for a Better Environment lacks standing. She would not read the Court’s opinion to disapprove every exceptional precedent in which a jurisdictional issue was reserved.

Breyer: He agrees that Citizens for a Better Environment lacks standing and that jurisdiction ordinarily comes first. He rejects an absolute constitutional order of operations and would preserve narrow authority to reserve an unusually difficult jurisdictional issue when an easy merits ruling necessarily produces the same result.

Stevens, joined in relevant part by Souter and Ginsburg: The Court may determine first whether EPCRA creates a cause of action for wholly past violations. He would resolve the case through the statute and conclude that no such citizen suit exists after complete pre-suit compliance.

Ginsburg: She agrees that the complaint must be dismissed and emphasizes the statutory limitation on citizen suits for wholly past reporting violations.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Court-of-appeals hypothetical-jurisdiction doctrine: overruled; federal courts may not adjudicate ordinary merits questions by assuming disputed Article III authority.  
*Norton v. Mathews* and *Secretary of the Navy v. Avrech*: limited; their exceptional dispositions do not establish general authority to bypass Article III.  
*Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife*: followed; injury, causation, and redressability remain independent constitutional requirements.  
*City of Los Angeles v. Lyons*: applied; completed past injury without continuing adverse effects does not support prospective relief.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions that the complaint be dismissed for lack of Article III standing.  
Effect: No civil penalty, retrospective declaration, inspection order, or litigation-expense award may issue in this action. The Court does not decide whether EPCRA authorizes citizen suits for wholly past violations when a plaintiff otherwise possesses standing.

---

### Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L’anza Research International, Inc.

Argued December 8, 1997 | Decided March 9, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: § 602(a) importation liability sustained notwithstanding the first-sale rule, 98 F.3d 1109 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523135/usrep523135.pdf))  
Area of Law: Copyright; first sale; importation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Copyright Act’s first-sale rule applies to copies lawfully made in the United States, sold abroad, and later reimported without the copyright owner’s permission.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and direct judgment for Quality King on the copyright-importation theory.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s integrated reading of §§ 106(3), 109(a), and 602(a).

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: First sale and reimported domestic copies

Holding and operative rule: Section 602(a) does not revive an exhausted § 106(3) distribution right when a copy made lawfully in the United States is sold abroad with the copyright owner’s authorization and later reimported by its lawful owner.

Reasoning: Section 602(a) treats unauthorized importation as infringement of the distribution right granted under § 106. Section 106 expressly makes that right subject to §§ 107 through 120, including § 109(a), which permits the lawful owner of a copy to dispose of it without further permission from the copyright holder. Once L’anza authorized the initial foreign sale of these domestically manufactured copies, the distribution right in those particular copies was exhausted. Their later return across the border did not recreate that right. The statutory exceptions to § 602(a), enforcement provisions concerning piratical copies, and policies favoring territorial price discrimination cannot displace the enacted relationship among the three provisions. The decision is limited to the round-trip copies before the Court and does not determine the treatment of copies manufactured abroad. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523135/usrep523135.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Ginsburg: She joins on the understanding that the judgment concerns copies made in the United States, sold abroad, and returned. The treatment of copies manufactured abroad remains unresolved.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bobbs-Merrill Co. v. Straus*: followed; an authorized first sale exhausts the copyright owner’s distribution control over the particular lawful copy.  
17 U.S.C. §§ 106(3), 109(a), and 602(a): clarified; § 602(a) does not enlarge the distribution right beyond the limitations incorporated through § 106.  
*K mart Corp. v. Cartier*: distinguished; foreign-manufactured gray-market goods remain outside this round-trip holding.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and judgment shall be entered for Quality King on the claim that reimportation infringed the § 106(3) distribution right through § 602(a).  
Effect: L’anza may not use copyright’s distribution right to prevent resale of these domestically made copies after its authorized first sale abroad. Any preserved contract, trademark, labeling, or consumer-protection claim remains governed by its own law.

### Gray v. Maryland

Argued December 8, 1997 | Decided March 9, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Appeals of Maryland; judgment below: conviction affirmed, 344 Md. 417, 687 A.2d 660 (1997). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523185/usrep523185.pdf))  
Area of Law: Confrontation Clause; joint trials; codefendant confessions

Questions Presented:
1. Whether admitting a nontestifying codefendant’s confession with the defendant’s name replaced by an obvious blank or the word deleted violates the Confrontation Clause under *Bruton*.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s functional distinction between a facially incriminating deletion and a confession that becomes incriminating only through later evidentiary linkage.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Facially incriminating redacted confession

Holding and operative rule: A nontestifying codefendant’s confession remains within *Bruton* when a conspicuous blank, deletion, symbol, or equivalent substitution directly identifies the defendant from the statement and its immediate context. A limiting instruction does not cure the resulting confrontation violation.

Reasoning: *Richardson v. Marsh* permits a properly instructed jury to hear a confession that eliminates the defendant’s name and every reference to the defendant’s existence, becoming incriminating only when linked to later evidence. The redaction here did something materially different. Its grammar, obvious blanks, limited cast of participants, and the prosecutor’s immediate linkage told the jury that a specific name had been removed and that the omitted person was Gray. The confession therefore functioned like the unredacted accusation in *Bruton*, and the conspicuous deletion could draw additional attention to the accusation rather than neutralize it. Joint trials and redacted confessions remain permissible when neutral substitute language does not facially identify the defendant. Whether this error was harmless under the governing standard remains for the Maryland courts. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523185/usrep523185.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: The confession did not identify Gray without inferential connection to other trial evidence, so *Richardson* and the limiting instruction should control. The Court’s rule will force alteration of confessions, severance, or exclusion even when the jury could follow an ordinary limiting direction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bruton v. United States*: followed and clarified; an obvious deletion that facially identifies the defendant is treated like an express accusation by the nontestifying codefendant.  
*Richardson v. Marsh*: distinguished; its protection is limited to confessions neutral on their face and incriminating only through later evidentiary linkage.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals of Maryland is vacated, and the case is remanded for harmless-error analysis and any further proceedings consistent with the confrontation holding.  
Effect: Maryland may not rely on the limiting instruction alone to validate this conspicuously redacted confession. The Court does not prohibit joint trials, genuinely neutral redactions, independently admissible confessions, or testimony subjected to cross-examination.

### Aktepe v. United States

Argued November 12, 1997 | Decided March 10, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the United States affirmed on political-question grounds, 105 F.3d 1400 (11th Cir. 1997). ([Eleventh Circuit Court of Appeals](https://media.ca11.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/files/19962167.OPA.pdf))  
Area of Law: Political question; military operations; maritime torts

Questions Presented:
1. Whether negligence and wrongful-death claims arising from accidental live missile fire during a NATO training exercise present a nonjusticiable political question, including whether judicial review of allegedly negligent communications, training, weapons procedures, and lower-level operational conduct necessarily intrudes upon military and foreign-affairs judgments constitutionally committed to the political branches.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s rejection of a categorical military-context bar and its requirement that political-question analysis be performed claim by claim.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Justiciability of military-training accident claims

Holding and operative rule: A military-training accident is not categorically nonjusticiable. The political-question doctrine bars a particular claim only when adjudication actually requires a court to select constitutionally committed military policy or when no judicially manageable legal standard governs the alleged conduct.

Reasoning: The missile strike occurred during a live multinational naval exercise and unquestionably implicates military and foreign-affairs interests. Those circumstances require care, but they do not themselves remove every retrospective legal claim from Article III adjudication. *Gilligan v. Morgan* rejected continuing judicial supervision over military training and discipline; it did not create immunity from every claim arising from completed military activity. Allegations that would require a court to redesign command structures, weapons doctrine, multinational rules of engagement, or the substance of training policy may be nonjusticiable. Other allegations, such as failure to follow an already prescribed safety procedure, communicate a known weapons status, or perform a defined operational task, may be resolvable through ordinary legal standards. The Eleventh Circuit treated the military setting as dispositive without separating those categories. A claim-specific remand is required, with every statutory, immunity, negligence, causation, and damages issue left open. ([Eleventh Circuit Court of Appeals](https://media.ca11.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/files/19962167.OPA.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Every pleaded theory would require a civilian court to define reasonable command, communications, training, and weapons procedures during a live NATO exercise. Because no judicial source supplies standards for those interdependent operational judgments, the dismissal should be affirmed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Baker v. Carr*: applied; political-question analysis turns on actual textual commitment and the availability of judicially manageable standards, not the subject-matter label alone.  
*Gilligan v. Morgan*: clarified; its rejection of continuing judicial supervision of military training does not categorically foreclose every retrospective claim arising from a completed military accident.  
*Japan Whaling Assn. v. American Cetacean Society*: applied; serious foreign-affairs implications do not alone make a legal claim nonjusticiable.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for separate analysis of each claim under the political-question principles stated here.  
Effect: No finding of negligence or liability is entered. Statutory coverage, sovereign immunity, the *Feres* doctrine, causation, damages, and the justiciability of any specifically identified high-level policy challenge remain unresolved.

---

### W.P. v. Verniero

Argued December 2, 1997 | Decided March 16, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: notification scheme held civil and higher-tier notification conditioned on clear-and-convincing pre-notification process, 119 F.3d 1077 (3d Cir. 1997), under the lower-court caption *E.B. v. Verniero*. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/119/1077/486597/))  
Area of Law: Due process; Ex Post Facto Clause; community notification

Questions Presented:
1. Whether community notification based on a convicted person’s predicted risk of future sexual offenses constitutes punishment for purposes of the Ex Post Facto and Double Jeopardy Clauses when applied to convictions predating the notification law.
2. Whether Due Process requires a hearing before community notification at which the State bears the burden of proving the assigned risk classification and proposed scope of notification by clear and convincing evidence.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Civil classification of the notification scheme | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The Ex Post Facto and Double Jeopardy challenges remain rejected. |
| Pre-notification procedures for Tier Two and Tier Three | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Higher-tier notification remains conditioned on the procedures and proof burden stated below. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer; civil-classification holding for O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm both judgment components.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s civil classification of the scheme and its conclusion that broad individualized notification requires predeprivation procedures and clear-and-convincing proof.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Civil or punitive character of community notification

Holding and operative rule: New Jersey’s notification scheme is civil in primary purpose and structure and therefore does not impose a second criminal punishment on persons whose convictions preceded the law. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The enacted system seeks to protect the public through registration, individualized risk assessment, and notification calibrated to the predicted danger. It does not impose confinement, extend the criminal sentence, or automatically attach the broadest disclosure to every conviction. Deterrence, stigma, and serious practical consequences are relevant, but they do not by themselves supply the clearest proof needed to override the legislature’s civil design. The scheme is materially different from a nominally civil measure whose actual operation becomes imprisonment or another functional criminal sanction. The Ex Post Facto and Double Jeopardy challenges therefore fail on this record, without foreclosing a different result for a materially different notification system whose design or actual operation is affirmatively punitive. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/119/1077/486597/))

##### Question 2: Process and proof before higher-tier notification

Holding and operative rule: Before Tier Two or Tier Three notification, the State must provide timely notice, disclose the proposed factual basis and notification scope, permit a meaningful opportunity to contest the classification before a neutral decisionmaker, issue written reasons, and prove the material facts and risk classification by clear and convincing evidence.

Reasoning: Higher-tier notification turns on individualized and predictive factual judgments rather than the conviction alone. An erroneous overclassification immediately publicizes the conviction, officially designates the registrant as a danger, and can produce serious and difficult-to-reverse consequences for safety, housing, employment, and social status. The State controls much of the evidence, proposes the risk classification, and can present its case before disclosure without disabling the regulatory program. Because the individual’s loss from erroneous overclassification is substantially graver and less reversible than the State’s temporary loss from an erroneous underclassification, a preponderance standard allocates the risk of error too evenly. Clear-and-convincing proof, combined with notice and a focused adversarial hearing, materially reduces that risk while preserving confidential registration and immediate law-enforcement access. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/119/1077/486597/))

#### Concurrences

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: The scheme is civil, and the State may provide notice and an opportunity to correct demonstrable factual mistakes. These Justices would not constitutionalize the clear-and-convincing burden or the Court’s complete set of procedures.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: A preponderance standard before a neutral decisionmaker adequately balances predictive uncertainty, public protection, and the registrant’s interests. The analogy to civil commitment and termination-like family orders does not justify the heightened proof burden for a noncustodial notification decision.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Hudson v. United States*: applied; a nominally civil regulatory measure remains civil absent the clearest proof of punitive design or operation.  
*Kansas v. Hendricks*: distinguished; the notification scheme does not impose prisonlike confinement or otherwise defeat its asserted civil structure through actual operation.  
*Mathews v. Eldridge*: applied; the process required depends on the affected interest, risk of error, value of additional safeguards, and governmental burden.  
*Addington v. Texas*, *Santosky v. Kramer*, and *A. St. P. C. v. B. C.*: extended; clear-and-convincing proof may be required where an individualized predictive judgment threatens grave and difficult-to-reverse consequences without imposing physical confinement.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed, and Tier Two or Tier Three notification may proceed only after the State provides the procedures and carries the clear-and-convincing burden stated in this opinion.  
Effect: New Jersey may continue confidential registration, law-enforcement monitoring, and notification supported by a valid higher-tier determination. The judgment does not govern every public registry, lifetime classification without review, confidential registration alone, or a materially different scheme operating as punishment.

---

### United States v. Robinson

Adjudication paused. Standing State grants review only on whether the Hobbs Act may constitutionally reach a local robbery based on its effect on an individual interstate business. The approved brief states a materially broader grant that also includes the vulnerable-victim sentencing enhancement, and Stone’s controlled disposition affirms the convictions while vacating the sentence on that added issue. Because the actual grant and the approved controlled judgment cannot operate together, no judgment, vote, opinion, holding, or remedial disposition is rendered for this matter. `STANDING_STATE.md` `OT_1997CHUNK4.md`

### Almendarez-Torres v. United States

Argued October 14, 1997 | Decided March 24, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: enhanced illegal-reentry sentence affirmed, 113 F.3d 515 (5th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523224/usrep523224.pdf))  
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; statutory elements; recidivism

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the prior aggravated-felony conviction specified in 8 U.S.C. § 1326(b)(2) is a sentencing factor or an element that must be charged and proved to a jury.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s narrow classification of § 1326(b)(2) as a recidivist penalty provision as applied to admitted, valid prior judgments of conviction.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Prior conviction as a sentencing factor

Holding and operative rule: Section 1326(b)(2) is a recidivist penalty provision rather than a separate offense. Almendarez-Torres’s admitted and valid prior aggravated-felony convictions could therefore support the higher statutory maximum without separate indictment allegations or jury findings.

Reasoning: Subsection (a) defines the unlawful-reentry offense, while subsection (b) prescribes increased punishment for an offender whose deportation followed specified convictions. That arrangement accords with the longstanding treatment of recidivism as a sentencing consideration and avoids placing prejudicial evidence of earlier crimes before the jury deciding the present offense. The Constitution does not require every increase in a statutory maximum to be treated as a separate offense element regardless of statutory structure, particularly where the relevant facts are prior judgments produced through proceedings carrying full constitutional safeguards. Almendarez-Torres admitted the convictions at his plea and sentencing, leaving no dispute about identity, validity, or the qualifying character of the judgments. The decision does not authorize judicial findings about contested underlying conduct or resolve the procedure for a genuinely disputed predicate. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523224/usrep523224.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The parallel text and substantial increase from a two-year to a twenty-year maximum indicate that subsection (b)(2) defines a greater offense. The prior conviction therefore had to be alleged in the indictment despite Almendarez-Torres’s later admission.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Custis v. United States*: followed; sentencing courts may identify valid outstanding prior convictions, subject to the established complete-denial-of-counsel boundary.  
*McMillan v. Pennsylvania*: applied and clarified; legislative classification of traditional recidivism as a sentencing factor does not itself violate the jury guarantee.  
*United States v. Gaudin*: distinguished; its rule governing facts already classified as offense elements does not determine whether admitted recidivism is an element or a penalty factor.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Almendarez-Torres’s sentence based on his admitted, valid prior aggravated-felony convictions remains in force. Disputed identity, an invalid or uncounseled predicate, underlying conduct, nonrecidivist maximum-increasing facts, and the burden governing a genuinely contested qualifying conviction remain unresolved.

---

### Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard

Argued December 10, 1997 | Decided March 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: self-incrimination claim rejected but limited due-process challenge permitted to proceed, 107 F.3d 1178 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523272/usrep523272.pdf))  
Area of Law: Capital clemency; due process; self-incrimination

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Ohio’s voluntary clemency interview compels self-incrimination.
2. Whether the Due Process Clause creates procedural requirements for Ohio’s clemency hearing and interview process.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion for O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; self-incrimination holding and judgment for Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete separate writing |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: A voluntary interview does not compel testimony merely because silence may reduce the prisoner’s opportunity to persuade. Clemency remains executive grace rather than a trial-type adjudication, although an established clemency channel may not be administered through wholly arbitrary exclusion or sham access.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Compulsion at the voluntary interview

Holding and operative rule: Ohio’s optional clemency interview does not compel self-incrimination merely because the prisoner must choose between remaining silent and presenting information that may improve the clemency request. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Woodard could decline the interview, and Ohio threatened no new punishment, prosecution, or automatic adverse legal consequence for silence. The strategic pressure to answer arose from the possibility that speaking might help his request for mercy, just as other lawful procedural choices may require a defendant to weigh the advantages and risks of presenting evidence. Even assuming the Authority could draw an adverse inference from silence in this noncriminal proceeding, the interview remained voluntary within the meaning of the Fifth Amendment. The Court does not permit compelled statements to be used in a later criminal prosecution or decide a procedure carrying a separate legal penalty for invocation of the privilege. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523272/usrep523272.pdf))

##### Question 2: Minimal constitutional limits on clemency access

Holding and operative rule: The Due Process Clause does not constitutionalize clemency procedures or require trial-type safeguards, but it forbids wholly arbitrary state action that effectively denies access to the clemency process the State has chosen to provide. Ohio’s procedures satisfy that narrow constitutional floor on this record. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Ohio law leaves the ultimate clemency decision to executive discretion and creates no substantive entitlement to commutation. That absence of entitlement forecloses a constitutional code governing notice periods, discovery, counsel participation, evidentiary presentation, written merits findings, or judicial review of the Governor’s reasons. Executive discretion is not, however, authority to administer the announced avenue through a coin flip, bribery, personal animus unrelated to the application, deliberate exclusion of a particular prisoner, or comparable sham access. Ohio offered Woodard notice, a hearing, and an optional interview under its established procedures. His disagreement with the amount of preparation time and the permitted role of counsel does not establish wholly arbitrary exclusion. No new clemency proceeding is required. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523272/usrep523272.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: These Justices agree that the interview is voluntary and that the Sixth Circuit’s remand must be reversed. They would not recognize a freestanding due-process floor for discretionary clemency, while leaving separately applicable constitutional prohibitions intact.

Stevens: He agrees that Ohio’s voluntary interview does not compel self-incrimination and that only minimal process could be required in clemency. He would preserve the Sixth Circuit’s remand so Woodard’s allegations of arbitrary administration could be tested rather than resolved against him at this stage.

#### Dissents

Stevens: Because a condemned person retains a protected interest in life and Ohio has made clemency part of the process preceding execution, the State’s procedures are not entirely beyond due-process review. He would allow the limited challenge to proceed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Connecticut Board of Pardons v. Dumschat*: followed and clarified; discretionary clemency creates no substantive entitlement, but the absence of entitlement does not authorize wholly arbitrary denial of access to an established process.  
*Baxter v. Palmigiano*: applied; the possibility of a permissible adverse inference in a noncriminal proceeding does not make an optional interview compelled.  
*Evitts v. Lucey*: declined to extend; executive clemency is not an appellate adjudication requiring the procedural protections associated with judicial review.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed, and judgment shall be entered for the Ohio officials on the due-process and self-incrimination claims presented.  
Effect: Ohio need not provide Woodard a new clemency hearing or interview, expand counsel’s role, permit discovery, or submit the Governor’s clemency judgment to judicial review. A materially different claim involving wholly arbitrary exclusion, sham access, or an independently unconstitutional criterion remains open.

**Source control:** The packet’s lower-court citation for *Feltner* is corrected to 106 F.3d 284 (9th Cir. 1997). Its historical opinion structure is also corrected because Justice Scalia concurred in the judgment rather than joining Justice Thomas’s opinion. *Campbell* reached the Court on certiorari to the Louisiana Court of Appeal, Third Circuit, although the Louisiana Supreme Court had supplied the intervening standing ruling. These corrections do not affect Stone’s controlled positions. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523340/usrep523340.pdf))

The verified record in *Bird* supplies a September 14, 1995 sentence of one year’s imprisonment followed by one year of supervised release, but it does not establish when the custodial and supervision terms began, whether execution was stayed, or whether the challenged restriction produced a concrete continuing consequence on April 13, 1998. Because Stone’s controlled merits reach and remedy for the supervised-release issue expressly depend on a live controversy, adjudication is paused only as to that issue. The separable FACE conviction is adjudicated. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/us-5th-circuit/1233765.html))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Scheffer

Argued November 3, 1997 | Decided March 31, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces; judgment below: conviction set aside because Military Rule of Evidence 707 was held unconstitutional as applied, 44 M.J. 442 (C.A.A.F. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523303/usrep523303.pdf))  
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; military evidence; right to present a defense

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Military Rule of Evidence 707’s categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence unconstitutionally abridges an accused’s right to present a defense.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court and additional opinion | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion for Stone, Scalia, and Souter; Parts I, II-A, and II-D for O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court and Justice Thomas’s additional Parts II-B and II-C in full.  
Announced ground: Rule 707 is a neutral exclusion supported by a substantial dispute over polygraph reliability. Scheffer retained meaningful means to deny knowing drug use and challenge the Government’s evidence, while the excluded material added an examiner’s mediated credibility assessment.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence

Holding and operative rule: Military Rule of Evidence 707 does not violate the constitutional right to present a defense. A neutral evidentiary rule may categorically exclude a class of disputed scientific evidence when the exclusion is reasonably related to legitimate reliability concerns and does not substantially disable presentation of the accused’s underlying factual defense.

Reasoning: The right to present relevant evidence remains subject to reasonable evidentiary restrictions. A rule becomes unconstitutional when it is arbitrary or disproportionate to its legitimate purposes and substantially impairs a weighty defense interest. Polygraph reliability remained the subject of serious scientific and judicial disagreement, permitting the military rulemaker to exclude the evidence categorically rather than require collateral litigation in every court-martial. Rule 707 did not prevent Scheffer from testifying, denying knowing drug use, describing his work as an informant, challenging the urinalysis, or presenting any underlying historical fact. It excluded an examiner’s opinion that his denial appeared truthful. That limitation did not remove the core of his defense in the manner of the rules invalidated in *Washington v. Texas*, *Chambers v. Mississippi*, or *Rock v. Arkansas*. The Constitution does not require admission merely because a disputed expert conclusion favors the accused. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523303/usrep523303.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The unresolved reliability dispute makes the categorical rule constitutional, although a per se exclusion may be unwise and materially improved technology or a stronger future record could present a different evidentiary question. These Justices do not join the broader proposition that polygraph evidence inherently usurps the factfinder’s credibility function. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523303/usrep523303.pdf))

#### Dissents

Stevens: Scheffer sought to introduce the result of a military-administered examination bearing directly on his claim of innocent ingestion. A categorical rule prevented individualized assessment of relevance, reliability, and prejudice and therefore excluded evidence central to the defense without adequate justification.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rock v. Arkansas*, *Washington v. Texas*, and *Chambers v. Mississippi*: clarified; neutral evidence rules remain valid unless they are arbitrary or disproportionate and materially impair a weighty defense interest.  
*Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc.*: distinguished; its interpretation of the Federal Rules of Evidence does not constitutionalize case-by-case admissibility or prohibit a military categorical exclusion.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces is reversed, and the military judgment may be reinstated subject to any preserved issue not resolved by this decision.  
Effect: Scheffer has no constitutional entitlement to admission of the polygraph result or examiner’s opinion. The decision does not validate every categorical scientific-evidence rule or foreclose a materially different technology and reliability record.

---

### Feltner v. Columbia Pictures Television, Inc.

Argued January 21, 1998 | Decided March 31, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: judge-determined statutory-damages award affirmed, 106 F.3d 284 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523340/usrep523340.pdf))  
Area of Law: Copyright; civil jury trial; statutory damages

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Seventh Amendment requires a jury to determine the amount of statutory damages in a copyright infringement action.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Section 504(c) does not independently confer a jury right, but the Seventh Amendment assigns willfulness and the amount of copyright statutory damages within the lawful range to the jury when a timely demand is made.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Statutory allocation under § 504(c)

Holding and operative rule: Section 504(c) does not itself confer a statutory right to jury determination of statutory damages. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Section 504(c) establishes the availability and permissible range of statutory damages and repeatedly refers to determinations by the court, including findings concerning willfulness and innocent infringement. It contains no express reference to juries. The surrounding remedial provisions use different language for actual damages and profits, while the statutory-damages provision assigns discretion through language ordinarily directed to the tribunal. That construction does not decide the constitutional allocation because Congress may not avoid the Seventh Amendment merely by using the word court. It means only that the statute supplies no independent jury entitlement, requiring the constitutional question to be resolved directly. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523340/usrep523340.pdf))

##### Question 2: Seventh Amendment decisionmaker

Holding and operative rule: On a timely demand, the Seventh Amendment assigns to the jury all factual issues pertinent to copyright statutory damages, including willfulness and selection of the amount within the lawful statutory range. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Copyright infringement seeking monetary relief has a strong historical analogue in actions at law, and juries historically determined damages in copyright and comparable legal actions. Selecting the amount of compensatory or punitive monetary relief within a prescribed range performs a traditional jury function. *Tull v. United States* does not transfer that task to the judge because it concerned a civil penalty payable to the Government, a remedy carrying a different historical and functional character. The judge retains authority to interpret § 504(c), identify the lawful range, determine legal sufficiency, instruct the jury, and review the award through constitutionally permissible procedures. Feltner demanded a jury, but the judge independently found willfulness, counted the pertinent infringements, and fixed the award. Those determinations cannot stand. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523340/usrep523340.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia: Section 504(c) can and should be construed to authorize jury determination of statutory damages, avoiding the constitutional ruling. He concurs in the judgment but does not join the Court’s statutory construction or reach the Seventh Amendment question. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523340/usrep523340.pdf))

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc.*: applied; historical analogue and the function performed determine the Seventh Amendment decisionmaker.  
*Tull v. United States*: distinguished; the amount of a governmental civil penalty does not control private copyright damages with a traditional legal analogue.  
*Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc.*: followed; initial jury determination remains compatible with constitutionally permissible judicial review of the resulting award.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to vacate the judge-selected statutory-damages determinations and conduct further proceedings honoring Feltner’s jury demand.  
Effect: A jury must determine willfulness and the amount of statutory damages within the lawful range. The court retains responsibility for legal interpretation, instructions, sufficiency review, and constitutionally valid postverdict review.

---

### United States v. United States Shoe Corp.

Argued March 4, 1998 | Decided March 31, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below: export-tax refund judgment affirmed, 114 F.3d 1564 (Fed. Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523360/usrep523360.pdf))  
Area of Law: Tax; Export Clause; user fees

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Harbor Maintenance Tax is an unconstitutional tax on exports or a permissible user fee.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: The Court of International Trade possessed statutory jurisdiction, and the ad valorem charge is an export tax because liability follows cargo value rather than any fair measure of services or facilities used.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Court of International Trade jurisdiction

Holding and operative rule: The Court of International Trade has residual jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1581(i) over this Harbor Maintenance Tax refund action.

Reasoning: The claim arises from the administration and enforcement of a federal customs-related revenue law. The Harbor Maintenance Tax applies to imports, exports, and domestic shipments, and Congress directed that it be treated as a customs duty for jurisdictional purposes. No more specific jurisdictional subsection provides the appropriate route for this export refund claim. Section 1581(i)’s residual grant therefore places the dispute within the Court of International Trade rather than requiring dismissal or transfer to a general federal tribunal. Recognition of jurisdiction is confined to administration and enforcement of this statutory exaction and does not enlarge the court’s authority beyond the jurisdiction Congress enacted. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523360/usrep523360.pdf))

##### Question 2: Export tax or compensatory user fee

Holding and operative rule: The Harbor Maintenance Tax violates the Export Clause as applied to exports because its ad valorem measure does not fairly approximate governmental services, facilities, or benefits used by the exporter.

Reasoning: The Export Clause categorically prohibits taxes and duties laid on exported articles, although it permits genuine charges compensating the Government for services actually supplied. The Harbor Maintenance Tax is imposed when export cargo is loaded and is calculated solely as 0.125 percent of cargo value. Cargo value does not reliably correspond to harbor maintenance, navigation services, facility use, or costs attributable to a particular shipment. Revenue enters a national fund supporting projects that are not tied to the exporter’s use of a specific service. General applicability to imports and domestic shipments does not cure the export application because nondiscrimination is not an exception to the Export Clause’s prohibition. Exporters remain subject to reasonable inspection, wharfage, storage, and facility-use charges that fairly measure services rendered. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523360/usrep523360.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. International Business Machines Corp.*: followed; general applicability and nondiscrimination do not cure a tax laid on exports.  
*Pace v. Burgess*: applied; a permissible Export Clause user fee must fairly measure services or facilities supplied rather than the value of exported goods.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is affirmed, and the Court of International Trade’s export refund judgment remains in force.  
Effect: The Harbor Maintenance Tax may not be collected on exports in its present ad valorem form. Nonexport applications and independently lawful charges fairly tied to harbor services or facilities remain unaffected.

### United States v. Hinds

Argued December 3, 1997 | Decided April 6, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: federal church-arson conviction affirmed, 125 F.3d 849, 1997 WL 636810 (4th Cir. 1997) (unpublished). ([Midpage](https://app.midpage.ai/document/united-states-v-hinds-993261))  
Area of Law: Federalism; federal arson; Commerce Clause nexus

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a church is used in interstate commerce or in an activity affecting interstate commerce within the meaning of 18 U.S.C. § 844(i) because it purchases utilities and insurance, employs staff, transmits funds to a national denomination, and receives or distributes religious materials through interstate channels.
2. If § 844(i) covers the church, whether applying the federal arson statute on the basis of those contacts exceeds Congress’s Commerce Clause authority after *United States v. Lopez*.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand with instructions to enter a judgment of acquittal on the § 844(i) count.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s construction of the statutory phrase used in commerce and its refusal to treat ordinary organizational and consumer contacts as active commercial use of worship premises.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Commercial use of the church building

Holding and operative rule: A church building devoted to worship is not used in interstate commerce or in an activity affecting interstate commerce under § 844(i) merely because the congregation purchases goods and utilities, carries insurance, employs staff, receives donations, uses the mail, or affiliates with a national religious organization.

Reasoning: The statutory word used requires active employment of the property in commerce or in an activity that itself affects commerce. The Government proved that the congregation purchased utilities and religious materials, carried insurance, employed several persons, sent funds to a national organization, and participated in interstate communications. Those facts show ordinary participation in modern economic life, not that the building was itself employed to produce, sell, rent, or provide a commercial service. Treating those incidents as sufficient would make nearly every occupied structure federal arson property and deprive the enacted commerce element of limiting work. Section 844(i) reaches rental property and buildings operating commercial schools, stores, broadcast enterprises, or other materially economic activities. The worship-only premises on this record do not satisfy that statutory category. ([Midpage](https://app.midpage.ai/document/united-states-v-hinds-993261))

##### Question 2: Constitutional limit

Not reached; the Government failed to prove the statutory used-in-commerce element, so no broader Commerce Clause ruling is necessary.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Russell v. United States*: clarified; § 844(i) reaches property actively employed in commerce, including rental property, but not every building with ordinary interstate purchases, insurance, utilities, or organizational contacts.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the district court vacate the § 844(i) conviction and enter a judgment of acquittal on that count.  
Effect: The federal arson conviction cannot rest on the church’s passive interstate contacts. State arson prosecution and any independently valid federal offense remain available, as does § 844(i) when religious property is actually employed in a materially commercial activity.
