### United States v. Bird

Argued December 9, 1997 | Decided April 13, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: FACE conviction and supervised-release condition affirmed, 124 F.3d 667 (5th Cir. 1997). ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/us-5th-circuit/1233765.html))  
Area of Law: Commerce Clause; reproductive-health access; supervised release

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Congress may, under the Commerce Clause, prohibit intrastate force and threats directed against a person providing reproductive-health services under the Freedom of Access to Clinic Entrances Act.
2. Whether a supervised-release condition prohibiting Bird from coming within 1,000 feet of any abortion clinic burdens substantially more First Amendment activity than reasonably necessary to serve the purposes of sentencing and supervision.

#### Judgment

Affirmed as to the FACE conviction, 8-1. Adjudication of the supervised-release component is paused.  
Supporting on the conviction: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing on the conviction: Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FACE conviction | Affirmed, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Thomas | The conviction remains in force. |
| Supervised-release condition | Adjudication paused | No vote rendered | No vote rendered | A verified April 13, 1998 live controversy or concrete continuing consequence is required before merits adjudication. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance of the conviction | FACE conviction only |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse the conviction | FACE Commerce Clause issue only |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the FACE conviction. No vote is rendered on the supervised-release condition because his approved merits reach depends on a live controversy not established by the verified record.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court concerning the FACE conviction; adjudication of the supervision question is paused.  
Announced ground: *American Life League v. Reno* controls because Bird used physical force and an express death threat against an identified physician to interfere with access to an operating reproductive-health service. Stone announces no ground concerning the paused restriction.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: FACE application to force and threats

Holding and operative rule: FACE validly reaches intentional force and a true threat directed at preventing a person from providing or obtaining reproductive-health services in an interstate service market.

Reasoning: Bird followed a physician as he entered clinic property, threw a bottle that shattered the physician’s windshield, and threatened to kill him. FACE applies because the force and threat were used with the access-directed purpose required by the statute. The commerce connection does not depend on generalized consequences of violence, medical expense, fear, or productivity loss. It rests on conduct directed at preventing participation in an actual service market that Congress may protect from coercive obstruction. The prosecution therefore does not convert every local assault or protest into a federal offense. Peaceful advocacy, picketing, prayer, leafletting, counseling, and criticism remain outside the rule, while true threats and intentional force receive no First Amendment protection merely because they express opposition to abortion. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/us-5th-circuit/1233765.html))

##### Question 2: Breadth of the supervised-release restriction

Adjudication paused; the verified record establishes the sentence imposed but does not establish whether the one-year restriction remained operative on April 13, 1998, whether execution of the sentence had been stayed, or whether a concrete continuing consequence preserved Article III jurisdiction. No merits vote, holding, or remedial ruling is entered on this question.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas: FACE cannot constitutionally reach this intrastate offense merely because the victim participates in a national service market. He would require an offense-specific interstate transaction or a more direct connection than the local force and threat proved here.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*American Life League v. Reno*: followed; FACE validly regulates intentional force and threats whose object and operation are to obstruct participation in an interstate reproductive-health service market.  
*United States v. Lopez*: distinguished; this prosecution rests on access-directed interference with an actual service market rather than generalized downstream effects of local violence.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed as to Bird’s FACE conviction. No mandate issues concerning the supervised-release condition until the stated Article III blocker is resolved.  
Effect: Bird’s conviction remains in force. The validity of the 1,000-foot restriction remains undecided, and this disposition establishes no authority for enforcing an expired or otherwise nonjusticiable condition.

---

### Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich

Argued December 10, 1997 | Decided April 20, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: central provisions of Ohio’s abortion law invalidated and their enforcement enjoined, 130 F.3d 187 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/187/634761/))  
Area of Law: Personal liberty; abortion regulation; vagueness

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the large-fraction standard associated with *Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey*, rather than the no-set-of-circumstances standard associated with *United States v. Salerno*, governs a facial challenge to an abortion regulation.
2. Whether Ohio’s prohibition of the dilation-and-extraction procedure is unconstitutionally vague or imposes an undue burden because its definitions and civil and criminal provisions fail to provide adequate notice or a sufficient scienter requirement.
3. Whether a postviability abortion prohibition must permit an abortion when necessary to prevent severe, nontemporary, and potentially irreversible harm to the patient’s mental or emotional health.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm on the granted questions and remand for severability and precise relief.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s application of *Casey*’s large-fraction standard, its refusal to rewrite a penal definition reaching ordinary dilation and evacuation, its requirement of culpability concerning disputed clinical judgments, and its limited protection for grave, medically established mental or emotional harm.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Governing facial standard

Holding and operative rule: *Casey*’s large-fraction standard governs a facial challenge to an abortion regulation. A provision is invalid when it creates a substantial obstacle in a large fraction of the cases in which the provision operates as a restriction.

Reasoning: *Casey* invalidated spousal notification by focusing on persons for whom the requirement operated as an actual impediment rather than demanding proof that no constitutional application existed. Persons untouched by a provision do not enlarge the relevant denominator. The same rule is already part of current simulated law through *Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer*. Ohio’s reliance on *Salerno* would permit constitutionally protected access to be obstructed for the affected class so long as some unrelated applications remained valid. Facial relief nevertheless must remain provision specific and fitted to the established operation of the law. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/187/634761/))

##### Question 2: Scope, notice, and scienter of the D&X prohibition

Holding and operative rule: Ohio’s D&X prohibition is invalid because its enacted definition reaches the predominant dilation-and-evacuation method used in the second trimester, while its penal provisions lack sufficient culpability concerning the disputed clinical judgment that separates lawful from unlawful conduct.

Reasoning: The statutory definition focuses on purposeful insertion of a suction device into the fetal skull without distinguishing intact extraction from common D&E techniques in which cranial contents may also be removed by suction. The record therefore showed that physicians could not reliably determine whether ordinary second-trimester D&E fell within the prohibition. An affirmative defense did not remove the initial threat of criminal prosecution, civil liability, and professional consequences. The provisions requiring purposeful performance of an abortion supplied no culpability concerning the physician’s contested classification of the procedure or medical necessity. In a penal regime regulating protected care, a physician cannot be subjected to liability merely because another clinician later disagrees with a good-faith judgment under an indistinct statutory line. Ohio may prohibit a genuinely distinct procedure through a precise definition and an adequate culpability rule. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/187/634761/))

##### Question 3: Postviability protection for grave health consequences

Holding and operative rule: A postviability prohibition must permit an abortion when appropriate medical judgment identifies a grave, medically established danger of severe, lasting, and potentially irreversible mental or emotional injury.

Reasoning: After viability, the State may generally prohibit abortion, but the prohibition must yield when continuation of the pregnancy threatens the patient’s life or health. Health is not confined categorically to immediate physical organ failure. At the same time, a constitutional exception does not arise from transient distress, generalized fear, a conclusory assertion, or an unrestricted physician preference. The required danger must be medically established, severe, nontemporary, and potentially irreversible. Ohio’s definition was expressly confined in operation to specified physical conditions and did not provide a usable route for a comparably grave mental-health danger. The State may require good-faith medical judgment, objective supporting evidence, documentation, and sufficient culpability for abuse of the exception. It may not exclude the constitutionally required category altogether. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/187/634761/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: *Salerno* remains the proper facial standard, and the Ohio provisions possess constitutional applications that defeat wholesale invalidation. The dissent would accept a narrowing construction distinguishing D&X from ordinary D&E and would not constitutionalize the Court’s mental-health formulation.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer*: followed; *Casey*’s large-fraction, operational, cumulative, emergency-route, and penal-notice rules govern.  
*Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey*: applied; previability regulation may not create a substantial obstacle, and postviability prohibition requires protection for life and health.  
*Colautti v. Franklin*: applied; culpability concerning uncertain clinical determinations is especially important when penal liability may chill constitutionally protected care.  
*Planned Parenthood of Central Missouri v. Danforth*: applied; a State may not prohibit the predominant previability method through a definition that materially obstructs access to abortion.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed on the granted questions, and the case is remanded for determination of severability and precise provision-specific relief consistent with this opinion.  
Effect: Ohio may not enforce the challenged D&X prohibition or the deficient penal medical-exception provisions. It may enact a precise procedure prohibition, neutral evidence-based safety rules, adequate culpability requirements, and a postviability ban preserving constitutionally required life-and-health circumstances.

---

### Campbell v. Louisiana

Argued January 20, 1998 | Decided April 21, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Appeal of Louisiana, Third Circuit; judgment below: conviction and rejection of the grand-jury discrimination challenge left in force, 673 So. 2d 1061 (La. App. 3 Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523392/usrep523392.pdf))  
Area of Law: Equal protection; due process; grand-jury selection

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a white criminal defendant has standing to challenge racial discrimination against Black persons in selection of the grand-jury foreperson.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; direct due-process standing and remand for Stone, Scalia, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | Scalia | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the direct due-process standing and remand portions of the Opinion of the Court but does not join its third-party equal-protection-standing rationale.  
Announced ground: Campbell was personally subjected to the charging authority of a grand jury whose composition allegedly depended on race. That direct injury is sufficient without deciding whether he may assert the rights of excluded prospective jurors.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Direct due-process standing

Holding and operative rule: A criminal defendant has standing to pursue his own due-process objection when alleged racial discrimination affected the composition of the grand jury exercising charging authority over him.

Reasoning: Louisiana selected the foreperson from the venire before completing the grand jury, and the selected person became a full voting member rather than a ministerial officer added after the body was constituted. The alleged discrimination therefore affected the composition of the tribunal that returned Campbell’s indictment. His injury does not depend on sharing the race of the excluded jurors. He was personally subjected to a charging process alleged to have been structured through an unconstitutional racial criterion, and vacation of the resulting indictment would redress that injury. The holding establishes standing only. Purposeful discrimination, the evidentiary sufficiency of the claim, any state procedural defense, and the appropriate remedy remain for further proceedings. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523392/usrep523392.pdf))

##### Question 2: Third-party equal-protection standing

Holding and operative rule: A white defendant may also assert the equal-protection rights of Black citizens allegedly excluded from grand-jury service when the defendant is directly injured by the discriminatory selection process, possesses a close litigation relationship to the excluded jurors’ rights, and practical barriers make separate suits by those jurors unlikely. (joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Excluded jurors suffer a direct denial of equal citizenship, while the defendant is uniquely positioned to expose and challenge the discrimination in the criminal proceeding where it operates. The shared interest is not an abstract objection to government conduct. Both the defendant and the excluded jurors seek a charging body selected without racial discrimination. Individual prospective jurors ordinarily lack notice, information, incentive, and an effective procedural route to litigate the recurring exclusion themselves. *Powers v. Ohio* therefore extends to this grand-jury setting. The ruling does not establish that exclusion occurred or authorize third-party standing whenever a litigant raises the rights of absent persons. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523392/usrep523392.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Campbell may pursue his own due-process objection because the alleged discrimination affected the membership of the body that indicted him. That direct ground is sufficient to reverse and remand.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Scalia: The defendant should not receive third-party standing to assert the equal-protection rights of excluded jurors. These Justices would not extend *Powers* beyond its existing setting. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523392/usrep523392.pdf))

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Powers v. Ohio*: extended; third-party standing reaches racial discrimination in grand-jury membership when the defendant and excluded jurors satisfy the established relationship and hindrance requirements.  
*Hobby v. United States*: distinguished; Louisiana selected the foreperson before completing the grand jury and gave the foreperson a full voting seat.  
*Vasquez v. Hillery*: applied; racial discrimination affecting the charging body implicates the defendant’s own right to a lawfully constituted grand jury.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Louisiana Court of Appeal is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the preserved discrimination challenge and any remaining defense or remedial issue.  
Effect: Campbell may litigate both his direct due-process claim and, under the controlling majority rationale, the excluded jurors’ equal-protection claim. The Court finds neither purposeful discrimination nor entitlement to dismissal on the present record.

---

### Miller v. Albright

Argued November 4, 1997 | Decided April 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: rejection of Miller’s citizenship and equal-protection claims affirmed, 96 F.3d 1467 (D.C. Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523420/usrep523420.pdf))  
Area of Law: Citizenship; equal protection; sex classification

Questions Presented:
1. Whether sex- and parentage-based requirements for citizenship of a child born abroad to one citizen parent deny equal protection.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: The Government may require reliable proof of biological parentage and a timely legal relationship, but it may not impose materially different paternal and maternal regimes through assumptions that mothers are naturally committed while fathers are presumptively absent.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Sex and parentage classifications

Holding and operative rule: Derivative-citizenship rules violate equal protection when they impose materially different acknowledgment and relationship requirements on citizen fathers and citizen mothers through sex stereotypes rather than a substantial, nonstereotyped fit to legitimate proof and connection interests.

Reasoning: The Government has important interests in verifying biological parentage, preventing fraudulent citizenship claims, and establishing a genuine legal relationship between the citizen parent and the foreign-born child. Differences in the immediate documentation of maternity and paternity may justify neutral evidentiary requirements responsive to actual proof problems. They do not justify a broader regime that assumes maternal knowledge and commitment while treating paternal absence as the governing norm. Acknowledgment under oath, timely adjudication, reliable records, support, custody, and other objective evidence can establish parentage and relationship without making the available route turn on the citizen parent’s sex. Miller’s father acknowledged paternity and supported her claim, so the statutory asymmetry materially affected her asserted citizenship. Congress’s naturalization authority remains substantial, but it does not eliminate the Fifth Amendment’s equal-protection constraint. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523420/usrep523420.pdf))

##### Question 2: Equality remedy in citizenship administration

Holding and operative rule: A court finding an unconstitutional citizenship classification must provide a real equality remedy while respecting Congress’s authority to prescribe neutral citizenship conditions. Automatic conferral of citizenship is not required when an equivalent lawful procedure can cure the injury.

Reasoning: The remedial task is to eliminate the sex-based disadvantage, not to write a comprehensive citizenship code. The lower court may apply an equivalent acknowledgment and proof regime, preserve the action while Congress receives a reasonable opportunity to enact a neutral replacement, or recognize citizenship if no narrower lawful cure remains and Miller satisfies every nondiscriminatory statutory prerequisite. That sequence avoids both nominal equality and an unnecessary exercise of judicial power over naturalization. It also prevents the Government from preserving the unconstitutional rule merely by invoking remedial difficulty. The judgment does not decide citizenship for every person born abroad or invalidate neutral deadlines and anti-fraud protections. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523420/usrep523420.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens: The statutory distinctions substantially correspond to differences in contemporaneous proof of maternity and paternity and in the opportunity to establish a relationship at birth. He would sustain the requirements as fitted to Congress’s citizenship and foreign-relations responsibilities.

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Courts lack power to confer citizenship on terms Congress did not enact. Even assuming an equal-protection defect, the requested judicial relief is unavailable, and the complaint should be dismissed rather than remanded for reconstruction of the statute.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Virginia*: extended; express sex classifications in derivative-citizenship administration require an important objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped fit.  
*Lehr v. Robertson*: distinguished; objective proof of biological and legal parentage may be required, but generalized assumptions about paternal disinterest cannot justify materially unequal statutory routes.  
*Fiallo v. Bell*: distinguished; congressional authority over entry and immigration does not remove equal-protection review of rules governing claimed citizenship at birth.  
*INS v. Pangilinan*: distinguished; an equality remedy may provide an equivalent statutory path without automatically granting equitable naturalization contrary to Congress’s lawful conditions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for a lawful equal-protection remedy consistent with the alternatives and limits stated in this opinion.  
Effect: Miller is not declared a citizen by this judgment. She must receive an equivalent opportunity to establish citizenship under neutral proof and relationship requirements, with recognition of citizenship available only if the remaining lawful conditions are satisfied and no narrower remedy cures the violation.

---

### California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc.

Argued December 1, 1997 | Decided April 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: federal in rem jurisdiction sustained and additional statutory and title issues resolved, 102 F.3d 379 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523491/usrep523491.pdf))  
Area of Law: Sovereign immunity; admiralty; in rem jurisdiction

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Eleventh Amendment bars a federal in rem admiralty action involving a shipwreck when the State asserts ownership but does not possess the res.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the judgment | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the jurisdictional ruling, vacate the remaining statutory and title conclusions, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Federal arrest of a res outside the State’s possession neither compels the State nor seizes state-held property. California’s title assertion therefore does not defeat jurisdiction, but it also does not establish abandonment, statutory coverage, ownership, or salvage rights.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: In rem jurisdiction over an unpossessed wreck

Holding and operative rule: The Eleventh Amendment does not bar federal in rem admiralty jurisdiction over property outside a State’s possession merely because the State asserts title. Abandonment, application of the Abandoned Shipwreck Act, ownership, and salvage remain separate merits questions.

Reasoning: Deep Sea Research located the *Brother Jonathan* and invoked the district court’s jurisdiction over the vessel and cargo themselves. California had not recovered, possessed, or exercised physical control over the wreck or artifacts when the federal court assumed jurisdiction. The proceeding therefore did not compel the State to appear, seek payment from its treasury, or seize property held by state officers. California may assert its ownership claim in the in rem action without surrendering immunity from personal liability. The lower courts nevertheless proceeded too far by resolving abandonment, preemption, title, and the treatment of uninsured cargo on an incomplete record. Jurisdiction establishes adjudicatory authority, not the merits of the State’s claim. Those additional conclusions must be vacated and reconsidered under the Abandoned Shipwreck Act, admiralty law, and applicable ownership principles. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523491/usrep523491.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: The historical character of admiralty in rem jurisdiction may support an even broader conclusion that a State can be bound by adjudication of the res regardless of the possession distinction. He joins the Court’s narrower holding and judgment. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523491/usrep523491.pdf))

Kennedy, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer: The Court’s reliance on nonpossession should not embed that distinction permanently in Eleventh Amendment admiralty law. The broader issue remains open to reconsideration. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523491/usrep523491.pdf))

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Florida Department of State v. Treasure Salvors, Inc.*: clarified; a State’s assertion of title does not bar in rem jurisdiction over a res outside state possession.  
*The Davis*: followed; actual possession and control remain central to the narrow immunity inquiry governing this case.  
*Ex parte New York*: distinguished; a personal claim against a State or an action directed at property under state possession presents a different immunity question.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The Ninth Circuit’s jurisdictional judgment is affirmed, its judgment in all other respects is vacated, and the case is remanded for source-specific adjudication of abandonment, statutory coverage, ownership, and salvage.  
Effect: The district court may retain jurisdiction over the wreck and bind claimants through a lawful in rem adjudication. California’s title claim is neither defeated nor established by this decision, and no personal or treasury relief may be entered against the State without an independent lawful basis.

---

### Chaudhuri v. Tennessee

Argued January 12, 1998 | Decided April 27, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: Establishment Clause challenges to university-arranged prayer and moments of silence rejected, 130 F.3d 232 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/232/634719/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Establishment Clause; public universities

Questions Presented:
1. Whether officially arranged, nonsectarian invocations at public-university functions violate the Establishment Clause when attendees are adults and attendance is not formally compulsory, including where a university employee alleges practical employment pressure to attend.
2. Whether an officially designated moment of silence adopted as an alternative to spoken prayer violates the Establishment Clause.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Officially arranged invocations | Reversed and remanded, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The lower courts shall resolve the remaining remedial and defendant-specific issues under the constitutional holding. |
| Officially designated moment of silence | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The university may retain a genuinely neutral moment of silence. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer; moment-of-silence holding for Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the moment-of-silence disposition, reverse the officially arranged invocation disposition, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: A neutral period of silence conducts no worship and prescribes no religious message. An invocation selected and installed by university officials is governmental prayer under *Tanford v. Brand*, and adult status or formal voluntariness does not alter that attribution.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Officially arranged university invocation

Holding and operative rule: A public university violates the Establishment Clause when it selects or arranges a speaker to deliver an invocation as part of an official university function. Adult status and formal voluntariness do not authorize government-conducted worship, and practical employment pressure to attend adds to the constitutional concern.

Reasoning: Tennessee State University invited clergy and other selected speakers, instructed them concerning the form of the invocation, and placed the resulting prayer within official programs that included commencement, faculty functions, dedications, and guest events. The university’s attempt to make the prayers generic or nonsectarian did not convert them into private speech. Government may not determine the acceptable content of worship and then present the exercise through its institutional authority. *Tanford v. Brand*, decided December 22, 1997, controls the attribution question. Chaudhuri’s status as an adult professor does not cure governmental sponsorship, while practical employment expectations concerning attendance may add coercive pressure. The Court does not adjudicate genuinely spontaneous private remarks or religious expression admitted neutrally to an open forum. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/232/634719/))

##### Question 2: Genuinely neutral moment of silence

Holding and operative rule: A public university does not violate the Establishment Clause by designating a genuinely neutral moment of silence that neither directs, endorses, nor prescribes religious exercise.

Reasoning: Silence carries no inherent devotional content. Participants remain free to reflect, pray privately, organize their thoughts, or do nothing. The constitutional classification turns on actual design and administration rather than the possibility that some individuals may use the period religiously. The challenged practice supplied no official prayer text, selected no religious leader, and required no participant to adopt a religious message. A spontaneous audience prayer during one period of silence was private conduct not directed or endorsed by university officials. A nominal period of silence expressly designed or administered as a command to pray would present a different case, but the record before the Court establishes a neutral observance. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/232/634719/))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: These Justices agree that a genuinely neutral moment of silence is constitutional and join that part of the judgment.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: Brief nonsectarian invocations at university functions attended by adults do not compel religious observance and may serve a permissible solemnizing function. These Justices would affirm the judgment concerning the invocations.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Tanford v. Brand*: followed; official university selection and presentation of prayer violate the Establishment Clause notwithstanding an adult audience or formally voluntary attendance.  
*Lee v. Weisman*: applied; governmental organization of prayer remains constitutionally distinct from private religious expression.  
*Wallace v. Jaffree*: distinguished; a genuinely neutral moment of silence that does not direct prayer is not equivalent to a statutory or administrative endorsement of religious exercise.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed as to the officially designated moment of silence, reversed as to officially arranged invocations, and remanded for resolution of standing, recurrence, damages, qualified immunity, and precise relief consistent with the granted questions.  
Effect: Tennessee State University may use a genuinely neutral moment of silence but may not arrange an official invocation as part of a university function. Spontaneous private remarks, neutral forum access, and individual liability remain governed by their own requirements.

Issue 1: Hobbs Act application to local commercial robbery

Controlling Simulated Law: United States v. Lopez bars use of attenuated aggregate consequences to convert Congress's commerce authority into a general police power. United States v. Hale permits aggregation of local economic conduct when the regulated class directly operates on a defined interstate market. American Life League v. Reno permits regulation of conduct whose object and operation directly interfere with an actual interstate service market. Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno permits regulation when an interstate transmission is itself an operative part of the commercial transaction.

Current Reusable Doctrine: Congress may regulate channels and instrumentalities of interstate commerce, persons or things in interstate commerce, and economic activity whose properly defined class substantially affects an actual interstate market. A jurisdictional element must perform genuine constitutional work; generalized crime costs, insurance effects, productivity effects, or an object's remote interstate history are insufficient.

Other Governing Law: Commerce Clause; 18 U.S.C. § 1951.

Other Authority Used: The Hobbs Act's enacted commerce element and the record showing direct depletion of operating assets of businesses engaged in interstate commerce are used to distinguish this application from the noneconomic possession invalidated in Lopez.

Issue 1: Hobbs Act application to local commercial robbery

Threshold and Merits Reach: Stone reaches only the granted Commerce Clause challenge to the Hobbs Act convictions. He does not reach the vulnerable-victim sentencing issue.

Stone's Legal Position: Stone applies the existing substantial-effects doctrine to a properly defined economic class. Robbery that directly depletes the operating assets of an active business is economic conduct, unlike the noneconomic firearm possession at issue in Lopez. The Hobbs Act's commerce element performs genuine work when the Government proves that the charged robbery actually affected a business participating in interstate commerce. The individual effect need not independently be substantial when the regulated class is economic and, in the aggregate, substantially affects interstate commerce. Hale, American Life League, and Pic-A-State support aggregation where the regulated conduct operates directly on an interstate commercial market or transaction; they do not authorize reliance on generalized social costs.

Stone's Proposed Holding: Congress may apply the Hobbs Act to robbery that directly depletes the operating assets of a business participating in interstate commerce when the enacted commerce element is proved. The individual effect may be slight when the properly defined class of economic robberies substantially affects interstate commerce.

Stone's Reasoning and Record Application: Robinson robbed operating commercial establishments and directly depleted assets used in their businesses. The Government therefore relied on an effect produced by the charged economic conduct itself, not on remote consequences such as insurance costs, lost productivity, or the interstate history of an object. The statutory commerce element requires a case-specific connection and was satisfied on the supplied record. Because the conduct is economic and the nexus is actual rather than hypothetical, aggregation does not erase the federal-state boundary recognized in Lopez.

Boundary or Reserved Question: This holding does not federalize every robbery, permit aggregation through generalized crime or social costs, or allow an object's remote interstate history to substitute for a genuine connection between the charged conduct and interstate commerce. The Court does not decide the vulnerable-victim sentencing enhancement.
