## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Robinson

Argued December 2, 1997 | Decided March 23, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: Hobbs Act convictions affirmed and sentence vacated on a separate ground, 119 F.3d 1205 (5th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/119/1205/486619/))  
Area of Law: Federalism; Commerce Clause; Hobbs Act

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Hobbs Act may constitutionally be applied to a local robbery based on a de minimis effect on an individual business engaged in interstate commerce, on the theory that the aggregate class of robberies substantially affects interstate commerce.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Robbery that directly depletes the operating assets of a business participating in interstate commerce is economic conduct, and the Hobbs Act’s case-specific commerce element permits aggregation of that properly defined class without relying on generalized social costs.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Local commercial robbery and the Hobbs Act

Holding and operative rule: Congress may apply the Hobbs Act to robbery that directly depletes the operating assets of a business participating in interstate commerce. The effect of the individual robbery may be slight when the regulated class is economic, the statutory commerce element is actually proved, and the class substantially affects interstate commerce.

Reasoning: The charged robberies took cash used by operating retail and check-cashing businesses, impaired their ability to cash out-of-state checks, restock goods shipped from other States, and continue ordinary commercial activity. The relevant connection therefore arose from the charged economic conduct itself. It did not depend on insurance costs, reduced productivity, public fear, or an object’s remote interstate history. The Hobbs Act also requires the Government to prove an actual effect on commerce in each prosecution, preventing the aggregation principle from becoming a general federal robbery power. Because robbery of business assets is economic conduct and these offenses directly operated on active commercial enterprises, Congress could regulate the class and reach an individual instance whose proved effect was slight. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/119/1205/486619/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas: A de minimis effect on one local business is insufficient to convert ordinary robbery into a federal offense. He would require either an interstate transaction within the offense itself or a materially more direct constitutional connection than aggregate depletion of business assets.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Lopez*: distinguished; the Hobbs Act regulates economic robbery through a case-specific commerce element rather than noneconomic possession linked to commerce by generalized consequences.  
*United States v. Hale*: applied; a properly defined class of economic conduct operating directly on an interstate market may be considered in the aggregate.  
*American Life League v. Reno* and *Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno*: applied; Congress may regulate conduct whose object and operation directly affect an actual interstate service market or commercial transaction.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it sustains Robinson’s Hobbs Act convictions.  
Effect: The Hobbs Act convictions remain in force. The Fifth Circuit’s separate sentencing disposition is outside the grant and is neither approved nor disturbed.

---

### United States v. Bird

Argued December 9, 1997 | Decided April 13, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: FACE conviction and the challenged 1,000-foot supervised-release condition affirmed, 124 F.3d 667 (5th Cir. 1997). ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/124/f3d/667/united-states-v-bird))  
Area of Law: Commerce Clause; First Amendment; supervised release

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Congress may, under the Commerce Clause, prohibit intrastate force and threats directed against a person providing reproductive-health services under the Freedom of Access to Clinic Entrances Act.
2. Whether a supervised-release condition prohibiting Bird from coming within 1,000 feet of any abortion clinic burdens substantially more First Amendment activity than reasonably necessary to serve the purposes of sentencing and supervision.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FACE conviction | Affirmed, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Thomas | The conviction remains in force. |
| Supervised-release condition | Vacated and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The lower court shall determine whether a live Article III controversy exists before reaching § 3583(d) or the First Amendment. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Thomas as to Question 2 | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Complete opinion for Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Question 2 for Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the FACE conviction and to vacate and remand the supervised-release component solely for determination of Article III jurisdiction.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: FACE validly reaches Bird’s intentional force and death threat directed at obstructing an operating reproductive-health service. The Court cannot adjudicate the separate restriction without first determining whether the condition remained operative or continues to produce a concrete legal consequence.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: FACE application to force and threats

Holding and operative rule: FACE constitutionally reaches intentional force and a true threat directed at preventing a person from providing or obtaining reproductive-health services in an interstate service market.

Reasoning: Bird followed a physician who provided abortions, attacked the physician’s vehicle, and issued an express death threat. The prosecution therefore rests on force and intimidation used for the access-directed purpose required by the statute, not on protected criticism, prayer, counseling, leafletting, or peaceful protest. The commerce connection likewise does not depend on the generalized effects of violence. Bird’s conduct was directed at obstructing participation in an existing reproductive-health service market, placing the offense within the rule established in *American Life League v. Reno*. The application does not federalize every assault or every protest concerning abortion; it reaches the intentional force and true threat proved here. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/124/f3d/667/united-states-v-bird))

##### Question 2: Article III status of the supervised-release challenge

Holding and operative rule: A federal court may not reach the First Amendment or § 3583(d) merits of an expired or potentially expired supervised-release condition until the record establishes either that the condition remains operative or that it produces a concrete continuing legal consequence. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The record establishes the sentence imposed but does not establish when Bird’s imprisonment began or ended, when supervised release commenced or expired, whether execution of either component was stayed, or whether the 1,000-foot condition remained operative on April 13, 1998. If the restriction expired, *Spencer v. Kemna* requires Bird to identify a concrete continuing consequence traceable specifically to that condition. Speculative future use or a generalized interest in obtaining a ruling is insufficient. The Fifth Circuit’s merits judgment concerning the condition must therefore be vacated. On remand, the court shall resolve those jurisdictional facts first. Only if a live controversy remains may it consider the condition under § 3583(d) and the applicable First Amendment law. No view is expressed concerning the restriction’s breadth or permissible replacement. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/124/f3d/667/united-states-v-bird))

#### Concurrences

Thomas: He agrees that the supervised-release judgment must be vacated and remanded for a threshold determination of Article III jurisdiction before any merits ruling.

#### Dissents

Thomas: FACE cannot constitutionally reach this intrastate offense merely because its victim participates in a national service market. He would reverse the conviction while joining the jurisdictional remand.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*American Life League v. Reno*: followed; FACE validly reaches intentional force and true threats directed at obstructing participation in an interstate reproductive-health service market.  
*Spencer v. Kemna*: applied; expiration of a criminal-supervision condition requires a concrete continuing consequence rather than a conjectural future effect.  
*Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment*: applied; Article III jurisdiction must be established before the merits of the supervised-release restriction are adjudicated.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed as to Bird’s FACE conviction, vacated as to the supervised-release condition, and remanded solely for determination of Article III jurisdiction before any merits proceedings concerning that condition.  
Effect: Bird’s conviction remains in force. No First Amendment or § 3583(d) ruling is entered concerning the 1,000-foot condition; if the challenge is moot, it must be dismissed, and if it remains live, the lower court may then adjudicate it under the governing merits law.

---

### Calderon v. Thompson

Argued December 9, 1997 | Decided April 29, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: mandate recalled and federal habeas relief granted, 120 F.3d 1045 (9th Cir. 1997) (en banc). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523538/usrep523538.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas; appellate mandate; finality

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a court of appeals abused its power by recalling its mandate in a capital habeas case to reconsider an earlier denial of relief.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand with instructions to reinstate the prior mandate.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between the continued existence of mandate-recall authority and the extraordinary showing required for its lawful exercise in a concluded capital habeas case.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Authority under AEDPA

Holding and operative rule: AEDPA did not eliminate a court of appeals’ authority to recall a mandate when the court acts solely on a prisoner’s first federal habeas application. A genuine second or successive application remains governed by § 2244(b).

Reasoning: The Ninth Circuit expressly stated that it acted sua sponte on the claims and evidence contained in Thompson’s first federal habeas proceeding rather than on a later application. The record did not disprove that characterization. Section 2244(b) therefore did not directly withdraw the court’s traditional mandate authority. *Felker v. Turpin* requires enforcement of AEDPA’s actual gatekeeping triggers, not expansion of those triggers by implication. That conclusion concerns authority only. It does not make recall routine, permit a court to evade AEDPA by relabeling a successive filing, or authorize consideration of new claims or evidence outside the first proceeding. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523538/usrep523538.pdf))

##### Question 1: Abuse of mandate-recall discretion

Holding and operative rule: A court of appeals abuses its discretion by recalling a completed habeas mandate merely to correct delayed internal rehearing misunderstandings or to undertake a renewed merits assessment. Recall remains reserved for fraud, nullity, a demonstrable adjudicatory breakdown, or reliable evidence satisfying the applicable actual-innocence gateway.

Reasoning: The ordinary panel, rehearing, en banc, certiorari, and mandate processes had concluded. The asserted internal misunderstandings were known months before the recall, yet the court delayed until two days before the scheduled execution. The evidence used to revisit the merits had been available in the first federal proceeding and consisted principally of additional impeachment and disputed expert interpretation rather than reliable proof satisfying *Schlup*. Finality could not be displaced simply because members of the court later evaluated the existing record differently. The rule does not foreclose correction of a clerical or jurisdictional nullity, fraud on the court, failure of the court’s actual judgment to issue, or a qualifying showing of factual innocence. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523538/usrep523538.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The court of appeals possessed inherent authority to correct a malfunction in its own decisional process and did not abuse its discretion by preventing execution under a judgment it concluded was unreliable. The dissent would not confine recall to the Court’s newly stated actual-innocence boundary.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Felker v. Turpin*: clarified; AEDPA’s successive-application provisions do not eliminate mandate-recall authority when the court acts solely on a first application.  
*Schlup v. Delo*: followed; reliable whole-record actual innocence supplies the relevant miscarriage-of-justice boundary.  
*Lonchar v. Thomas*: applied; source-governed habeas limits and finality constrain extraordinary reopening of a completed judgment.  
*Hawaii Housing Authority v. Midkiff*: clarified; appellate mandate recall is a last-resort power reviewable for abuse of discretion.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to reinstate its original mandate denying federal habeas relief.  
Effect: The habeas relief granted through the recall is set aside. No further reassessment may rest on the delayed rehearing concerns or renewed merits evaluation used in the recalled mandate.

---

### Crawford-El v. Britton

Argued December 1, 1997 | Decided May 4, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: clear-and-convincing motive proof and special procedures imposed for motive-based constitutional claims, 93 F.3d 813 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (en banc). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523574/usrep523574.pdf))  
Area of Law: Civil rights litigation; unconstitutional motive; qualified immunity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a civil-rights plaintiff alleging unconstitutional motive must prove that motive by clear and convincing evidence or satisfy special heightened procedures.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | O’Connor | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that qualified immunity supplies an objective affirmative defense but does not authorize courts to rewrite the burden governing an element of a motive-defined constitutional claim.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Proof and procedure for unconstitutional-motive claims

Holding and operative rule: Qualified immunity does not authorize clear-and-convincing proof or an equivalent special merits barrier merely because unconstitutional motive is an element of a § 1983 claim. Ordinary pleading, causation, discovery, summary-judgment, and immunity rules apply.

Reasoning: *Harlow v. Fitzgerald* changed the qualified-immunity defense by removing subjective bad faith from that defense and permitting early resolution through an objective legal inquiry. It did not alter the elements of constitutional rights for which governmental motive remains material. Neither § 1983 nor the Federal Rules establishes a heightened burden for those claims, and a court may not create one to address anticipated litigation costs. Trial courts retain substantial authority to require nonconclusory allegations, identify the precise constitutional rule, sequence discovery, limit requests to material issues, resolve immunity first when possible, and grant summary judgment when the evidence cannot support causation or unlawful motive. Those tools address insubstantial litigation without changing the plaintiff’s burden at trial. Britton’s actual motive, causation, lawful same-decision defense, and entitlement to qualified immunity remain unresolved. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523574/usrep523574.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy: Existing case-management authority should be used firmly to protect officials from unnecessary discovery and trial. That authority remains procedural and does not include a heightened burden of persuasion.

#### Dissents

O’Connor: Motive-based claims can defeat the practical protection of qualified immunity by requiring intrusive discovery into official decisionmaking. She would sustain a heightened preliminary showing before such claims proceed.

Scalia, joined by Thomas: An objectively lawful official act should not ordinarily become actionable because of an alleged improper motive. These Justices would affirm on a broader immunity ground rather than preserve motive-based liability under ordinary civil procedure.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Harlow v. Fitzgerald*: clarified; objective qualified immunity does not rewrite the plaintiff’s burden on a motive-defined constitutional claim.  
*Leatherman v. Tarrant County Narcotics Intelligence and Coordination Unit*: followed; federal courts may not invent heightened pleading or proof rules absent statutory or rule-based authority.  
*Johnson v. Jones* and *Behrens v. Pelletier*: applied; legal immunity questions remain distinct from evidentiary sufficiency and factual motive disputes.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for application of the ordinary civil burden and the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.  
Effect: Crawford-El need not prove unconstitutional motive by clear and convincing evidence. The lower courts may limit discovery and resolve immunity or summary judgment under ordinary standards, while liability, causation, any lawful same-decision defense, and Britton’s ultimate immunity remain open.

---

### Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department

Argued January 12, 1998 | Decided May 4, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: the ordinance’s nonprofit-affiliation distinction invalidated and judgment for the city reversed, 121 F.3d 1365 (9th Cir. 1997). ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/121/f3d/1365/perry-v-los-angeles-police-department))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; traditional public forum; speaker discrimination

Questions Presented:
1. Whether an ordinance allowing nonprofit organizations, but not unaffiliated individual speakers, to solicit donations and sell message-bearing merchandise in a traditional public forum violates the First Amendment.
2. Whether the nonprofit-status distinction is narrowly tailored to the city’s asserted traffic-flow and unfair-competition interests and leaves individual speakers with adequate alternative channels of communication.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that nonprofit affiliation bears no demonstrated relationship to the physical effects the City seeks to regulate.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Expressive sales and speaker classification

Holding and operative rule: Sales of recordings, literature, buttons, clothing, and other message-bearing items remain protected expression when the sale is integrated with the speaker’s political, religious, or ideological communication. A city may not condition comparable expressive solicitation or sales on nonprofit organizational status.

Reasoning: Perry’s performances and recordings and Newman’s literature and slogan-bearing merchandise conveyed religious, political, and ideological messages. Their sale did not strip those works of protection, and the expressive and financial components could not realistically be separated. The ordinance nevertheless allowed qualifying nonprofit organizations to engage in the same solicitation and sales while excluding unaffiliated individuals. That line classified speakers by organizational form rather than by the conduct’s effect on the forum. The City may distinguish genuinely commercial vending from protected expression through neutral rules, but nonprofit status does not itself identify which activity is commercial, expressive, fraudulent, congesting, or disruptive. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/121/f3d/1365/perry-v-los-angeles-police-department))

##### Question 2: Narrow tailoring

Holding and operative rule: The nonprofit-status distinction is not narrowly tailored to pedestrian flow or unfair-competition interests because affiliated and unaffiliated speakers engaged in physically identical conduct produce the same asserted effects.

Reasoning: Traffic management and protection against genuinely commercial obstruction are significant interests. The challenged classification, however, permits nonprofit speakers to occupy space, approach pedestrians, request donations, and sell expressive merchandise while prohibiting unaffiliated speakers from doing precisely the same things. Nothing in the record shows that the latter group creates greater congestion or a different competitive burden. The distinction therefore removes a substantial amount of expression that does not create the evil the City identified. Because the unsupported speaker classification is sufficient to invalidate the scheme, the Court does not separately decide whether every plaintiff possessed an adequate alternative channel. Neutral limits concerning location, stand size, hours, safety, fraud, licensing, and purely commercial goods remain available. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/121/f3d/1365/perry-v-los-angeles-police-department))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas: The City may regulate vending as conduct and use nonprofit status as an administrable method of distinguishing expressive activity from ordinary commerce. He would sustain the ordinance as a reasonable incremental response to Boardwalk congestion.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, Inc.*: followed; an exemption unrelated to the physical harm invoked by the government defeats narrow tailoring.  
*Riley v. National Federation of the Blind of North Carolina*: applied; inextricably intertwined expressive and financial components receive full First Amendment protection.  
*Ward v. Rock Against Racism*: applied; a time, place, or manner rule may not burden substantially more protected expression than necessary to serve the asserted interest.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Los Angeles may not condition otherwise comparable expressive solicitation or sales on nonprofit affiliation. It may adopt viewpoint-neutral and speaker-neutral rules governing congestion, safety, fraud, physical structures, and genuinely commercial vending.

---

### Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10

Argued January 13, 1998 | Decided May 11, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: exclusion of weekly religious worship services from the school-access program affirmed, 127 F.3d 207 (2d Cir. 1997). ([Digital Commons at NYLS](https://digitalcommons.nyls.edu/circuit_opinions/198/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; limited public forum; religious viewpoint

Questions Presented:
1. Whether excluding religious worship services from a public school’s limited public forum, while permitting discussion of religious subjects and comparable secular community activities, is a permissible subject-matter restriction or unconstitutional viewpoint discrimination.
2. Whether avoiding an Establishment Clause violation supplies a constitutionally sufficient reason for excluding private religious worship from the forum.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s classification of worship and religious instruction as a private religious viewpoint within the broad educational, moral, family, and community subjects the District opened to other groups.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Religious worship within the opened forum

Holding and operative rule: A school district that opens its facilities to private educational, moral, family, and community expression may not exclude an otherwise eligible group because its presentation includes religious worship or instruction integral to its viewpoint.

Reasoning: The District permitted broad private uses addressing family life, education, moral development, community welfare, culture, and related subjects. Bronx Household proposed an after-hours gathering addressing those same matters through Christian teaching, prayer, and worship. The exclusion therefore turned on the religious mode and viewpoint of the presentation rather than on a neutral physical, temporal, or subject-matter boundary. Government may define a limited forum, but it may not admit secular perspectives on permitted subjects while identifying the religious perspective as the forbidden form of expression. The activity remained subject to the same scheduling, safety, supervision, and nondisruption rules as other private uses. The simulation’s prior school-access and private-forum holdings govern that classification. `HOLDINGS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

##### Question 2: Establishment Clause justification

Holding and operative rule: Equal access for privately sponsored after-hours worship does not establish religion when neutral rules, private control, voluntary attendance, and clear attribution distinguish the activity from school-sponsored worship.

Reasoning: The District transferred no public funds for devotional content, selected no officiant, composed no prayer, compelled no attendance, and exercised no control over the Church’s religious message. The activity occurred outside instructional hours in facilities made available to diverse private groups. Neutral disclaimers and sponsorship rules may further prevent misattribution. Equal access under those conditions differs from the official university-arranged prayer invalidated in *Tanford v. Brand* and *Chaudhuri v. Tennessee*, where the public institution itself selected and installed a devotional exercise. Avoiding establishment therefore supplies no sufficient reason for the discriminatory exclusion imposed here. ([Digital Commons at NYLS](https://digitalcommons.nyls.edu/circuit_opinions/198/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg: Weekly worship is a distinct activity rather than merely another viewpoint on a permitted subject, and its recurring use of a public middle school creates attribution concerns that the District may address through exclusion. They would affirm the forum limitation.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Good News/Good Sports Club*: followed; religious teaching and worship within permitted moral and community subject matter constitute a viewpoint rather than an excluded subject.  
*Church on the Rock*: followed; neutral access for privately sponsored religious activity does not convert that activity into governmental worship.  
*Tanford v. Brand* and *Chaudhuri v. Tennessee*: distinguished; those decisions concerned officially selected and presented prayer rather than neutral access to a private forum.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions that the District permit access on the same terms as comparable private groups or redefine the forum through genuinely viewpoint-neutral subject rules.  
Effect: Bronx Household may use the facility subject to neutral scheduling, safety, supervision, nondisruption, and attribution requirements. The District need not permit activity during instruction, teacher-led worship, coercion, public financing of devotion, or uses outside a lawfully defined forum.

---

### Rice v. Paladin Enterprises, Inc.

Argued January 14, 1998 | Decided May 14, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the publisher reversed and wrongful-death claims remanded for trial, 128 F.3d 233 (4th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/128/233/525203/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; aiding unlawful conduct; publisher liability

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the First Amendment bars civil aiding-and-abetting liability where a publisher intentionally produces and markets detailed instructions to facilitate murder and those instructions are used to commit the murders at issue.
2. What level of intent, specificity, assistance, and causal connection is required before instructional expression may be treated as speech integral to criminal conduct rather than protected abstract advocacy or publication.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s narrow rejection of categorical publisher immunity where specific criminal intent, operational assistance, material use, and causal connection are established.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Categorical First Amendment immunity

Holding and operative rule: The First Amendment does not categorically bar generally applicable tort liability when a publisher intentionally provides detailed operational assistance for murder, intends that assistance to be used criminally, and the publication materially facilitates the resulting crime.

Reasoning: For summary-judgment purposes Paladin stipulated that it intended to attract and assist murderers, intended the manual to be used to plan and execute murder for hire, and materially assisted the killer in the murders at issue. The killer followed numerous operational directions concerning weapons, disguises, travel, concealment, execution, and destruction of evidence. Those stipulations remove the ordinary risk that liability will be inferred merely from unpopular subject matter, foreseeability, or a reader’s independent criminal choice. The action applies ordinary aiding-and-abetting principles to communication intentionally supplied as practical assistance to criminal conduct. Abstract advocacy, crime reporting, fiction, historical description, and general discussion remain protected. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/128/233/525203/))

##### Question 2: Required intent and assistance

Holding and operative rule: Liability requires proof of specific intent to facilitate criminal use, operationally concrete assistance, material use of that assistance in the crime, and the causation and other elements required by governing tort law. Negligent publication, generalized dangerous ideas, or remote foreseeability are insufficient.

Reasoning: The constitutional line does not turn on a publication’s title, offensiveness, realism, or description of illegal conduct. It turns on the publisher’s proved purpose and the communication’s function in the unlawful transaction. The plaintiffs must establish that Paladin intended criminal use, supplied assistance sufficiently specific to operate as practical aid, and materially contributed to the murders through the ordinary requirements of Maryland law. Paladin may contest the stipulations and every element that remains legally open at trial. The Court decides only that the First Amendment supplies no complete defense on the confined record presented. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/128/233/525203/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Brandenburg v. Ohio*: distinguished; protection for abstract advocacy does not immunize intentional operational assistance to a specific serious crime.  
*Giboney v. Empire Storage & Ice Co.*: applied and clarified; speech integral to unlawful conduct may be reached through a generally applicable liability rule when the required intent and assistance are proved.  
*NAACP v. Claiborne Hardware Co.*: distinguished; liability here rests on specific criminal purpose and material assistance rather than association, persuasion, or abstract rhetoric.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for trial under ordinary tort elements, proof requirements, causation principles, defenses, and damages law.  
Effect: Paladin receives no categorical First Amendment immunity on the stipulated record. The plaintiffs may not recover merely because the publication discussed crime or because criminal misuse was foreseeable.

---

### Bousley v. United States

Argued March 3, 1998 | Decided May 18, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: collateral relief denied, 97 F.3d 284 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523614/usrep523614.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas; guilty pleas; actual innocence

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a guilty plea based on a materially incorrect understanding of use under 18 U.S.C. § 924(c) may be challenged collaterally, and what actual innocence requires after procedural default.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: A plea entered under a materially incorrect understanding of the charged offense may be unintelligent, but failure to challenge the plea on direct review produces procedural default. Bousley may obtain merits review only through the ordinary cause-and-prejudice or factual-innocence gateway.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): Plea intelligence and Bailey

Holding and operative rule: A guilty plea is not intelligent if the defendant was materially misinformed about an essential element of the charged offense, and *Bailey v. United States* supplies the controlling meaning of use on collateral review. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: A guilty plea requires real notice of the offense’s actual elements. *Bailey* did not create a new procedural rule; it stated what § 924(c)’s use provision had meant since enactment. A plea based on the contrary understanding that mere storage or possession constituted use may therefore be constitutionally defective. Finality principles still govern the route to relief, but neither *Teague* nor the fact that lower courts previously misconstrued the statute transforms legally innocent conduct into the offense Congress enacted. The present judgment does not itself invalidate Bousley’s plea because procedural default and factual innocence remain unresolved. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523614/usrep523614.pdf))

##### Question 1(b): Procedural default and actual innocence

Holding and operative rule: Failure to challenge the plea on direct review procedurally defaults the claim, and perceived futility alone is not cause. The default may be excused if, on the whole admissible record, it is more likely than not that no reasonable juror would convict under § 924(c)’s correct use elements. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Bousley appealed his sentence without challenging the plea, although the legal objection was available. The ordinary default rule therefore applies. On remand, he may invoke factual actual innocence, not merely legal insufficiency. The Government may present any admissible evidence of active employment and may require innocence of a genuinely more serious charge actually forgone in the plea bargain. It may not substitute an uncharged carry theory merely because that different statutory route might fit the facts. The indictment alleged use, and the present record does not show that a carry charge was abandoned in exchange for the plea. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523614/usrep523614.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: He agrees that *Bailey* supplies the governing statutory meaning and that the plea was based on a materially incorrect understanding. He would vacate the § 924(c) conviction and permit a new plea without requiring Bousley to pass through the Court’s procedural-default gateway.

#### Dissents

Stevens: He disagrees that failure to challenge a constitutionally unintelligent plea on direct appeal requires the prisoner to prove actual innocence before the conviction may be set aside.

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The actual-innocence exception should not be extended to a conviction based on a guilty plea after an unexcused default. Reconstructing hypothetical trials years later, without an original trial record, undermines the finality obtained through the plea.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Bailey v. United States*: followed; use requires active employment, and that statutory meaning governs the plea’s legal validity.  
*Schlup v. Delo*: extended; whole-record factual innocence may excuse procedural default of a guilty-plea challenge.  
*Teague v. Lane*: distinguished; authoritative interpretation of a substantive criminal statute is not a new procedural rule.  
*Engle v. Isaac*: followed; a claim’s likely rejection under then-prevailing precedent does not itself establish cause.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for determination whether Bousley can overcome procedural default through factual actual innocence under the correct use elements.  
Effect: Bousley’s conviction is not vacated by this judgment. The Government may rely on the whole admissible record and any genuinely more serious charge actually forgone in the plea bargain, but it may not replace the charged use offense with an uncharged carry theory.

### Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal

Argued February 25, 1998 | Decided May 18, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: renewed Ford claim held not to be a second or successive application, 118 F.3d 628 (9th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523637/usrep523637.pdf))  
Area of Law: Capital habeas; successive applications; competency to be executed

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a Ford incompetency-to-be-executed claim, dismissed as premature in an initial habeas petition and renewed when execution becomes imminent, is second or successive.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Section 2244(b)(3)(E) does not bar review of a determination that no successive application exists, and a claim timely raised in the first petition but dismissed solely as premature remains part of that proceeding when it first becomes ripe.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Certiorari jurisdiction

Holding and operative rule: Section 2244(b)(3)(E) does not bar certiorari review of a court-of-appeals ruling that no second or successive application exists.

Reasoning: Congress barred review of an actual decision granting or denying authorization to file a successive application. The Ninth Circuit did not exercise that gatekeeping authority. It held instead that the renewed Ford claim remained part of the original proceeding and therefore required no authorization. Extending the review bar to that antecedent classification would enlarge the jurisdictional restriction beyond the action Congress identified. *Felker v. Turpin* remains controlling when a court of appeals actually grants or denies authorization; it does not foreclose review of whether the gatekeeping mechanism applies at all. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523637/usrep523637.pdf))

##### Question 1: Renewal of a previously premature claim

Holding and operative rule: A materially identical claim timely raised in the first federal habeas petition and dismissed solely as premature is not second or successive when renewed at its first ripening.

Reasoning: Martinez-Villareal presented the Ford claim in his first adjudicated federal proceeding, but no court could decide competency to be executed before execution became imminent. He returned when the claim first became justiciable, doing precisely what the prematurity ruling required. Treating that return as a prohibited successive application would convert orderly timing into permanent forfeiture of a claim that never received merits adjudication. The case differs from *Calderon v. Thompson*, where a court reopened a completed merits determination on an existing record. The holding does not excuse omission from the initial petition, permit relitigation of a decided claim, eliminate exhaustion, or resolve Martinez-Villareal’s competency. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523637/usrep523637.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The renewed request is a second application presenting a claim previously included in an earlier application, and § 2244(b)(1) therefore requires dismissal. The Court’s concern about harsh consequences cannot displace the enacted text.

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Once the earlier petition was denied, the Ford claim was no longer before the district court, and a new request was necessarily a new application. Historical habeas practice supplies no basis for treating the renewed filing as part of the closed case.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Felker v. Turpin*: clarified; § 2244(b)(3)(E)’s review bar applies to genuine authorization decisions, not to a ruling that no successive application exists.  
*Calderon v. Thompson*: distinguished; reopening a previously adjudicated merits claim differs from adjudicating a timely claim dismissed solely as premature when it first becomes ripe.  
*Lonchar v. Thomas*: applied; procedural handling of a first petition may not eliminate lawful review of a timely claim that has never received merits adjudication.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the Ford claim may proceed as part of the original federal habeas case.  
Effect: The lower courts may adjudicate competency, exhaustion, and any remaining merits or procedural issue. The Court expresses no view on whether Martinez-Villareal is competent to be executed.

### Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes

Argued October 8, 1997 | Decided May 18, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: exclusion of Forbes from the candidate debate held unconstitutional, 93 F.3d 497 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523666/usrep523666.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; public broadcasting; candidate debates

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a public broadcaster creates a public forum by sponsoring a candidate debate and whether excluding a candidate under neutral journalistic criteria violates the First Amendment.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and direct judgment for the Commission on the forum claim.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s classification of the selectively composed debate as a nonpublic forum and its record-specific conclusion that Forbes was excluded through reasonable, viewpoint-neutral journalistic criteria.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Candidate selection for a public-broadcast debate

Holding and operative rule: A public broadcaster’s selectively composed candidate debate is a nonpublic forum when the broadcaster retains editorial selection and has not opened the program to every ballot-qualified candidate. Exclusion is permissible when reasonable in light of the program’s journalistic purpose and not a pretext for viewpoint discrimination.

Reasoning: The debate was designed for candidate speech, but the Commission did not make it generally available to all candidates. It retained editorial control and selected participants individually according to public interest, campaign activity, and newsworthiness. Selective eligibility followed by candidate-specific permission does not create a designated public forum. The record also contained express findings that political pressure and disagreement with Forbes’s views did not cause his exclusion. His lack of appreciable public support was a reasonable journalistic consideration in composing a finite broadcast. The holding does not authorize viewpoint exclusion, incumbent favoritism, selectively administered criteria, or exclusion from a program genuinely opened to every qualifying candidate. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523666/usrep523666.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter and Ginsburg: A state broadcaster that creates a candidate debate assumes a special obligation of political neutrality and should not exclude a ballot-qualified candidate through unpublished judgments of viability. These Justices would affirm the First Amendment judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Cornelius v. NAACP Legal Defense & Educational Fund, Inc.*: followed; selective access without purposeful general opening creates a nonpublic forum.  
*Perry Education Association v. Perry Local Educators’ Association*: applied; reasonable viewpoint-neutral restrictions govern a nonpublic forum.  
Public-broadcast candidate debates: established; journalistic selection is constitutionally permissible when the broadcaster retains editorial control and acts through reasonable, viewpoint-neutral criteria.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is reversed, and judgment shall be entered for the Arkansas Educational Television Commission on Forbes’s forum claim.  
Effect: The Commission need not include Forbes in the debate or publish constitutional selection rules for future programs. Viewpoint discrimination, sham criteria, and a program genuinely opened to all candidates remain subject to First Amendment review.

### Ohio Forestry Association, Inc. v. Sierra Club

Argued February 25, 1998 | Decided May 18, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: forest-plan challenge held ripe and aspects of the plan invalidated, 105 F.3d 248 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523726/usrep523726.pdf))  
Area of Law: Article III; administrative law; ripeness

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a challenge to a federal forest plan is ripe before any site-specific logging decision or permit applies the plan to concrete land.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand with instructions to dismiss the substantive plan challenge without prejudice.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the plan authorizes no immediate logging, imposes no present legal hardship, and leaves later site-specific authorizations open to review.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Ripeness of the programmatic plan challenge

Holding and operative rule: A substantive challenge to a programmatic forest plan is unripe when the plan authorizes no immediate logging, imposes no present legal obligation or hardship, and preserves review of later site-specific authorizations.

Reasoning: The plan identifies management goals, areas potentially suitable for timber production, and probable harvesting methods, but it permits no tree to be cut. Before logging may occur, the Forest Service must identify a project, conduct further analysis, provide notice and participation, determine consistency with the plan, and issue a final site-specific authorization subject to administrative and judicial review. Withholding review therefore imposes no present legal sanction or necessary change in conduct. Later proceedings may modify or eliminate the anticipated project and will provide a concrete factual record. Immediate review would require adjudication of broad predictions concerning varied parcels and methods rather than the legal consequences of an actual agency decision. Newly asserted immediate-harm theories were not fairly presented and are not decided. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep523/usrep523726/usrep523726.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Abbott Laboratories v. Gardner*: followed; fitness for judicial decision and hardship from delayed review govern ripeness.  
*Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation*: applied; a nonself-executing programmatic plan ordinarily must be reviewed through concrete agency action.  
*FTC v. Standard Oil Co. of California*: applied; further agency action and factual development may make immediate review unnecessary or materially alter the dispute.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the substantive plan challenge without prejudice as unripe.  
Effect: The plan’s legality is not adjudicated. The plaintiffs may challenge a site-specific logging authorization, an immediately binding plan provision, or a properly preserved completed procedural violation when the independent requirements for review are satisfied.

### United States v. Thompson

Argued January 14, 1998 | Decided May 21, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces; judgment below: convictions and sentence affirmed, 47 M.J. 378 (C.A.A.F. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/caaf/96-1412-AF/96-1412-AF-1997-12-16.html))  
Area of Law: Military justice; marital privacy; court-martial procedure

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the constitutional protection for marital privacy bars an Article 125 conviction for a private sexual act between spouses when the record shows that the wife initiated the act to divert the accused during a violent assault.
2. Whether a military judge’s entry into the members’ closed deliberative session without the accused or counsel violated Article 39(b) of the Uniform Code of Military Justice, and whether the Government demonstrated that the error was nonprejudicial.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s fact-specific conclusion that the charged conduct was not freely chosen marital intimacy and its determination that the completely recorded, exclusively ministerial Article 39(b) violation caused no prejudice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Marital privacy during an ongoing assault

Holding and operative rule: Constitutional protection for consensual marital intimacy does not cover an act undertaken by one spouse to divert the other during an ongoing deadly assault.

Reasoning: Thompson’s wife acted after he had beaten her, pressed a loaded pistol to her forehead, attempted to fire it, and begun loading another magazine. Her purpose was to interrupt the immediate violence and survive the encounter. The charged conduct therefore did not involve freely chosen marital intimacy of the kind protected by the asserted privacy interest. The classification does not rest on judicial approval or disapproval of a particular sexual practice. It rests on the absence of voluntary intimate conduct in the proved circumstances. The Court does not decide whether Article 125 may constitutionally punish genuinely voluntary private sexual conduct between spouses or other adults. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/caaf/96-1412-AF/96-1412-AF-1997-12-16.html))

##### Question 2: Judicial entry into closed deliberations

Holding and operative rule: A military judge violates Article 39(b) by entering the members’ closed deliberative session without the accused and counsel, but the resulting presumption of prejudice may be rebutted when a complete record establishes that the contact was exclusively ministerial, concerned findings already reached, and affected no substantial right.

Reasoning: The judge and court reporter entered the deliberation room to answer questions about recording lesser-included offenses on the findings worksheet. The verbatim transcript established that the members had completed their substantive deliberations and sought no instruction concerning evidence, guilt, legal standards, or the merits. Every answer would have been proper in open court, and Thompson identifies no objection, clarification, argument, or participation that could have changed the result. The Government therefore rebutted the presumption of prejudice. The holding does not authorize substantive, unrecorded, private, or otherwise unreconstructable judicial contact with deliberating members. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/caaf/96-1412-AF/96-1412-AF-1997-12-16.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Griswold v. Connecticut*: declined to extend; protection for consensual marital intimacy does not encompass conduct undertaken during an ongoing deadly assault.  
*United States v. Allbee*: applied; unauthorized judicial entry into a closed deliberative session creates a rebuttable presumption of prejudice.  
Article 39(b), UCMJ: clarified; a fully recorded and exclusively ministerial exchange concerning findings already reached may be nonprejudicial.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces is affirmed.  
Effect: Thompson’s convictions and sentence remain in force. The Court does not decide the constitutionality of Article 125 as applied to genuinely voluntary private adult conduct, and it does not permit substantive or unrecorded judicial participation in court-martial deliberations.

**Source control:** In *Kiowa Tribe*, the court under review is corrected from the Oklahoma Supreme Court to the Oklahoma Court of Civil Appeals, First Division; the Oklahoma Supreme Court merely declined review. In *Duffy*, the packet’s description of the requested relief is reversed: Duffy appealed the summary judgment and sought reversal, while the judicial defendants sought affirmance. Neither correction alters Stone’s controlled position. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/523/751/))

The *Waste Management* flow-control question is separable and adjudicated. The diversion-fee question is paused because Stone’s approved disposition depends on the fee reasonably corresponding to diversion-related costs, while the stipulated lower-court record states that Metro offered no evidence of a specific burden for which the fee compensated and acknowledged a nondiscriminatory alternative. That conflict cannot be repaired without a revised and approved Section II. `OT_1997CHUNK7.md` ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))

*National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt* and *Judicial Council v. McBryde* are paused in full. The packet assigns merits decisions on June 8 and June 10, 1998, but the controlled Standing State records certiorari grants on June 22, 1998 and no later merits action. A plenary judgment cannot precede the grant authorizing merits review. `STANDING_STATE.md` `OT_1997CHUNK7.md`

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies, Inc.

Argued January 12, 1998 | Decided May 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Civil Appeals of Oklahoma, First Division; judgment below: judgment for Manufacturing Technologies affirmed and tribal immunity rejected for an off-reservation commercial contract, 951 P.2d 51 (Okla. Civ. App. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/523/751/))  
Area of Law: Federal Indian law; tribal sovereign immunity; commercial contracts

Questions Presented:
1. Whether tribal sovereign immunity extends to a tribe’s off-reservation commercial contract absent waiver or congressional abrogation.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Thomas, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Thomas, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of the existing federal tribal-immunity rule.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Tribal immunity for off-reservation commercial contracts

Holding and operative rule: An Indian tribe retains sovereign immunity from suit on an off-reservation commercial contract unless the tribe clearly waives immunity or Congress unequivocally abrogates it.

Reasoning: Tribal immunity attaches to the sovereign and has not been limited by the Court’s precedents to governmental conduct or transactions occurring on reservation land. States may apply nondiscriminatory substantive law to some off-reservation tribal activity, but authority to regulate conduct does not necessarily include judicial power to entertain an unconsented suit against the tribe. The commercial exception proposed by Manufacturing Technologies would require the Court to determine which transactions are governmental, proprietary, or sufficiently removed from tribal lands, while unsettling reliance on the existing immunity rule. Congress can adopt a comprehensive commercial exception and specify its scope, but it has not done so here. The promissory note expressly preserved the Tribe’s sovereign rights, supplied no clear waiver, and was governed by no statute abrogating immunity. The state courts therefore lacked authority to adjudicate the contract claim against the Tribe. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/523/751/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Thomas and Ginsburg: Tribal immunity should not extend to ordinary commercial activity conducted beyond reservation boundaries when neither statute nor treaty requires that result. The dissent would permit Oklahoma courts to apply their generally applicable contract law.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Three Affiliated Tribes of the Fort Berthold Reservation v. Wold Engineering, P.C.*: followed; a tribe is subject to suit only upon clear waiver or congressional authorization.  
*Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Citizen Band Potawatomi Indian Tribe*: reaffirmed; revision of established tribal immunity is principally for Congress.  
*Mescalero Apache Tribe v. Jones*: distinguished; state authority to apply substantive law to off-reservation activity does not eliminate tribal immunity from suit.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Oklahoma Court of Civil Appeals is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the contract action against the Kiowa Tribe on sovereign-immunity grounds.  
Effect: Manufacturing Technologies may not pursue the note directly against the Tribe without a clear waiver or congressional authorization. Claims against nonimmune parties, remedies against property not protected by immunity, and relief supported by a separate lawful waiver remain open.

---

### County of Sacramento v. Lewis

Argued December 9, 1997 | Decided May 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the officer reversed and the substantive-due-process claim remanded, 98 F.3d 434 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/523/833/))  
Area of Law: Substantive due process; emergency police conduct; high-speed pursuits

Questions Presented:
1. What culpability standard governs a substantive-due-process claim arising from a high-speed police pursuit that causes death.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | O’Connor | Supports reversal | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between an unintended collision and a seizure, and its context-specific purpose-to-harm standard for an emergency pursuit.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): Intentional means and the Fourth Amendment

Holding and operative rule: An unintended collision is not a Fourth Amendment seizure when the patrol car was not the means intentionally applied to stop or control the person. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The Fourth Amendment governs governmental termination of movement through means intentionally applied. The deputy intentionally pursued the motorcycle, but he did not intend to strike Lewis with the patrol car. The motorcycle fell independently, and the later collision occurred after the deputy braked unsuccessfully. The injury therefore did not result from the deliberate use of the patrol car as an instrument of restraint. Intent to pursue is not equivalent to intent to accomplish the particular termination of movement that produced the injury. Because no seizure occurred, the specific-source rule does not displace consideration of the materially distinct substantive-due-process claim. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/523/833/))

##### Question 1(b): Culpability during a rapidly evolving pursuit

Holding and operative rule: A high-speed pursuit requiring instantaneous operational judgment violates substantive due process only when the officer acts with a purpose to cause harm unrelated to legitimate apprehension. Deliberate indifference may remain sufficient in materially different settings allowing genuine deliberation. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Substantive due process reaches only executive conduct sufficiently arbitrary to shock the conscience, and the culpability necessary to meet that standard depends on the opportunity for reasoned choice. Deliberate indifference can be constitutionally sufficient when an official has time to assess a known danger and choose among alternatives. A rapidly unfolding pursuit is different. The officer must respond immediately to a suspect’s flight while balancing public safety, enforcement duties, and the risks of continued pursuit or withdrawal. In that setting, liability requires a purpose to injure, punish, terrorize, or otherwise cause harm for an objective unrelated to lawful apprehension. The record shows an instinctive effort to catch a motorcycle that refused to stop and no purpose to strike or injure Lewis. The federal constitutional claim therefore fails even if the pursuit was negligent, reckless, or contrary to sound police practice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/523/833/))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor: The conscience-shocking inquiry must remain objective and disciplined by history, precedent, and the operational necessities of law enforcement rather than a judge’s personal reaction.

Breyer: Courts retain flexibility to resolve an appropriate case through qualified immunity before deciding a difficult constitutional question. He nevertheless joins the Court’s constitutional holding here.

Stevens: The officer was entitled to qualified immunity because the governing law was not clearly established when the pursuit occurred. He would avoid deciding the broader substantive-due-process question.

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The claim fails because no historically grounded substantive right protects a fleeing suspect or passenger from deliberate or reckless police pursuit. These Justices reject the Court’s conscience-shocking methodology while agreeing with the judgment.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Brower v. County of Inyo*: applied; a seizure requires termination of movement through means intentionally applied.  
*Albright v. Oliver*: clarified; the specific-source rule does not foreclose substantive-due-process analysis when no seizure or other directly governing constitutional event occurred.  
*Daniels v. Williams*: reaffirmed; negligence is categorically beneath the substantive-due-process threshold.  
*Whitley v. Albers*: extended; purpose-to-harm culpability governs emergency police action requiring instantaneous judgment.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and judgment shall be entered for the defendants on the Fourth Amendment and substantive-due-process claims presented.  
Effect: The federal constitutional claims arising from the accidental collision are terminated. State tort claims, intentional ramming, roadblocks, planned operations permitting genuine deliberation, and conduct occurring after custody remain governed by their own rules.
