### Waste Management, Inc. of Tennessee v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville

Argued January 20, 1998 | Decided May 27, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: municipal flow-control provision invalidated, injunction against the diversion-fee provisions affirmed, and separate property claims remanded, 130 F.3d 731 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))  
Area of Law: Dormant Commerce Clause; municipal flow control; compensatory fees

Questions Presented:
1. Whether municipal flow-control provisions requiring locally generated waste to be delivered to a municipally owned disposal facility facially discriminate against interstate commerce.
2. Whether a disposal fee imposed only on waste sent away from the municipal disposal system is a discriminatory burden on interstate commerce or a valid compensatory charge for costs imposed by diverted waste.

#### Judgment

Reversed in part and remanded, 7-2, on the adjudicated component.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Kennedy. No vote is rendered on the paused component.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Municipal flow-control provision | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy | The Commerce Clause judgment against routing to the municipally owned facility is reversed. |
| Diversion-fee provision | Adjudication paused | No vote rendered | No vote rendered | No judgment or merits ruling is entered until the record conflict affecting Stone’s controlled disposition is resolved. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Municipal flow-control question |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Municipal flow-control question |
| Dissent | Stevens | Kennedy | Would affirm | Municipal flow-control question |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand on the municipal flow-control question. No vote is rendered on the diversion-fee question.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court concerning municipal flow control in full.  
Announced ground: A municipality may direct locally generated waste to a genuinely public facility providing a traditional public service when local private and out-of-state processors are treated alike. Stone announces no position concerning the paused fee.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Routing to a municipally owned disposal facility

Holding and operative rule: A municipal requirement directing locally generated waste to a genuinely municipally owned facility providing a traditional public service does not facially discriminate against interstate commerce when local private and out-of-state processors receive equal treatment.

Reasoning: *C & A Carbone* invalidated coercive favoritism toward a designated private processor. The current provision instead directs residential waste to a facility owned by Metro and used to perform the municipality’s traditional waste-disposal and energy-recovery functions. The relevant distinction is between the government’s public facility and all private competitors, not between in-state and out-of-state commerce. Waste Management’s Tennessee and Kentucky facilities, other local private processors, and out-of-state processors are excluded on the same terms. Public ownership does not create automatic immunity from dormant Commerce Clause review, and a municipality may not conceal private favoritism behind nominal title. On this stipulated record, however, Metro owns the facility, bears the public-service responsibility, and has not selected one private market participant for commercial advantage. The facial discrimination holding against the routing provision therefore cannot stand. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))

##### Question 2: Diversion fee

Not reached; adjudication is paused because Stone’s approved judgment depends on a supported relationship between the fee and diversion-related costs, while the stipulated record states that Metro offered no evidence of a specific compensatory burden and acknowledged a nondiscriminatory alternative. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The dormant Commerce Clause does not authorize invalidation of a municipality’s preference for its own public facility absent congressional legislation. These Justices concur in the flow-control judgment without joining the Court’s public-private discrimination framework.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Kennedy: The ordinance uses legal compulsion to foreclose private interstate competition and reserve the local waste stream for Metro’s chosen facility. Public ownership does not alter the discriminatory market effect, so these Justices would affirm the injunction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown*: distinguished; coercive favoritism toward a designated private processor differs from routing to a genuinely public facility performing a traditional municipal service.  
*SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown*: clarified; its reserved public-facility category permits evenhanded routing to a municipally owned service while preserving scrutiny of nominal public ownership used to benefit private enterprise.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed insofar as it invalidated the municipal flow-control provision, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with that ruling. No mandate issues concerning the diversion-fee provision.  
Effect: Nashville may enforce the challenged public-facility routing requirement subject to independent issues outside the grant. The diversion fee remains without a Supreme Court disposition, and no takings question is decided.

### United States v. Lacy

Argued January 20, 1998 | Decided May 28, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: denial of suppression and conviction affirmed, 119 F.3d 742 (9th Cir. 1997). ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-9th-circuit/1089691.html))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; digital evidence; staleness and particularity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether information showing that a person downloaded digital contraband ten months before issuance of a warrant was too stale to establish probable cause, notwithstanding an agent’s assertion that collectors ordinarily retain such material.
2. Whether a warrant authorizing seizure and search of an entire computer system, more than 100 disks, and related records satisfied the Fourth Amendment’s particularity requirement or authorized an unconstitutional general search.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s context-specific treatment of staleness and its requirement that digital-warrant categories remain tied to the investigated offense.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Staleness of probable cause for durable digital files

Holding and operative rule: A ten-month interval does not defeat probable cause to search for downloaded digital contraband when the affidavit establishes actual acquisition, durable storage, and a supported likelihood that the material will be retained.

Reasoning: Staleness is not determined by elapsed time alone. It depends on the nature of the suspected conduct, the character of the property sought, the manner in which that property is stored, and the reasons for believing it remains at the place to be searched. The affidavit did not rest on attempted access, curiosity, or a subscription. BAMSE’s records and matching telephone information showed that Lacy actually downloaded multiple image files. Digital files and disks can persist without physical deterioration, and the affiant supplied experience-based reasons to believe possessors retain such material in secure home storage. The Court does not presume permanent retention or accept an unsupported collector profile standing alone. Actual acquisition, durable storage, and the supported retention inference together supplied a fair probability that the files remained present when the warrant issued. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-9th-circuit/1089691.html))

##### Question 2: Particularity of the computer warrant

Holding and operative rule: A warrant may authorize seizure and examination of identified computer equipment and storage media when probable cause extends to the system and the warrant confines the search to evidence and records connected to the specified offense.

Reasoning: The Fourth Amendment requires as much specificity as the investigation reasonably permits; it does not require officers to identify in advance the precise disk or hardware location in which a digital file is stored when that information is unavailable. The affidavit established probable cause that Lacy’s computer system contained the downloaded images but could not determine whether they were on the hard drive or among more than 100 disks. The warrant tied the search to the identified equipment, child-exploitation evidence, BAMSE records, and related transaction or access material. Those objective limits distinguish the warrant from one authorizing examination of every personal file for any possible offense. The holding concerns facial particularity. It does not approve indiscriminate examination of unrelated data or decide an unpreserved challenge to the manner of execution. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-9th-circuit/1089691.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Sgro v. United States*: applied; staleness depends on the temporal relationship among the offense, the property sought, and the supported likelihood of continued possession.  
*Andresen v. Maryland*: applied; offense-linked categories can satisfy particularity even when numerous records must be examined to identify responsive evidence.  
*Stanford v. Texas*: distinguished; the warrant did not authorize a general exploration of unrelated expression or personal information.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Lacy’s conviction survives the stale-probable-cause and facial-particularity challenges. Warrants lacking an offense limitation, relying only on generalized profiles, or authorizing indiscriminate examination of unrelated data remain outside the judgment.

---

### Federal Election Commission v. Akins

Argued January 14, 1998 | Decided June 1, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: voters held to possess standing and the Commission’s dismissal remanded for review of its political-committee interpretation, 101 F.3d 731 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (en banc). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/11/))  
Area of Law: Article III; federal elections; administrative review

Questions Presented:
1. Whether voters denied information required by the Federal Election Campaign Act have Article III standing and whether the FEC’s dismissal of their complaint is reviewable.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Voters denied electoral information FECA specifically requires suffer a concrete injury, and intervening agency rules require first-instance reconsideration before judicial resolution of the disputed political-committee construction.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): Informational injury and reviewability

Holding and operative rule: Voters allege concrete Article III injury when they are denied electoral information that FECA specifically identifies for disclosure, even though many voters share the same statutory entitlement. FECA authorizes judicial review of the Commission’s dismissal.

Reasoning: The voters do not assert only a generalized interest in lawful administration. They seek contributor and expenditure information that FECA requires political committees to report and that bears directly on evaluating candidates, supporters, and political influence. The alleged denial is therefore a concrete informational injury of the kind the statute addresses. Its broad distribution does not make it abstract. The Commission’s dismissal caused the continued nondisclosure, and a lawful reconsideration could produce the requested reports, satisfying causation and redressability. FECA also gives an aggrieved complainant a specific route to challenge dismissal, displacing the ordinary presumption that an agency’s refusal to enforce is unreviewable. Whether the voters ultimately receive information depends on the statutory classification reached after reconsideration. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/11/))

##### Question 1(b): Intervening membership rules and the major-purpose question

Not reached; intervening FEC membership rules may change whether the communications count as expenditures and therefore whether the major-purpose issue remains material. The Commission must apply those rules in the first instance. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/11/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: The voters assert a generalized interest in information concerning another organization rather than invasion of a personal legal right sufficient for Article III. These Justices would dismiss without requiring reconsideration of the Commission’s statutory construction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Public Citizen v. Department of Justice*: applied; denial of information a statute specifically makes public can constitute concrete injury.  
*Richardson v. United States*: distinguished; a broad but specific statutory information entitlement differs from an undifferentiated demand that government obey the law.  
*Heckler v. Chaney*: distinguished; FECA expressly authorizes review of the Commission’s dismissal of a complaint.  
*Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment*: applied; Article III standing is established before the statutory merits are addressed.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded with instructions that the complaint return to the FEC for reconsideration under the intervening membership rules and a reasoned application of FECA.  
Effect: The voters possess standing and may obtain judicial review of the resulting agency action. The Court does not decide that AIPAC is a political committee, invalidate every major-purpose inquiry, or guarantee that disclosure will follow.

### City of Florence v. Chipman

Argued February 23, 1998 | Decided June 1, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: municipal summary judgment affirmed, but dismissal of substantive-due-process and equal-protection claims against individual officers reversed and those claims remanded, 126 F.3d 856 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/126/856/497900/))  
Area of Law: Substantive due process; state-created danger; equal protection; qualified immunity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether officers violate substantive due process when they threaten an incapacitated woman with arrest, physically place her in the vehicle of a visibly intoxicated and allegedly abusive companion, and act with deliberate indifference to the resulting danger.
2. Whether arresting or prosecuting a person solely because officers perceive her to be homosexual violates the Equal Protection Clause even when probable cause otherwise supports the arrest.
3. Whether those constitutional prohibitions were sufficiently established at the time to defeat the individual officers’ qualified-immunity defense.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the remand for further proceedings.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Officers who deliberately use police authority to force an incapacitated person into a known immediate danger may be liable under substantive due process, and probable cause does not excuse enforcement selected solely because of perceived sexual orientation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Affirmative use of police authority to create danger

Holding and operative rule: An officer violates substantive due process by knowingly using police authority to force an incapacitated person into an immediate and grave danger, when the officer has time to deliberate and acts with deliberate indifference to the known risk.

Reasoning: The claim does not rest on a general governmental duty to protect a person from private violence. On the plaintiff-supported facts, the officers threatened Black with arrest, removed the safer means of departure, lifted or assisted her from Stemler’s vehicle, and physically placed her in Kritis’s truck. They allegedly knew that Kritis was visibly intoxicated, had assaulted Black, had threatened her, and had pursued the women dangerously. The conduct therefore increased Black’s vulnerability through an affirmative exercise of state authority. Unlike the instantaneous pursuit in *County of Sacramento v. Lewis*, the roadside encounter allowed time to gather information, test Kritis, preserve separation, or choose another disposition. Deliberate indifference to the known danger can consequently satisfy the conscience-shocking threshold. Knowledge, participation, causation, and damages remain to be proved defendant by defendant. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/126/856/497900/))

##### Question 2: Selective enforcement based on perceived sexual orientation

Holding and operative rule: Probable cause does not excuse an enforcement decision made solely because officers perceive the person to be homosexual. Selective enforcement for that reason violates equal protection when similarly situated persons outside the classification are treated differently and no legitimate enforcement purpose supports the distinction.

Reasoning: Probable cause defeats the separate Fourth Amendment false-arrest theory, but it does not authorize intentionally discriminatory enforcement. Stemler alleges that officers repeatedly emphasized her perceived sexual orientation, credited Kritis’s account for that reason, arrested her while leaving a more intoxicated and comparably situated Kritis unarrested, and pursued prosecution from hostility toward the perceived status. Equal protection has long prohibited selective application of neutral law through an arbitrary classification. The current simulated equality decisions additionally reject status-based hostility as a legitimate governmental objective. The Court need not decide the complete scrutiny framework for every sexual-orientation classification. An arrest selected solely through animus fails even the minimum constitutional requirement of a legitimate enforcement purpose. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/126/856/497900/))

##### Question 3: Qualified immunity and defendant-specific participation

Holding and operative rule: On the plaintiff-supported facts, officers who knew the material danger and participated in forcing Black into it, or who selected enforcement solely because of Stemler’s perceived sexual orientation, are not entitled to qualified immunity.

Reasoning: By February 1994, settled law prohibited officials from deliberately exposing a person under their immediate control to a known grave danger and prohibited selective enforcement of neutral laws through an arbitrary group classification. No materially identical earlier case was necessary to tell an officer that he could not force an incapacitated woman into the vehicle of a visibly intoxicated alleged abuser, or arrest one similarly situated driver solely because officers believed she was a lesbian. The appellate court may resolve those legal propositions on the plaintiff-supported facts, but it may not decide which officer possessed the necessary knowledge, issued the command, performed the physical placement, shared the discriminatory purpose, or caused the resulting injury. Those evidentiary disputes remain for further proceedings. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/126/856/497900/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The Due Process Clause creates no sufficiently established state-created-danger rule for this roadside encounter, and the officers should receive qualified immunity even if their conduct was reckless. The equal-protection claim likewise should not proceed absent a classification receiving heightened scrutiny or proof negating every nondiscriminatory enforcement reason.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*County of Sacramento v. Lewis*: distinguished; deliberate indifference may be conscience shocking when officials have a genuine opportunity to deliberate and affirmatively use state authority to expose a person to a known danger.  
*Pinder v. Johnson*: followed; state-created danger requires affirmative governmental conduct, known or obvious risk, sufficient culpability, and causation rather than a mere failure to protect.  
*Whren v. United States*: applied; objective probable cause does not foreclose a distinct equal-protection claim for intentionally selective enforcement.  
*Romer v. Evans*: applied; hostility toward perceived sexual orientation supplies no legitimate governmental purpose.  
*Johnson v. Jones*: applied; interlocutory qualified-immunity review does not authorize appellate resolution of disputed knowledge, participation, purpose, or causation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it permits the substantive-due-process and equal-protection claims against participating individual officers to proceed, and the case is remanded for defendant-specific proceedings.  
Effect: The estate and Stemler may attempt to prove knowledge, affirmative participation, discriminatory purpose, causation, and damages. Final individual liability and the lower court’s separate municipal-liability disposition remain unaffected by this judgment.

### Duffy v. Wolle

Argued February 23, 1998 | Decided June 3, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the three-judge hiring panel affirmed, 123 F.3d 1026 (8th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/123/1026/624823/))  
Area of Law: Fifth Amendment equal protection; Bivens remedies; judicial immunity; discovery

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a federal judicial employee may maintain a constitutional damages action against federal judges for alleged sex discrimination in administrative hiring, notwithstanding the existence of internal judicial equal-employment procedures.
2. Whether federal judges possess absolute judicial immunity from damages for administrative employment and hiring decisions.
3. Whether summary judgment was proper before meaningful discovery where the plaintiff established a prima facie case and offered evidence of sex-conscious recruitment and allegedly pretextual explanations for selecting a female applicant.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete separate writing |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: The existing *Davis* damages action is not displaced by a nonstatutory internal procedure, administrative hiring receives no absolute judicial immunity, and targeted discovery is required before motive and pretext are resolved.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Existing constitutional damages action and internal procedures

Holding and operative rule: A federal judicial employee may maintain the individual-capacity constitutional damages action recognized in *Davis v. Passman* for intentional sex discrimination in employment. A court-created internal equal-employment procedure does not displace that existing action absent congressional authorization.

Reasoning: *Davis* already recognizes an individual-officer damages action for sex discrimination by a federal employer. This case therefore does not require creation of a new constitutional right or extension of the remedy to a new substantive setting. Congress may adopt a comprehensive alternative scheme and make it exclusive, as the analysis in *Bush v. Lucas* illustrates. The Southern District’s EEO plan, however, was established administratively under Judicial Conference direction and supplies no indication that Congress intended it to replace the existing damages action. Availability of that process may bear on relief, mitigation, or case management, but it cannot itself extinguish the remedy. Duffy must still prove intentional discrimination, personal participation, causation, damages, and the absence of a lawful defense. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/123/1026/624823/))

##### Question 2: Absolute immunity for administrative hiring

Holding and operative rule: Federal judges do not possess absolute judicial immunity for an administrative employment or hiring decision. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Absolute immunity follows the function performed, not the title of the official. Adjudicating disputes, issuing judgments, and performing acts integrally connected to the resolution of cases are judicial functions. Selecting a chief probation officer is an important act of judicial administration, but it does not resolve a dispute between parties invoking judicial power. *Forrester v. White* therefore controls. The fact that judges rather than executive administrators made the hiring decision does not transform it into adjudication. The panel may invoke qualified immunity and ordinary defenses, but not absolute immunity from the claim. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/123/1026/624823/))

##### Question 3: Discovery concerning intent and pretext

Holding and operative rule: Summary judgment on discriminatory intent was premature when Duffy made a prima facie showing, identified evidence bearing on sex-conscious recruitment and comparative qualifications, and was denied focused discovery into the decisionmakers’ stated reasons.

Reasoning: Duffy alleged that a qualified male applicant was rejected in favor of a woman after statements concerning recruitment of women, and he disputed the panel’s comparative explanations. The panel responded through affidavits addressing qualifications, experience, and interview performance. The court then accepted those affidavits without permitting the depositions and targeted discovery requested under then-Rule 56(f). Qualified immunity protects officials from unnecessary litigation, but it does not authorize adjudication of a disputed motive claim solely on the defendants’ untested account. The district court may confine discovery to the decisionmakers’ participation, the criteria actually used, comparative qualifications, the alleged recruitment statements, and pretext. The Court does not find discrimination or determine the judges’ ultimate entitlement to qualified immunity. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/123/1026/624823/))

#### Concurrences

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: These Justices agree that administrative hiring is not protected by absolute judicial immunity. They would treat the internal judicial employment system and separation-of-powers concerns as sufficient reason not to permit the damages action and would affirm the summary judgment without compelled discovery.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: The dissenting portion concludes that an internal judicial remedy, institutional independence, and the burdens of motive discovery counsel against extending personal damages litigation in this setting. These Justices would leave the judgment for the hiring panel in place.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Davis v. Passman*: followed; an individual-capacity constitutional damages action remains available for intentional sex discrimination by a federal employer.  
*Bush v. Lucas*: distinguished; a nonstatutory internal process does not displace an existing constitutional damages action without congressional authorization.  
*Forrester v. White*: followed; administrative employment decisions are not judicial acts receiving absolute immunity.  
*Crawford-El v. Britton*: applied; motive-based constitutional claims use ordinary proof and disciplined discovery rather than a special merits barrier.  
*FDIC v. Meyer*: followed; constitutional damages remain directed to responsible federal officers rather than the employing institution.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to deny absolute judicial immunity and permit appropriately limited discovery before resolving intent, pretext, personal participation, damages, or qualified immunity.  
Effect: Duffy may pursue the existing individual-capacity claim. He receives no appointment, liability finding, damages award, or final immunity ruling from this judgment.

### United States v. Bestfoods

Argued March 24, 1998 | Decided June 8, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: the district court’s parent-corporation liability ruling reversed under a standard focusing on control of the subsidiary, 113 F.3d 572 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/51/))  
Area of Law: Environmental law; corporate separateness; CERCLA operator liability

Questions Presented:
1. When a corporate parent is derivatively or directly liable as an owner or operator of a polluting facility under CERCLA.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Corporate separateness governs derivative liability, while CERCLA independently reaches a parent corporation’s own pollution-related operation of the facility.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): Derivative liability for a subsidiary’s obligations

Holding and operative rule: A parent corporation is derivatively liable for a subsidiary’s CERCLA obligations only when ordinary veil-piercing principles permit attribution of the subsidiary’s liability to the parent.

Reasoning: CERCLA imposes broad liability on persons who own or operate a polluting facility, but it does not expressly abolish the settled corporate-law distinction between parent and subsidiary. Ownership of all subsidiary stock, appointment of directors, financial oversight, adoption of general policies, and monitoring of performance are ordinary incidents of the corporate form. Those facts do not alone make the parent liable for the subsidiary’s obligations. Derivative liability remains available where the applicable corporate law permits disregard of separateness because the subsidiary is a sham, alter ego, instrument of fraud, or otherwise satisfies the governing veil-piercing rule. The lower courts must assess that route under ordinary corporate law rather than a CERCLA-specific presumption based on the parent’s capacity to control the subsidiary. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/51/))

##### Question 1(b): Direct operator liability for the parent’s own conduct

Holding and operative rule: A parent corporation is directly liable as a CERCLA operator when the parent itself manages, directs, or conducts facility operations specifically related to pollution, hazardous-waste disposal, leakage, or environmental compliance.

Reasoning: Respect for corporate separateness does not immunize the parent’s own statutory conduct. CERCLA directly reaches any person that operates the facility, regardless of whether that person also owns the facility or stands in a parent-subsidiary relationship to its owner. The inquiry therefore concerns the parent’s control of the polluting facility, not merely its control of the subsidiary as a corporation. Shared officers and directors must be evaluated under the ordinary presumption that they act for the subsidiary when performing subsidiary functions. A parent agent who independently directs waste disposal, pollution-control systems, environmental compliance, or comparable facility operations may place the parent within the statute. The present record contains evidence requiring reconsideration under that standard, but the Court makes no ultimate liability finding. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/51/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Corporate separateness under federal statutes: reaffirmed; a statute does not silently impose derivative parent liability merely from ownership or ordinary oversight.  
CERCLA § 107(a)(2): clarified; direct operator liability turns on management, direction, or conduct of pollution-related facility operations rather than general control of the subsidiary.  
Ordinary veil-piercing law: applied; derivative liability remains available when its independent requirements are satisfied.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for separate consideration of ordinary veil piercing and the parent corporation’s own pollution-related operation of the facility.  
Effect: The parent is neither held liable nor exonerated by this judgment. Ordinary investor oversight and dual officeholding are not enough by themselves, while direct management of waste disposal or environmental operations may support liability without veil piercing.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation

Argued January 13, 1998 | Decided June 15, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: district-court summary judgment for the Texas IOLTA officials reversed after the interest was held to be client property, and the case remanded for the remaining takings questions, 94 F.3d 996 (5th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524156/usrep524156.pdf))  
Area of Law: Property; Takings Clause; IOLTA interest

Questions Presented:
1. Whether interest generated by client funds placed in a mandatory IOLTA account is the private property of the client for Takings Clause purposes.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would vacate and remand | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Texas law assigns ownership of the principal to the client, and the settled rule that interest follows principal assigns the interest generated by that money to the same owner. Pooling, account costs, public use, and the inability to realize positive net interest individually bear on the later taking and compensation questions, not on the antecedent identification of the property.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Ownership of interest generated by client funds

Holding and operative rule: Interest actually generated by client funds in an IOLTA account is the private property of the owner of the principal unless a valid background property rule assigns it elsewhere. The holding determines ownership only and does not decide whether a compensable taking occurred.

Reasoning: Property interests protected by the Takings Clause are identified through independent sources such as state law and settled background property principles. Texas recognizes the clients’ ownership of the principal held in attorney trust accounts and supplies no rule transferring ownership of the interest produced by that principal. The established rule that interest follows principal therefore governs. Texas may create exceptions grounded in traditional property law, but the IOLTA program’s public purpose and administrative structure do not themselves establish such an exception. The relevant funds can generate gross interest when pooled; the fact that an individual client could not earn positive net interest after banking, accounting, and tax costs does not erase title to the increment that actually exists. Economic value, governmental use, and the measure of any loss remain relevant to the separate taking and just-compensation inquiries. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524156/usrep524156.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Property, taking, and compensation should be evaluated together because the asserted interest exists only within a mandatory pooling arrangement and has no realizable net value to an individual client. He would vacate and remand rather than issue a freestanding property ruling detached from its constitutional consequence.

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The program and governing banking rules create the only circumstance in which the disputed interest can exist, while denying the client any lawful route to obtain it individually. He would hold that the increment is not the client’s property on this record.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Board of Regents v. Roth*: applied; state law and background property principles identify the asserted interest before federal takings consequences are considered.  
*Webb’s Fabulous Pharmacies, Inc. v. Beckwith*: followed; interest generated by private principal ordinarily belongs to the principal’s owner.  
*Loretto v. Teleprompter Manhattan CATV Corp.*: applied; the absence of positive net economic value does not by itself eliminate property status.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed, and the case shall proceed under its existing remand with the interest treated as the clients’ property.  
Effect: The lower courts must separately determine standing, whether Texas has taken the property, whether just compensation exceeds zero, and what relief is lawfully available. The Court does not invalidate the IOLTA program or award compensation.

### Pennsylvania Department of Corrections v. Yeskey

Argued April 28, 1998 | Decided June 15, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: dismissal for failure to state a claim reversed after Title II of the ADA was held applicable to state prisons and prison programs, 118 F.3d 168 (3d Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524206/usrep524206.pdf))  
Area of Law: Disability discrimination; statutory coverage; prisons

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Title II of the Americans with Disabilities Act applies to state prisons and prison programs.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the enacted definitions include state correctional departments and their programs without a custodial exception.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Title II coverage of state prisons

Holding and operative rule: A state correctional department is a public entity under Title II, and its prison services, programs, and activities fall within the statute’s unqualified coverage. Involuntary custody does not create an exception.

Reasoning: Title II defines a public entity to include every state department, agency, and instrumentality and prohibits disability-based exclusion from its services, programs, and activities. A state prison falls directly within that definition. Prisons also administer medical services, educational and vocational programs, law libraries, recreational activities, and specialized programs such as the boot camp at issue. Some are voluntary and some are compulsory, but the statutory terms eligibility and participation do not require that every covered person enter the institution voluntarily. The text contains no prison exclusion, and the Court may not create one from generalized federalism concerns or assumptions about what Congress anticipated. Security, essential eligibility requirements, reasonable modification, direct threat, and fundamental alteration may affect application in a particular case. They do not erase statutory coverage at the threshold. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524206/usrep524206.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Gregory v. Ashcroft*: distinguished; Title II contains no ambiguity comparable to the statutory exception at issue there, and its language unmistakably includes state prisons.  
Title II of the Americans with Disabilities Act: clarified; public correctional institutions and their services, programs, and activities are covered without a categorical custodial exception.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed, and the action may proceed under Title II’s ordinary elements and defenses.  
Effect: Yeskey has established statutory coverage only. Disability, qualification, reasonable modification, direct-threat and security defenses, sovereign immunity, congressional power, damages, and remedy remain unresolved.

### Hohn v. United States

Argued March 3, 1998 | Decided June 15, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: application for a certificate of appealability denied, 99 F.3d 892 (8th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524236/usrep524236.pdf))  
Area of Law: Supreme Court jurisdiction; federal habeas; certificates of appealability

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Supreme Court has jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1254(1) to review a court of appeals’ denial of a certificate of appealability.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence | Souter | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Scalia | O’Connor, Thomas | Would dismiss for want of jurisdiction | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: concurs in the judgment and files a separate opinion.  
Announced ground: Without deciding whether § 1254(1) independently reaches certificate denials, he would use the common-law certiorari authority preserved by *House v. Mayo*. Ordinary appellate review is closed, the Government concedes that the case-ending certificate premise was erroneous, and no express restriction comparable to § 2244(b)(3)(E) bars the extraordinary route.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Statutory review of a certificate denial

Holding and operative rule: A certificate-of-appealability application adjudicated by a circuit judge or court-of-appeals panel is a case in the court of appeals reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1254(1). (joined by Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Hohn’s application was a judicial proceeding seeking relief from continued detention, was docketed and resolved by a three-judge panel, produced a published decision and dissent, and was followed by a judgment, mandate, and rehearing proceedings. The denial had immediate legal consequences because it ended the attempted appeal. The fact that § 2253 permits a circuit justice or judge to issue the certificate does not transform the judicial order into an administrative act outside the court of appeals. Court rules also permit panel and en banc treatment of certificate applications. Congress’s express prohibition of certiorari review for successive-application authorization decisions in § 2244(b)(3)(E), coupled with the absence of a comparable prohibition for certificate denials, confirms that the ordinary § 1254 route remains available here. The contrary statutory holding in *House v. Mayo* cannot be reconciled with the governing scheme and is overruled. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524236/usrep524236.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stone: *House v. Mayo* preserves a distinct common-law certiorari route sufficient to vacate this conceded, case-ending legal error. He would leave § 1254(1)’s application to certificate denials unresolved and would not make extraordinary-writ review routine.

Souter: The preserved common-law route could resolve the case, but he joins the Court’s statutory holding because it supplies a clearer and more administrable rule for future certificate proceedings.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas: Until a certificate issues, no appeal exists in the court of appeals and therefore no case is present there for review under § 1254(1). *House v. Mayo* and AEDPA’s threshold structure require dismissal, and the circumstances do not justify extraordinary common-law intervention.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*House v. Mayo*: overruled in part; its conclusion that § 1254(1) does not permit review of a certificate denial no longer governs.  
*Felker v. Turpin*: distinguished; § 2244(b)(3)(E) expressly bars review of successive-application authorization decisions but does not reach certificate-of-appealability denials.  
*Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal*: followed; AEDPA’s specialized restrictions apply according to their enacted triggers rather than through implied expansion.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The order denying Hohn a certificate of appealability is vacated, and the matter is remanded to the Eighth Circuit for reconsideration under the correct legal premise.  
Effect: The Supreme Court does not issue a certificate or adjudicate Hohn’s § 2255 claim. The Eighth Circuit must decide the certificate application anew in light of the Government’s concession and the governing certificate standard.

### Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District

Argued March 25, 1998 | Decided June 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the school district affirmed because no official with final corrective authority had actual notice of the teacher’s sexual relationship with the student, 106 F.3d 1223 (5th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524274/usrep524274.pdf))  
Area of Law: Title IX; institutional liability; teacher sexual abuse

Questions Presented:
1. What showing permits a private damages recovery against a school district under Title IX for a teacher’s sexual harassment of a student.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: Title IX does not impose automatic respondeat superior for every employee wrong, but recipient fault is not confined to actual notice received by one final corrective official. Damages may be available when school-conferred authority materially enables the discrimination and the recipient’s own actual knowledge or willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure causes it to continue.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Recipient fault for Title IX damages

Holding and operative rule: Title IX does not impose respondeat superior for every employee wrong. A funding recipient may nevertheless be liable in damages when school-conferred authority materially enables the discrimination and the recipient’s own actual knowledge or willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure causes the discrimination to continue; actual notice to an official with corrective authority is sufficient but not exclusive.

Reasoning: Title IX addresses discrimination under a federally funded education program, and damages in the implied action require fault attributable to the recipient rather than employee misconduct alone. Fair notice therefore excludes liability based solely on employment status or ordinary negligence. It does not permit a recipient to avoid responsibility by designing a reporting structure that prevents substantial warning information from reaching the official formally designated to act. When school authority materially enables the abuse, institutional personnel receive substantial warning information, and the recipient deliberately maintains or tolerates a reporting failure that prevents correction and causes the discrimination to continue, the resulting fault belongs to the recipient itself. The complaint to the principal concerning the teacher’s inappropriate conduct, the asserted internal warning, and the alleged absence of a functioning grievance route create a factual dispute under that rule. They do not establish abuse, recipient fault, causation, or damages as a matter of law. The case therefore requires further proceedings rather than judgment for either side. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524274/usrep524274.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Damages under the implied Title IX action require actual notice to an official with authority to institute corrective measures and deliberate indifference by that official. Regulatory deficiencies, constructive notice, and failures to transmit information cannot substitute for the recipient’s conscious decision not to remedy known discrimination.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Cannon v. University of Chicago*: clarified; the implied Title IX action requires discrimination and fault attributable to the funding recipient.  
*Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools*: clarified; damages remain available, but not through automatic respondeat superior.  
*Pennhurst State School & Hospital v. Halderman*: applied; recipient liability must rest on a source of institutional fault for which the funding condition supplied fair notice.  
*Meritor Savings Bank v. Vinson*: declined to extend as an automatic agency-liability rule for implied Title IX damages.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for application of the recipient-fault and causation standards stated here.  
Effect: Gebser has not established district liability or entitlement to damages. The lower courts must determine whether school-conferred authority materially enabled the discrimination, whether the district possessed actual knowledge or maintained a willfully blind and deliberately indifferent reporting failure, and whether that institutional fault caused the misconduct to continue.

### United States v. Bajakajian

Argued November 4, 1997 | Decided June 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: refusal to order forfeiture of the entire $357,144 affirmed, with a $15,000 forfeiture left in place because Bajakajian did not cross-appeal, 84 F.3d 334 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524321/usrep524321.pdf))  
Area of Law: Excessive Fines Clause; punitive forfeiture; proportionality

Questions Presented:
1. Whether forfeiture of the entire unreported currency transported out of the country is an excessive fine.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that forfeiture of the entire lawfully owned sum would be grossly disproportional to the reporting offense, while the existing $15,000 forfeiture remains outside the question decided.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Gross disproportionality of the full forfeiture

Holding and operative rule: A punitive forfeiture violates the Excessive Fines Clause when it is grossly disproportional to the gravity of the offense. Forfeiture of the entire $357,144 would be excessive on this record.

Reasoning: The forfeiture is punitive because it is imposed following conviction as an additional sanction for the reporting offense. Its constitutionality therefore turns on proportionality rather than on a categorical rule requiring or excluding instrumentality status. Bajakajian lawfully owned the currency and could lawfully transport it abroad had he submitted the required report. The district court found no connection to fraud, tax evasion, drug trafficking, money laundering, or another crime, and no loss of public funds occurred. The offense deprived the Government of required information but caused no injury corresponding to the entire amount sought. The authorized imprisonment and monetary penalties also reflected substantially lower culpability than confiscation of the full sum would imply. Legislative judgments receive substantial respect, and the Constitution does not require strict proportionality or a fixed numerical ratio. It nevertheless requires meaningful review, and the disparity here is gross. The Ninth Circuit’s broader instrumentality reasoning is not adopted. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524321/usrep524321.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor and Scalia: The entire currency shipment was involved in the offense and was the means by which the reporting requirement was violated. Congress could treat full forfeiture as a proportionate response to concealed international currency movement, and the Court gives insufficient weight to legislative judgment and the enforcement interests served by reporting laws.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Austin v. United States*: clarified; a punitive forfeiture is a fine and is tested for gross disproportionality to the gravity of the offense.  
*Solem v. Helm*: applied; gross disproportionality, rather than strict proportionality, supplies the constitutional measure.  
*Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch*: applied; the legal character and operation of the exaction determine whether it functions as punishment.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, and the Government may not obtain forfeiture of the entire $357,144 on this record.  
Effect: The existing $15,000 forfeiture remains in force because Bajakajian did not cross-appeal it. The judgment establishes no fixed forfeiture ratio and does not protect contraband, criminal proceeds, or property materially connected to fraud, trafficking, laundering, or comparable harm.

### Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott

Argued March 30, 1998 | Decided June 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania; judgment below: use of evidence obtained through an unconstitutional parole-officer search rejected and the parole revocation set aside, 548 Pa. 418, 698 A.2d 32 (1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524357/usrep524357.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; exclusionary rule; parole revocation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Fourth Amendment exclusionary rule applies in a parole-revocation hearing when parole officers themselves conduct the unconstitutional search.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s narrow application of exclusion where parole officers themselves conduct an unconstitutional search for use in the supervision and revocation process they administer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Exclusion in the searching agency’s revocation proceeding

Holding and operative rule: Evidence obtained through an unconstitutional search by parole officers for use in parole supervision and revocation must be excluded from the resulting revocation proceeding.

Reasoning: The Court assumes the search was unconstitutional and addresses only the remedy. The relevant institutional connection is direct: parole officers responsible for supervising Scott conducted the search, obtained the evidence, and supplied it for the revocation process administered by their own agency. Permitting use would allow those officers to obtain the practical equivalent of renewed punishment through evidence the Fourth Amendment forbade them to secure. Exclusion therefore provides meaningful deterrence at the point where the violation and the proposed governmental benefit coincide. Parole supervision remains flexible, and the State may impose valid search conditions, act on reasonable suspicion when governing law permits, use independently obtained evidence, and invoke ordinary good-faith, attenuation, independent-source, and inevitable-discovery doctrines. The decision does not impose exclusion whenever independent police evidence later becomes relevant to parole or transform revocation into a criminal trial. It applies to the same supervisory enforcement chain responsible for the unlawful search. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524357/usrep524357.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: The Fourth Amendment’s protection would be materially incomplete if the same government officials could use evidence obtained in direct violation of it to return the parolee to confinement. He joins the Court’s narrower institutional and deterrence analysis.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy: The exclusionary rule is a judicial remedy rather than an individual constitutional right, and its substantial costs in flexible parole proceedings exceed any marginal deterrent benefit. Criminal-trial exclusion and administrative discipline provide adequate incentives without suppressing reliable evidence at revocation.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Arizona v. Evans*: distinguished; the search and proposed use arise within the same supervisory enforcement chain rather than from an isolated error by personnel outside the officers’ law-enforcement function.  
*United States v. Leon*: applied; exclusion turns on whether it can meaningfully deter culpable governmental search conduct.  
*Morrissey v. Brewer*: applied; the flexibility of parole revocation does not eliminate Fourth Amendment limits or every remedy for their direct violation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania is affirmed, and the unlawfully obtained evidence shall remain excluded from Scott’s revocation proceeding.  
Effect: The Pennsylvania courts may determine the lawful consequence for the revocation judgment using the remaining admissible record. Valid parole-search conditions, independently obtained police evidence, good-faith reliance, attenuation, independent source, and inevitable discovery remain governed by their own requirements.

### Wisconsin Department of Corrections v. Schacht

Argued April 20, 1998 | Decided June 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: federal judgment vacated and the entire removed action ordered returned to state court because one claim was barred by the Eleventh Amendment, 116 F.3d 1151 (7th Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524381/usrep524381.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; removal; sovereign immunity

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the presence of a claim barred by the Eleventh Amendment destroys removal jurisdiction over an entire action containing otherwise removable claims.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete concurrence |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s separation of statutory removal jurisdiction from a claim-specific sovereign-immunity defense.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Removal when one claim encounters sovereign immunity

Holding and operative rule: A potential Eleventh Amendment bar affecting one or more claims does not destroy otherwise valid removal jurisdiction over the entire civil action. The federal court must resolve immunity and any remaining remand or supplemental-jurisdiction questions claim by claim.

Reasoning: Schacht’s complaint included federal claims that could have been filed originally in federal district court, so §§ 1331 and 1441 supplied statutory removal jurisdiction. The Eleventh Amendment does not operate like an unwaivable absence of subject-matter jurisdiction that automatically nullifies the entire case. It gives the State a defense that may bar particular claims and that the State may assert or waive. Section 1447(c) directs remand when the federal court lacks subject-matter jurisdiction over the case; it does not require return of an otherwise removable action merely because one claim cannot proceed against one defendant. The district court must therefore identify the claims and parties within federal jurisdiction, resolve the State’s immunity defense where asserted, and then apply the ordinary rules governing dismissal, remand, and supplemental jurisdiction. The Court does not decide whether Wisconsin waived immunity by removing the action or whether every remaining state-law claim should be retained. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524381/usrep524381.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy: The Court properly leaves open whether a State waives Eleventh Amendment immunity by voluntarily removing an action to federal court. That distinct issue should be resolved only in a case where it is fully presented and briefed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Chicago v. International College of Surgeons*: followed; statutory adjudicatory power is identified before claim-specific retention, immunity, or remand questions are resolved.  
*Pennhurst State School & Hospital v. Halderman*: clarified; sovereign immunity may bar particular claims without eliminating federal jurisdiction over an otherwise removable action.  
28 U.S.C. § 1447(c): clarified; a defect affecting one claim does not require remand of the entire case when federal subject-matter jurisdiction otherwise exists.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for claim-by-claim consideration of removal jurisdiction, sovereign immunity, supplemental jurisdiction, dismissal, and remand.  
Effect: The entire action may not be returned to state court solely because one claim encounters an Eleventh Amendment defense. The Court does not decide removal waiver, the validity of any claim against Wisconsin, or whether the district court should retain every remaining state-law claim.

---

### Swidler & Berlin v. United States

Argued June 8, 1998 | Decided June 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: order protecting a deceased client’s confidential consultation notes reversed and disclosure directed under a criminal-investigation need exception, 124 F.3d 230 (D.C. Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524399/usrep524399.pdf))  
Area of Law: Evidence; attorney-client privilege; grand-jury process

Questions Presented:
1. Whether attorney-client privilege survives the client’s death and yields to a posthumous criminal-investigation balancing test.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: The attorney-client privilege ordinarily survives the client’s death, and its effectiveness depends on a predictable boundary rather than a posthumous balancing of investigative importance. No recognized waiver or crime-fraud exception applies on this record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Posthumous attorney-client privilege

Holding and operative rule: The federal attorney-client privilege ordinarily survives the client’s death and does not yield to an ad hoc balancing test based on the asserted need for evidence in a later criminal investigation.

Reasoning: The privilege encourages full and frank communication by allowing a client to consult counsel without having to predict every later civil, criminal, estate, reputational, or family dispute in which the communication might be sought. That reliance does not necessarily end at death. Confidential advice may concern wills, property, family members, potential liability, or conduct affecting the client’s reputation and estate, and later disclosure may injure interests the client expected counsel to protect. A case-by-case criminal-need exception would make the protection uncertain when the advice is given because neither attorney nor client could know how a later court would assess the importance and availability of evidence. Federal privilege law already recognizes defined doctrines such as waiver and crime-fraud, and the Court does not enlarge or contract them here. The grand jury’s substantial investigatory authority does not permit displacement of a valid privilege merely because the client can no longer personally suffer prosecution. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524399/usrep524399.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: After the client’s death, a narrowly confined exception should permit disclosure when the evidence is unavailable elsewhere and is demonstrably important to a serious criminal investigation. The dissent would balance that compelling need against the reduced confidentiality interests presented by the particular communication.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Jaffee v. Redmond*: extended; a recognized confidential-communications privilege is not subjected to ad hoc evidentiary balancing whenever the information appears important.  
*Upjohn Co. v. United States*: reaffirmed; predictable confidentiality is essential to the attorney-client privilege’s function.  
*Hunt v. Blackburn*: followed; attorney-client privilege ordinarily survives the client’s death.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is reversed, and the district court’s order protecting the privileged consultation notes shall be reinstated.  
Effect: The grand jury may not obtain the notes through the rejected posthumous criminal-need exception. Work product, waiver, crime-fraud, the communication’s legal character, and any rights of a lawful successor remain governed by their own requirements.
