## Decisions and Dispositions

### Waste Management, Inc. of Tennessee v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville

Argued January 20, 1998 | Decided May 27, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below: municipal flow-control provisions invalidated, injunction against the diversion-fee provisions affirmed, and separate takings issues remanded, 130 F.3d 731 (6th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))  
Area of Law: Dormant Commerce Clause; municipal flow control; disposal fees

Questions Presented:
1. Whether municipal flow-control provisions requiring locally generated waste to be delivered to a municipally owned disposal facility facially discriminate against interstate commerce.
2. Whether a disposal fee imposed on waste sent away from the municipal disposal system is a discriminatory burden on interstate commerce or a valid compensatory charge for costs imposed by diverted waste.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Kennedy.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Stevens | Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand as to both challenged Commerce Clause provisions.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s conclusion that the ordinances distinguish Metro’s public disposal service from all private alternatives rather than distinguishing local from interstate commerce, and that the fee’s nondiscriminatory classification makes a compensatory-charge defense unnecessary.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Routing waste to a municipal facility

Holding and operative rule: A municipality does not facially discriminate against interstate commerce by directing locally generated waste to a genuinely municipally owned facility that performs a traditional public service, when local private processors and out-of-state processors receive equal treatment.

Reasoning: *C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown* invalidated legal favoritism toward a designated private processor. Metro’s ordinance instead directs specified local waste to a facility owned by the municipality and used to discharge Metro’s public responsibility for waste disposal and energy recovery. The relevant classification is therefore between the public facility and every private competitor. Waste Management’s Tennessee transfer station and landfills, its Kentucky landfill, and other private facilities are excluded on the same terms. Public ownership cannot be used as a nominal label to confer private commercial advantage, but the stipulated record does not establish such a device here. Because the ordinance does not distinguish according to interstate origin or destination, the Sixth Circuit’s facial-discrimination holding cannot stand. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))

##### Question 2: Uniform fee on diversion from the public system

Holding and operative rule: The uniform $8 fee does not facially discriminate against interstate commerce because liability turns on diversion from Metro’s public disposal system, not on whether the waste is sent to an in-state or out-of-state private facility.

Reasoning: The fee applies when Metro-generated waste is disposed of outside the municipal system. It reaches a private facility operating within Metro, a landfill elsewhere in Tennessee, and a landfill in Kentucky on the same basis. The exemption for Metro’s own facility corresponds to the public service from which the waste has been diverted; it is not an interstate-origin exemption. *Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality* requires a demanding compensatory-charge showing only after a charge has first been classified as discriminatory against interstate commerce. Metro’s failure to quantify a specific offsetting burden would defeat that alternative justification if it were necessary, but it cannot transform an otherwise destination-neutral fee into interstate discrimination. A higher fee for out-of-state disposal, an exemption for comparable local private diversion, or evidence that the fee operates as protection for local private commerce would present a different case. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/731/634609/))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The dormant Commerce Clause does not authorize invalidation of a municipality’s preference for its own public service absent congressional legislation. They concur in the judgment without joining the Court’s public-private discrimination framework.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Kennedy: Public ownership does not alter the ordinances’ practical foreclosure of private interstate competition. They would affirm the injunctions because Metro reserves the local waste stream for its own facility and financially penalizes every competing disposal route.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown*: distinguished; coercive favoritism toward a private enterprise differs from evenhanded routing to a genuinely public facility.  
*SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown*: clarified; a municipality may operate and finance its traditional public waste service when local private and out-of-state competitors are treated alike.  
*Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality*: clarified; compensatory-charge analysis follows, rather than precedes, classification of a charge as discriminatory against interstate commerce.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed as to the municipal flow-control and diversion-fee provisions, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with this opinion.  
Effect: Nashville may enforce both challenged provisions subject to any independent issue outside the grant. No ruling is made on the separate passenger-vehicle ordinance or any takings claim.

### National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt

Argued February 23, 1998 | Decided June 8, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment sustaining application of Endangered Species Act § 9 to the Delhi Sands Flower-Loving Fly affirmed, 130 F.3d 1041 (D.C. Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/1041/634655/))  
Area of Law: Commerce Clause; Endangered Species Act; federalism

Questions Presented:
1. Whether Congress may prohibit the purely intrastate taking of an endangered species found only within one State, not itself bought or sold, and not involved in a commercial transaction, based on the aggregate effects of species loss on interstate commerce.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.  
Announced ground: The Commerce Clause does not permit aggregation of the purely local and noncommercial taking of a species solely through generalized biodiversity, development, tourism, research, or possible future commercial effects.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Intrastate taking of a noncommercial species

Holding and operative rule: Congress may not apply Endangered Species Act § 9 to a purely intrastate taking of a species that is neither an article of commerce nor involved in a commercial transaction when the asserted federal nexus rests solely on aggregated species loss, possible future discoveries, or generalized effects on development and the national economy.

Reasoning: The Delhi Sands Flower-Loving Fly exists only within a limited area of California and is not bought, sold, transported, or otherwise used in an interstate commercial transaction. The construction projects surrounding the alleged taking are economic, but the Government may not substitute the commercial character of nearby development for a commerce connection in the regulated act itself without showing that the prohibition is necessary to an otherwise valid regulation of that market. *United States v. Lopez* rejects a chain of reasoning under which every local act becomes regulable because its cumulative environmental or economic consequences may affect commerce. *United States v. Hale* and *American Life League v. Reno* permit aggregation when the regulated conduct itself operates on a defined interstate market or transaction. The record supplies no comparable anchor here. The holding is as applied and does not disturb protection of species in interstate trade, species whose taking directly affects a commercial market, or prohibitions necessary to prevent evasion of a valid market regulation. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/130/1041/634655/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: The Endangered Species Act regulates a national problem through a comprehensive scheme, and commercial development that destroys protected habitat directly frustrates that scheme. They would permit Congress to aggregate the economic consequences of habitat destruction and affirm the judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Lopez*: applied; purely local noncommercial conduct cannot be aggregated through generalized economic consequences without a genuine commerce anchor.  
*United States v. Hale*: distinguished; the taking of the fly does not itself operate within a defined interstate product market.  
*Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon*: clarified; statutory coverage of habitat-related harm does not independently establish Congress’s constitutional authority for every application.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to enter appropriate declaratory and prospective relief against application of § 9 to the plaintiffs’ challenged intrastate activities on the supplied record.  
Effect: The Federal Government may not enforce the challenged take prohibition against these projects on the theories rejected here. The listing of the species and materially different applications of the Endangered Species Act remain unaffected.

---

### Judicial Council v. McBryde

Argued February 24, 1998 | Decided June 10, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: mandamus granted in substantial part and judicial-council reassignment orders limited, 117 F.3d 208 (5th Cir. 1997), under the lower-court caption *In re John H. McBryde*. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/117/208/534324/))  
Area of Law: Judicial administration; Article III; mandamus

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a court of appeals may use the All Writs Act to review and vacate a district chief judge’s implementation of a circuit judicial council’s reassignment order when the court lacks direct appellate jurisdiction over the judicial council itself.
2. Whether 28 U.S.C. § 332 authorizes a judicial council to reassign pending cases and invalidate or override an Article III judge’s findings as a response to alleged misconduct.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s distinction between unavailable direct review of the Judicial Council and lawful mandamus directed to district officers implementing its order, together with the statutory limit on punitive merits-based reassignment.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Mandamus to implementing district officers

Holding and operative rule: Although a court of appeals lacks direct appellate jurisdiction over a circuit judicial council, it may issue mandamus to a district court or chief district judge whose implementation of the council’s directive is subject to the court of appeals’ ordinary supervisory jurisdiction.

Reasoning: The Council’s order did not transfer the pending cases by its own force. Section 332 requires district judicial officers to implement council directives, and the operative reassignment orders therefore issued within the Northern District of Texas. The Fifth Circuit possessed appellate and mandamus authority over that district court and could determine whether the implementing orders exceeded lawful authority. This route does not convert the Judicial Council into an inferior court directly reviewable by the circuit, nor does it authorize a collateral attack on every administrative instruction. It permits relief only where district officers have entered or are enforcing an order that lies within the court of appeals’ established supervisory jurisdiction and the demanding requirements for mandamus are otherwise met. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/117/208/534324/))

##### Question 2: Statutory limits on disciplinary reassignment

Holding and operative rule: Section 332 authorizes neutral administration of judicial business but does not permit a judicial council to punish an Article III judge by removing pending cases because it disagrees with the judge’s merits-related conduct, nullifying findings, or substituting administrative supervision for appellate review.

Reasoning: Effective administration includes prospective assignment rules, docket equalization, emergency management, responses to incapacity, enforcement of recusal law, and implementation of remedies expressly authorized by the judicial-misconduct statute. It does not include review-like power to vacate judicial findings or transfer identified pending matters as punishment. Congress separately provided a disciplinary structure in § 372 and specified available sanctions, including limitations on future assignments in appropriate circumstances. Reading § 332’s general administrative language to authorize broader punitive control would make those limits largely unnecessary and would permit an administrative body to interfere with adjudication without the protections of appeal, recusal, or statutory discipline. The Satz and Torres reassignments therefore could not be sustained on the asserted punitive and merits-based rationale. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/117/208/534324/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chandler v. Judicial Council*: clarified; judicial councils possess substantial administrative authority but no unbounded power to control the merits of pending Article III adjudications.  
The All Writs Act: established; mandamus may run to implementing district officers when the court of appeals possesses supervisory jurisdiction over their operative orders.  
28 U.S.C. § 332: clarified; general administrative authority does not authorize punitive merits-based reassignment or nullification of judicial findings.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed, and its writ governing the district officers’ implementation of the challenged reassignments shall remain in force.  
Effect: The affected pending cases must be restored or administered consistently with the Fifth Circuit’s writ. Neutral prospective assignments, lawful recusal, appellate remand, emergency administration, and discipline expressly authorized by statute remain available.

---

### Zeran v. America Online, Inc.

Argued February 24, 1998 | Decided June 15, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: judgment on the pleadings for America Online affirmed, 129 F.3d 327 (4th Cir. 1997). ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/129/f3d/327/zeran-v-america-online-incorporated))  
Area of Law: Statutory interpretation; online intermediaries; defamation

Questions Presented:
1. Whether 47 U.S.C. § 230(c)(1) bars notice-based distributor liability against an interactive computer service for failing to remove, retract, investigate, or screen defamatory material created by a third party.
2. Whether § 230 applies to a suit filed after the statute’s enactment when the alleged service-provider conduct occurred before enactment.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He announces no ground beyond the Court’s classification of distributor liability as a form of publisher liability and its enforcement of Congress’s express temporal command.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Notice-based distributor liability

Holding and operative rule: Notice-based distributor liability treats an interactive computer service as the publisher of information supplied by another information-content provider and is barred by § 230(c)(1).

Reasoning: Distributor liability uses a knowledge requirement different from the rule governing an original publisher, but both forms of liability arise from participation in publication. Once notified, a service provider must decide whether to retain, remove, edit, investigate, screen, or retract the third party’s material. A tort duty based on those choices necessarily treats the service as the publisher of the information. Zeran’s negligence labels do not change the legal character of the asserted duties, and notice does not make AOL the creator or developer of the defamatory postings. The rule does not protect the original author, content created or materially developed by the service, conduct outside the statutory definitions, or an independently enforceable promise whose duty does not depend on publisher status. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/129/f3d/327/zeran-v-america-online-incorporated))

##### Question 2: Actions filed after § 230’s effective date

Holding and operative rule: Then-§ 230(d)(3) governs actions brought and liabilities imposed after its effective date, including claims based on earlier service-provider conduct.

Reasoning: Congress directed that no inconsistent cause of action may be brought and no inconsistent liability may be imposed. Zeran filed after the statute became effective and sought a later judgment imposing the very liability the provision addresses. *Landgraf v. USI Film Products* therefore ends the inquiry at its first step because Congress expressly prescribed the statute’s temporal reach. Applying that command does not infer a new consequence for earlier conduct; it governs the later filing and imposition of liability. *Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer* is distinguishable because the amendment there lacked comparable language governing the institution of actions and the entry of liability. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/129/f3d/327/zeran-v-america-online-incorporated))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

47 U.S.C. § 230(c)(1): established; publisher treatment includes notice-based distributor duties concerning third-party information.  
*Landgraf v. USI Film Products*: followed; an express temporal command controls before any presumption against retroactivity is considered.  
*Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer*: distinguished; § 230 directly governs when inconsistent causes may be brought and liability imposed.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Zeran’s notice-based claims against AOL remain dismissed. Claims against the original author, claims based on content AOL itself created or materially developed, and duties genuinely independent of publisher status remain governed by their own elements.

---

### Pearson v. Hines

Argued February 24, 1998 | Decided June 22, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: jury verdict against Officer Pearson for filing a false disciplinary charge in retaliation for protected grievance activity affirmed, 108 F.3d 265 (9th Cir. 1997), under the lower-court caption *Hines v. Gomez*. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/108/f3d/265/hines-v-gomez))  
Area of Law: First Amendment retaliation; prison discipline; § 1983

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a prison official violates the First Amendment by filing a knowingly false disciplinary charge in retaliation for an inmate’s filing or threatened filing of a grievance.
2. Whether the existence of some evidence supporting the resulting disciplinary finding defeats the retaliation claim, and whether the inmate must prove an additional injury or actual chilling beyond the disciplinary action itself.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s conclusion that intentional fabrication motivated by protected grievance activity is actionable retaliation, and that the disciplinary board’s evidentiary sufficiency does not resolve the accusing officer’s motive.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retaliatory fabrication of a disciplinary charge

Holding and operative rule: A prison official violates the First Amendment by knowingly filing a materially false disciplinary charge because an inmate filed or threatened to file a protected, nonfrivolous grievance, unless the official establishes that the same action would have been taken for an independently lawful reason.

Reasoning: Prisoners retain protection for nonfrivolous use of the grievance and court-access systems, subject to legitimate rules of prison administration. The jury heard evidence that Hines was known for filing grievances, told Pearson that he would grieve the officer’s conduct, and was then accused by Pearson of a rule violation the jury found knowingly false. Deliberate fabrication serves no legitimate penological interest. Under *Mt. Healthy*, Hines had to prove that the protected activity substantially motivated Pearson’s accusation and caused the adverse action; Pearson remained free to prove a lawful same-decision ground. The circumstantial evidence, credibility findings, and rejection of Pearson’s asserted account permitted the verdict. *Crawford-El v. Britton* confirms that ordinary proof rules govern motive rather than a judicially created heightened burden. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/108/f3d/265/hines-v-gomez))

##### Question 2: Disciplinary evidence and adverse action

Holding and operative rule: The existence of some evidence supporting a disciplinary board’s ultimate finding does not defeat a separate retaliation claim against an officer accused of intentionally filing a false charge. Actual disciplinary confinement or loss of privileges is adverse action without an additional showing that protected activity was subjectively chilled.

Reasoning: *Superintendent v. Hill* establishes the minimum evidence necessary for a prison disciplinary judgment to satisfy procedural due process. It does not confer immunity on the official who initiated the proceeding for an unconstitutional retaliatory purpose. A disciplinary board may possess some evidence independent of the accusation while the initial charge remains intentionally false and retaliatory. Hines also suffered ten days of confinement and a loss of privileges, consequences sufficient to deter a person of ordinary firmness from protected grievance activity. Requiring separate proof that Hines actually abandoned future grievances would reward unusual persistence and leave completed retaliation without a remedy. On the jury-supported facts, no reasonable officer could understand deliberate fabrication for the purpose of punishing a grievance as lawful. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/108/f3d/265/hines-v-gomez))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Prison disciplinary proceedings require substantial institutional deference, and an independently supported finding should ordinarily break the causal chain from an allegedly false accusation. They would also find the asserted right insufficiently established at the necessary level of specificity and reverse the damages judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Johnson v. Rodriguez*: followed; prison officials may not impose a discretionary adverse consequence because of protected, nonfrivolous legal activity.  
*Crawford-El v. Britton*: applied; ordinary civil proof rules govern unconstitutional-motive claims.  
*Superintendent v. Hill*: distinguished; its some-evidence rule governs procedural sufficiency of the disciplinary judgment, not retaliatory motive in initiating a false charge.  
*Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*: followed; retaliatory causation and the lawful same-decision defense remain distinct.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The damages judgment against Pearson remains in force. The Court does not impose liability for a mistaken but good-faith accusation, a charge causing no material consequence, or the conduct of any separate defendant.

---

### Clinton v. City of New York

Argued April 27, 1998 | Decided June 25, 1998  
On appeal from the United States District Court for the District of Columbia; judgment below: the Line Item Veto Act’s cancellation authority held unconstitutional, 985 F. Supp. 168 (D.D.C. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524417/usrep524417.pdf))  
Area of Law: Separation of powers; Presentment Clause; executive authority

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Line Item Veto Act constitutionally authorizes the President to cancel spending and tax provisions after signing a bill into law.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O’Connor; Breyer as to Part III | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Complete opinion for O’Connor; Part III for Breyer |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor and Scalia as to Part III | Would reverse | Part III for O’Connor and Scalia |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: He joins the Court’s conclusion that completed cancellations inflicted redressable fiscal injuries and that unilateral post-enactment cancellation revises law outside bicameralism and presentment.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): Standing after completed cancellations

Holding and operative rule: Parties whose legal or financial positions were concretely altered by completed presidential cancellation notices have standing when a judgment restoring the cancelled provisions would redress those injuries.

Reasoning: The cancellation of the Medicaid provision revived substantial contingent recoupment exposure borne in part by the governmental and provider plaintiffs. The cancellation of the cooperative tax provision impaired an identified transaction and removed a benefit designed to facilitate cooperative acquisition of processing facilities. These are personal fiscal injuries caused by completed executive acts, not an abstract institutional disagreement of the kind presented in *Raines v. Byrd*. Treating the provisions as legally operative would remove or materially alleviate the claimed disadvantages, satisfying redressability. The holding does not confer standing on every person who objects to executive administration. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524417/usrep524417.pdf))

##### Question 1(b): Post-enactment cancellation of statutory provisions

Holding and operative rule: After a provision becomes law through bicameralism and presentment, the President may not use independent policy judgment to make that provision cease to have legal force.

Reasoning: The President signed the Balanced Budget Act and Taxpayer Relief Act, after which each complete enactment became law. The later notices selected portions of those statutes and removed their legal effect unless Congress enacted a new disapproval bill. That operation amended or repealed completed law. Article I permits the President to sign or return an entire bill before enactment; it supplies no authority to revise selected provisions afterward. This conclusion does not impair executive factfinding, implementation of Congress-defined contingencies, selection within enacted ranges, ordinary enforcement discretion, or genuine statutory discretion concerning whether and when to spend. Those activities execute the law Congress enacted. The Line Item Veto Act instead authorized the President to change which enacted provisions remained law for policy reasons of his own. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524417/usrep524417.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy: The prescribed lawmaking process protects individual liberty by preventing one branch from accumulating the power to revise completed statutes. Administrative efficiency cannot replace that structural safeguard.

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and by Breyer as to Part III: The Act prospectively authorized executive cancellation as part of the statutory scheme and, in his view, did not amount to presidential repeal. His separate writing also disputes portions of the Court’s standing and separation-of-powers analysis.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by O’Connor and Scalia as to Part III: Congress may delegate substantial policy discretion within a statute, and the cancellation authority is comparable to other forms of conditional implementation and spending discretion. He would reverse the constitutional judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger*: followed; completed law may not be altered through a legally operative process outside bicameralism and presentment.  
*INS v. Chadha*: applied; repeal or amendment of statutory law must use the constitutionally prescribed lawmaking procedure.  
*Field v. Clark*: distinguished; execution of a congressionally defined contingency differs from cancellation based on independent presidential policy choice.  
*Loving v. United States*: distinguished; bounded implementation within enacted choices does not authorize post-enactment revision of the law itself.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States District Court for the District of Columbia is affirmed.  
Effect: The challenged cancellation notices are ineffective, and the Medicaid and cooperative-tax provisions remain legally operative. The Court does not decide unrelated spending discretion, administrative prioritization, or severability questions unnecessary to these cancellations.

---

### Eastern Enterprises v. Apfel

Argued March 4, 1998 | Decided June 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: application of the Coal Act’s retiree-benefit assessment to Eastern Enterprises upheld, 110 F.3d 150 (1st Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524498/usrep524498.pdf))  
Area of Law: Retroactivity; due process; takings

Questions Presented:
1. Whether imposing severe retroactive coal-industry health-benefit liability on a company long removed from the industry violates the Constitution.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: The Coal Act imposes a general payment obligation rather than appropriating identified property, and its retroactive application survives ordinary due-process review because Congress could rationally connect the assigned miners’ costs to Eastern’s employment and participation in industry benefit arrangements.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): General monetary liability and the Takings Clause

Holding and operative rule: A statutory obligation to pay money from a regulated party’s general assets does not effect a taking of identified property when the law creates no lien, appropriation of a specified fund, physical invasion, or transfer of a particular asset.

Reasoning: Section 9706(a)(3) requires Eastern to make payments supporting assigned retirees’ health benefits, but it does not identify a particular bank account, fund, lien, parcel, or other discrete property for governmental appropriation. The obligation runs from Eastern’s general assets to the benefit program. The Takings Clause protects specific property interests and their constitutional equivalents; it is not a general proportionality provision for every monetary assessment imposed through economic regulation. *Webb’s Fabulous Pharmacies, Inc. v. Beckwith* involved interest generated by a separately identifiable fund that the government took for itself. Here there is only a general liability, making due process the proper source for evaluating the asserted unfairness of retroactive legislation. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524498/usrep524498.pdf))

##### Question 1(b): Rationality of the retroactive assignment

Holding and operative rule: The Coal Act’s application to Eastern satisfies due process because assigning benefit costs for former miners to an employer that employed them and participated in industry benefit arrangements bears a rational relationship to Congress’s legitimate purpose of securing promised retiree health coverage.

Reasoning: Retroactive economic legislation receives real but deferential review. Eastern left the coal industry before later bargaining agreements made the benefit structure more explicit, and the resulting liability is severe, unexpected, and remote. Those facts demonstrate harshness, but not constitutional irrationality. Eastern employed the assigned miners and participated in earlier industry arrangements through which health benefits became an established feature of coal employment. Congress could rationally spread the cost of stabilizing the failing benefit system among signatory operators with defined employment relationships to the retirees. *Turner Elkhorn*, *R.A. Gray*, *Connolly*, and *Concrete Pipe* preserve broad authority to impose retroactive economic obligations when the allocation is connected to the regulated party and serves a legitimate corrective purpose. The record establishes neither punishment, animus, sham reasoning, nor a complete absence of connection. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524498/usrep524498.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas: The assessment imposes a severe and disproportionate burden based on events completed long before Eastern could anticipate the later promises or statutory scheme. They would classify the burden as an unconstitutional taking and reverse.

Kennedy: A general payment obligation does not take identified property, but the extreme retroactive assignment is arbitrary under due process because Eastern left the industry before the benefit commitments that produced the liability. He would reverse on that narrower ground.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Usery v. Turner Elkhorn Mining Co.*: followed; retroactive economic legislation survives when supported by a rational corrective purpose and a real connection to the regulated party.  
*Connolly v. Pension Benefit Guaranty Corp.*: followed; a general statutory payment obligation does not ordinarily appropriate identified property.  
*Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust*: followed; economic burden and disappointed expectations do not alone establish a taking or due-process violation.  
*Webb’s Fabulous Pharmacies, Inc. v. Beckwith*: distinguished; the Coal Act appropriates no specific fund or interest generated by identified private principal.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: The Coal Act assignment to Eastern Enterprises remains enforceable on the constitutional theories presented. The Court does not decide an appropriation of a specified fund, lien, or asset, punitive retroactive liability, or an assignment with no rational connection to the regulated party.

---

### National Endowment for the Arts v. Finley

Argued March 31, 1998 | Decided June 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: the decency-and-respect criteria held facially unconstitutional, 100 F.3d 671 (9th Cir. 1996). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524569/usrep524569.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; public subsidies; arts funding

Questions Presented:
1. Whether requiring the National Endowment for the Arts to consider general standards of decency and respect in awarding competitive grants is facially unconstitutional.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Souter.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Souter | Stone | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the First Amendment invalidation of the decency-and-respect proviso.  
Public role: joins Souter’s dissent in full.  
Announced ground: The statutory command requires decency and respect for prevailing beliefs to influence the selection of independent private expression, and the provision cannot be saved by treating those enacted considerations as legally inconsequential.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Facial validity of the grant considerations

Holding and operative rule: Section 954(d)(1) is not facially unconstitutional when construed as adding noncategorical considerations to a competitive and discretionary arts-grant process without requiring denial of works considered indecent, disrespectful, controversial, or offensive.

Reasoning: A facial challenger must establish a substantial risk that the statutory provision will suppress protected expression across its applications. Section 954(d)(1) requires consideration of artistic excellence and merit together with general standards of decency and respect, but it assigns no fixed weight to the latter considerations, identifies no prohibited category, and does not make them an automatic condition of eligibility. Competitive arts funding necessarily involves qualitative and content-sensitive judgments because the Government cannot support every applicant. The statute is therefore sustained on the understanding that decency and respect may inform the overall process but may not operate as a categorical exclusion or as retaliation for a disfavored viewpoint. A particular denial based on viewpoint discrimination, an unconstitutional condition, or an impermissible application remains open to challenge. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524569/usrep524569.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Government has no obligation to subsidize private artistic expression and may choose the expression it wishes to support. They would uphold the statute without relying on the Court’s limiting account of the criteria.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stone: The command that applications be judged while taking decency and respect into consideration necessarily favors deferential or conventional expression over irreverent, offensive, or challenging viewpoints. Treating the criteria as hortatory or satisfied through panel composition deletes the work Congress required them to perform. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524569/usrep524569.pdf))

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rust v. Sullivan*: applied; government may define a subsidy program and select the activities it will support within constitutional limits.  
*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: distinguished; the NEA program is a competitive funding process rather than a generally available forum governed by objective access criteria.  
*Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes*: applied; qualitative selection in a limited expressive program may be content sensitive, but viewpoint discrimination remains independently forbidden.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for entry of judgment rejecting the facial First Amendment challenge.  
Effect: Section 954(d)(1) may remain part of the NEA grant process under the limiting construction stated by the Court. Specific viewpoint-based denials, retaliation, unconstitutional conditions, and claims outside the facial challenge remain unresolved.

---

### Bragdon v. Abbott

Argued March 30, 1998 | Decided June 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below: asymptomatic HIV held to be a disability and summary judgment rejecting the dentist’s direct-threat defense affirmed, 107 F.3d 934 (1st Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524624/usrep524624.pdf))  
Area of Law: Disability discrimination; public accommodations; direct threat

Questions Presented:
1. Whether asymptomatic HIV infection is a disability under the Americans with Disabilities Act and what evidence governs a dentist’s direct-threat defense.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, by the component votes stated below.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Disability determination | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Abbott shall be treated as disabled on this record. |
| Direct-threat judgment | Vacated and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Stevens, Breyer | The contemporaneous medical record shall be reconsidered under the objective individualized-assessment rule. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg in full; Stevens and Breyer as to the disability holding and direct-threat standard; O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas as to the direct-threat standard and remand | Supports the component judgments | Complete opinion for Stone, Souter, and Ginsburg; issue propositions as stated for the remaining Justices |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Breyer | Supports affirmance of the disability determination | Would also affirm the direct-threat judgment |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports the component judgments | Complete concurrence |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the direct-threat disposition and dissents from the disability disposition | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | No other Justice | Concurs in the direct-threat disposition and dissents from the disability disposition | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm the disability determination, vacate the direct-threat judgment, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Asymptomatic HIV substantially limited Abbott’s major life activity of reproduction on this record, while the direct-threat defense requires an individualized judgment grounded in objective medical evidence reasonably available when treatment was refused.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1(a): Asymptomatic HIV and disability

Holding and operative rule: On this record, asymptomatic HIV is a physical impairment that substantially limits Abbott’s major life activity of reproduction. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: HIV infection begins affecting the body before outward symptoms appear, and the ADA’s definition does not postpone coverage until visible illness. Reproduction is a major life activity because it is central to the life process and to decisions concerning family formation. The medical consequences and transmission risks associated with HIV materially constrained Abbott’s reproductive choices. The conclusion is individualized and record specific. It does not establish that every person with HIV is disabled through the same activity, dispense with proof that an impairment substantially limits the particular claimant, or decide coverage under the regarded-as provision. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524624/usrep524624.pdf))

##### Question 1(b): Objective direct-threat assessment

Holding and operative rule: A direct-threat defense requires an individualized assessment of a significant risk grounded in objective medical evidence reasonably available when treatment was refused, including whether reasonable precautions could eliminate or reduce the risk. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer). The direct-threat judgment is vacated and remanded by a 7-2 vote for application of that rule to the complete contemporaneous record.

Reasoning: The ADA does not compel a provider to perform treatment that creates a significant, unmitigable health risk. The provider’s sincere professional belief receives no special legal deference, however, because fear and stereotype are among the barriers the statute addresses. The inquiry must consider the nature, duration, severity, probability, and avoidability of the asserted harm using evidence available at the time of the refusal. Portions of Bragdon’s scientific evidence appeared inconclusive, while other materials in the record may not have been evaluated according to their availability in September 1994. Because the granted question did not produce complete adversarial treatment of every study and factual submission, the proper course is a remand rather than a new Supreme Court finding about dental transmission risk. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524624/usrep524624.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Breyer: The objective record is insufficient to create a triable direct-threat issue, so they would affirm the First Circuit’s judgment in full. They agree with the Court’s governing legal standard.

Ginsburg: HIV affects major life choices before symptoms become visible. She supports remand to ensure a complete and carefully informed determination of the risk to health-care providers.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, and O’Connor separately: These Justices agree that the direct-threat record requires reconsideration, but they would not affirm the disability determination because Abbott did not establish the required individualized substantial limitation.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Reproduction should not be treated as substantially limited for Abbott without evidence that it was one of her own major life activities before infection. They dissent from the disability component.

O’Connor: The statutory inquiry must be individualized, and she would not treat reproduction as a major life activity on the supplied showing. She joins the remand concerning direct threat.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*School Board of Nassau County v. Arline*: extended; asymptomatic disease may constitute a disability, while contagiousness is evaluated separately through an individualized risk inquiry.  
*General Electric Co. v. Joiner*: applied; scientific assertions require an objective analytical connection to the evidence supporting them.  
The ADA direct-threat provisions: established; sincere professional belief cannot replace objective, contemporaneous, individualized evidence of significant unmitigable risk.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The disability determination is affirmed, the judgment concerning direct threat is vacated, and the case is remanded for further proceedings under the objective individualized-assessment rule.  
Effect: Abbott shall be treated as disabled in this action. Bragdon may still establish that in-office treatment presented a significant risk that reasonable precautions could not eliminate, but no ultimate finding on that defense is entered.

---

### United States v. Balsys

Argued April 20, 1998 | Decided June 25, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below: assertion of the Fifth Amendment privilege sustained based solely on feared foreign prosecution, 119 F.3d 122 (2d Cir. 1997). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524666/usrep524666.pdf))  
Area of Law: Self-incrimination; foreign prosecution; governmental attribution

Questions Presented:
1. Whether the Fifth Amendment privilege against self-incrimination permits refusal to testify based solely on a reasonable fear of prosecution by a foreign sovereign.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. Opposing: Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy in full; Scalia and Thomas as to Parts I, II, and III | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, and Kennedy; Parts I, II, and III for Scalia and Thomas |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete concurrence |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete dissent |
| Dissent | Breyer | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete dissent |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: A real fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign does not activate the Fifth Amendment privilege without a threatened domestic use or a coordinated foreign prosecution attributable to the United States.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign

Holding and operative rule: A reasonable fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign, without more, does not permit a witness to refuse testimony under the Fifth Amendment.

Reasoning: The Self-Incrimination Clause restrains compelled testimony used in a criminal case within the constitutional system it binds. *Murphy v. Waterfront Commission* prevents domestic sovereigns subject to the Constitution from compelling testimony and transferring it to another domestic sovereign for prosecution. That rule reflects a shared constitutional restraint and does not extend automatically to a foreign government acting under independent law. Balsys established a genuine fear of foreign prosecution but identified no realistic domestic criminal case and no coordinated foreign proceeding functioning as a proxy for the United States. A materially integrated prosecution, deliberate circumvention through foreign authorities, unlawful transfer of testimony, breach of immunity, or a treaty-based restriction may present a different issue. The undeveloped possibility of future cooperation does not establish that exception here. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep524/usrep524666/usrep524666.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: The privilege remains fully applicable to any domestic criminal use and may extend to a foreign proceeding sufficiently controlled by or attributable to the United States. Neither circumstance is established on this record.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg: Compelled testimony creates the same personal dilemma when the realistic prosecution will occur abroad, and the privilege should protect the witness from a substantial foreign danger.

Breyer, joined by Ginsburg: Increasing international law-enforcement cooperation makes the domestic-foreign distinction too formal. He would protect against a real and substantial foreign prosecution risk, subject to appropriate immunity arrangements.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Murphy v. Waterfront Commission*: clarified; its protection against intersovereign whipsawing applies to sovereigns sharing the Constitution’s self-incrimination constraint and does not automatically extend to independent foreign prosecution.  
*United States v. Murdock*: limited; foreign criminal exposure alone does not activate the Fifth Amendment privilege.  
Government-attribution doctrine: established; a coordinated foreign prosecution may present a different question when foreign punishment is effectively attributable to the United States.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for enforcement of the subpoena subject to any preserved domestic-use, immunity, treaty, or attribution objection.  
Effect: Balsys may not refuse to testify solely because of feared independent foreign prosecution. The Government receives no authority to make prohibited domestic use of compelled testimony or to evade the privilege through a coordinated foreign proxy.

---

### Bauchman v. West High School

Argued February 25, 1998 | Decided June 26, 1998  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit; judgment below: student-specific prospective claims dismissed as moot and constitutional damages claims rejected, 132 F.3d 542 (10th Cir. 1997). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/132/542/469905/))  
Area of Law: Establishment Clause; Free Exercise Clause; compelled expression; mootness

Questions Presented:
1. Whether a public-school choir’s selection, rehearsal, and performance of religious music, including performances in religious buildings, violates the Establishment Clause when the school asserts secular artistic and pedagogical purposes.
2. Whether requiring a student to rehearse or perform religious music, subject to a grade-neutral opt-out, violates the Free Exercise Clause or the prohibition against compelled expression.
3. Whether the student’s graduation moots claims for prospective relief and, if so, whether the remaining damages claim preserves a live controversy concerning the underlying constitutional issues.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: Graduation moots Bauchman’s individual prospective claims but not a damages claim for completed conduct. On the merits, the secular study and performance of religious music, coupled with a genuine grade-neutral opt-out, neither establishes religion nor compels religious affirmation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 3: Graduation and the continuing damages controversy

Holding and operative rule: Graduation moots a former student’s individual claims for prospective regulation of school practices absent continuing exposure or a certified class, but a legally sufficient damages claim for completed constitutional injury preserves a live controversy concerning that past conduct.

Reasoning: Bauchman no longer attends the school system and will not again be required to participate in the choir. An injunction or prospective declaration would therefore make no change to her legal position. The damages claim is different because a monetary judgment could redress a completed constitutional injury if one were proved. Article III permits adjudication of the granted merits questions for that purpose while requiring dismissal of student-specific prospective relief. The holding does not address a certified class, a continuing school-imposed consequence, or claims brought by current students. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/132/542/469905/))

##### Question 1: Religious music and religious performance venues

Holding and operative rule: The Establishment Clause does not prohibit a public-school choir from selecting, rehearsing, and performing religious music, including in religious buildings, when the choices serve genuine secular artistic and pedagogical purposes and the school does not conduct or sponsor worship.

Reasoning: Sacred works form a substantial part of the choral canon, and their study can be necessary to teach composition, harmony, language, technique, and musical history. A church or other religious building may likewise be selected for acoustics, capacity, access to an instrument, or the historical context of a work. The constitutional question is the school’s actual purpose and operation, not the religious origin of the composition or ownership of the venue. The supplied record supports the asserted educational and artistic reasons and does not establish that the choir performance became prayer, devotional instruction, or denominational observance. *Tanford v. Brand* and *Chaudhuri v. Tennessee* concerned government-selected prayer presented as worship at official events; they do not prohibit secular performance of religious art. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/132/542/469905/))

##### Question 2: Grade-neutral opt-out and asserted compulsion

Holding and operative rule: Requiring study or performance of religious music as part of a neutral choir curriculum, subject to a genuine grade-neutral opt-out, does not violate the Free Exercise Clause or compelled-expression principles when the student is required neither to profess belief nor to participate in worship.

Reasoning: The curriculum assigned music rather than religious doctrine. Performing words drawn from a religious tradition for educational purposes does not itself require the singer to affirm their truth, just as studying or reciting historical and literary texts does not amount to ideological adoption. The offered opt-out permitted Bauchman to decline the challenged performance without loss of grade or academic standing, removing the asserted forced participation. A nominal alternative accompanied by lost credit, stigma, exclusion from the course, or another practical penalty would require different analysis, as would an instruction to pray, worship, or profess religious belief. None is established within the granted record. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/132/542/469905/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Spencer v. Kemna*: applied; prospective claims expire when personal exposure ends, while a live damages claim for completed conduct remains independently justiciable.  
*Tanford v. Brand* and *Chaudhuri v. Tennessee*: distinguished; official prayer differs from secular curricular use of religious art.  
*School District of Abington Township v. Schempp*: applied; public schools may teach about religion and use religious material for genuine secular educational purposes.  
*West Virginia State Board of Education v. Barnette*: distinguished; secular performance with a genuine no-penalty opt-out does not compel profession of belief.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Bauchman’s individual prospective claims remain dismissed as moot, and the damages claims fail on the religious-music, venue, and grade-neutral-opt-out theories decided here. The Court expresses no view on separate allegations of teacher-led devotional exercises outside the simulated grant.
